# COPYRIGHT STATEMENT

The copyright laws of the United States <note>Referencing Title 17 of the United States Code, which establishes the legal rights of creators and the public's access to creative works.</note> and of India <note>Specifically the Copyright Act of 1957, the primary legislation governing intellectual property in India.</note> govern the making of photocopies or other reproductions of copyrighted material, including foreign works, under certain conditions. In addition, the United States extends protection to foreign works through various international conventions, bilateral agreements, and proclamations.

Under certain conditions specified in these laws, libraries and archives are authorized to furnish a photocopy or other reproduction. One of these specified conditions is that the photocopy or reproduction is not to be used for any purpose other than **private study**, **scholarship**, or **research**. If a user makes a request for, or later uses, a photocopy or reproduction for purposes in excess of <term>fair use</term> <note>A legal doctrine that permits limited use of copyrighted material without needing permission from the rights holders for purposes such as criticism, news reporting, teaching, or research.</note>, that user may be liable for <term>copyright infringement</term> <note>The unauthorized use of works under copyright, violating the owner's exclusive rights.</note>.

These institutions reserve the right to refuse to accept a copy order if, in their judgment, fulfillment of the order would involve a violation of the copyright laws of the United States or India.

***

### **Key Legal Terms**

*   **Copyright Laws:** Legal protections that grant creators of original work exclusive rights to its use and distribution.
*   **Title 17, United States Code:** The set of federal laws that govern copyright in the USA.
*   **Copyright Act of 1957:** The legal foundation for copyright protection in India.
*   **Photocopies:** Reproductions of documents made using photographic or electronic processes.
*   **Fair Use:** A legal allowance for using copyrighted material for educational or research purposes without being considered a violation.
*   **Copyright Infringement:** Breaking the law by using copyrighted material without permission outside the bounds of fair use.

# Gopalakrishnayya Siddhanti, Rallabhandi <note>Original: 'Gopalakrishnayya Siddhanti, Rallabhandi'. Rallabhandi is the family name. 'Siddhanti' is a traditional title for an expert in the Siddhantas, the ancient Indian treatises on astronomy and mathematics.</note>

## The Mathematician’s Delight <note>Original: *Ganakaranjani*. This title is composed of 'Ganaka' (mathematician, calculator, or astrologer) and 'Ranjani' (delighting or pleasing), common in Sanskrit-influenced titles to suggest a work that makes a complex subject enjoyable.</note>

## Madras, 1912 <note>Madras is the historical name for the city now known as Chennai, in Tamil Nadu, India.</note>

# The Delight of Mathematicians
<note>original: "Ganaka Ranjani"</note>

---

<margin>Verse</margin> I meditate upon the all-pervading one, clothed in white, moon-colored, with four arms and a serene face, so that all obstacles may be stilled.
<note>This is a traditional Sanskrit invocation to the deity Ganesha (the remover of obstacles) or Vishnu, used to ensure the successful completion of a task.</note>

Victorious is the Sun, the soul of the universe and the source of the world’s birth, the natural ornament of the sky, who is adorned with a garland of a thousand rays resembling molten gold. (1)
<note>As a work on astronomy and astrology, it is customary to praise the Sun (Savita/Surya), the primary celestial body used in calculations.</note>

Having observed the infallible words spoken by the first sages and the great extent of their works, I have composed this to clearly state the meaning—neither too briefly nor too extensively, but in a very pleasing manner. (2)
<note>The author explains his intent: to distill the vast, complex ancient Sanskrit astronomical texts (Siddhantas) into a more readable and balanced format.</note>

## Salutations to the Teacher
<note>original: "Guru-vandanam"</note>

<margin>Verse</margin> I bow perpetually to the two feet of the leader of the Makkapati family, Venkataramana Siddhanti, a scholar of the <term>Dualist philosophy</term> and astronomical principles.
<note>The teacher, Makkapati Venkataramana, is identified here as a master of both Dwaita (the philosophy of dualism) and Siddhanta (mathematical astronomy).</note>

## Genealogy of the Author
<note>original: "Granthakartru Vamshavali"</note>

<margin>Verse</margin> Residing always in the prosperous town named Tammuvaram, he is like a honeybee at the lotus feet of the holy Ramachandra.
A treasury of astrological science and a scholar, born of the "Stone-cart" lineage—which is like an ocean—he is named Kuppayya, son of Ramamba, of the Vapaga-ayana <term>gotra</term>.
<note>The author uses the Sanskrit "Shila-shakata" (Stone-cart) to refer to his Telugu surname, "Rallabandi" (Ralla = stones, Bandi = cart). Gotra refers to an ancestral lineage or clan.</note>

<page-num>2</page-num>
# The Delight of Mathematicians
<note>original: "Ganaka Ranjani"</note>

<margin>Verse</margin> He, known by the name Gopalakrishna, who delights the minds of scholars, was born to the goddess-like Rajalakshmi, ever-observant of poetic inquiry.

Having repeatedly examined the foundations of the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>The *Surya Siddhanta* is one of the most important ancient Sanskrit treatises on Indian astronomy.</note>, he composed this *Delight of Mathematicians* for the easy understanding of students.

### Calculation of the Shaka Era
<note>original: "Shaka Sadhana"</note>

<margin>Verse</margin> Sixty (60) multiplied by twenty-three (23), combined with the elapsed years, and added to four hundred and nine (409)—know this to be the time of the Shaka King.

**Explanation:** Set down 60 and multiply it by 23. To this, add the number of elapsed years in the current sixty-year cycle (starting from the year <term>Prabhava</term>). Then add 409 to the total. The result is the current Shaka Era year.
<note>The Shaka Era is a historical Hindu calendar era beginning in 78 CE. The cycle starting with *Prabhava* refers to the 60-year Jupiter cycle used in Indian chronology.</note>

### Deriving the Constant for Elapsed Kali Years
<note>original: "Kaligatabda Dhruvanayanamu"</note>

<margin>Verse</margin> Eighty (80) multiplied by sixty (60), combined with two hundred and eighteen (218), and further combined with the years since <term>Prabhava</term>—these shall be the years of the Kali age.

**Explanation:** Set down 80 and multiply it by 60. Add 218 to the product. Then, add the number of elapsed years from the current cycle beginning with *Prabhava*. The resulting sum is the total number of years elapsed in the <term>Kali Yuga</term>.
<note>The Kali Yuga is the fourth and current age in the Hindu cycle of cosmic time, traditionally calculated as beginning in 3102 BCE.</note>

### Calculating the Epochal Year
<note>original: "Karanabda Sadhanamu"</note>

<margin>Verse</margin> Subtracting three thousand one hundred and seventy-nine (3179) from the Kali years is said to give the Shaka year. Subtracting four thousand five hundred and forty-eight (4548) gives the <term>Karanabda</term>.

**Explanation:** If you subtract 3179 from the total elapsed years of the Kali age, the result is the <term>Shaliwahana Shaka</term> year. If you subtract 4548 from the elapsed Kali years, the remaining balance is the <term>Karanabda</term> <note>The "Karanabda" refers to the specific epochal year used as a starting point for simplified astronomical calculations in a *Karana* text.</note>. These should be understood as the "Constant Years" (Dhruvabda).

### Calculating the Savana Day-Count
<note>original: "Savana Dyugananayanamu" - This refers to calculating the Ahargana, or the number of civil days elapsed from a fixed epoch.</note>

<margin>Verse</margin> Multiply the Epochal Constant by twelve (12) and add the current months... [place in two positions]... add the remainder... divided by seventy (70)... adding the intercalary months resulting from the division by thirty-three (33)...

<summary>This page introduces the author, Gopalakrishna, and provides mathematical formulas to convert between major Indian chronological eras: the Kali Yuga, the Shaka Era, and the Karanabda (Epochal year).</summary>
<keywords>Gopalakrishna, Surya Siddhanta, Shaka Era, Kali Yuga, Karanabda, Prabhava cycle, Indian astronomy, chronological calculation</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous calculations of the Shaka and Kali eras. Having established the current year in these eras, the author now moves to the "Chapter on Mean Motions," which provides the formulas to calculate the total number of elapsed days (Ahargana) and the mean positions of the celestial bodies, starting with the Sun.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
# Chapter on Mean Motions
<note>original: "Madhya Adhyayam"</note>

<margin>Verse</margin> Multiply by thirty (30), add the lunar days; then multiply by eleven (11) and divide by the fire-void-mountains (703) <note>In ancient Indian numerology, "fire" represents 3, "void" represents 0, and "mountain" represents 7. Reading right-to-left, this equals 703.</note>. If an intercalary month occurs, the day-count begins from Friday. If the day does not align, one day should be adjusted; if it still does not align, then adjust accordingly.

**Explanation:** Multiply the constant years <term>Dhruvabda</term> <note>A fixed number of years used as a starting point for calculations.</note> by 12 and add the months elapsed in the current year. Place this value in three separate positions. In the bottom position, add 46 and divide by 70. The quotient, added to the months in the second position and divided by 33, gives the number of **intercalary months** <note>original: "Adhimasa"; these are extra months added to the lunar calendar to keep it synchronized with the solar seasons.</note>. 

Add these intercalary months to the first position and multiply the total by 30. Add the number of elapsed lunar days (*tithis*) of the current month. Place this result in two positions. Multiply the bottom position by 11 and divide by 703. The resulting quotient represents the **omitted lunar days** <note>original: "Kshaya-tithi"; because a lunar day is slightly shorter than a solar day, some days are "skipped" in the civil calendar count.</note>. Subtract these omitted days from the top position to arrive at the total count of civil days <term>Ahargana</term> starting from a Friday. 

To find the current day of the week, divide this total day-count by 7. The remainder indicates the day, counting from Friday. If the calculated day does not match the actual weekday, one must add or subtract one (or sometimes two) from the total day-count to align it correctly. This adjustment is sometimes necessary due to local variations or specific astronomical conditions.

<margin>Verse</margin> If the number of intercalary months in the month preceding an intercalary month, or in the intercalary month itself, is greater, subtract one from the intercalary month count and proceed with the calculation of the day-count as described before.

### Calculation of the Mean Planets
<note>original: "Madhya Graha Sadhanam"</note>

->**For the Sun**<-
<note>original: "Suryasya"</note>

<margin>Verse</margin> Multiply the day-count by thirteen (13) and divide by the qualities-void-nine (903). The result in degrees should be subtracted from the day-count; the result is the mean position of the Sun (and also Mercury) starting from Friday. 
The constants are 11 - 16 - 52 - 35. Subtract the minutes obtained from the day-count divided by the three-qualities-ocean-earth (1433) <note>Again using symbolic numbers: Earth=1, Ocean=4, Qualities=3, Three=3.</note> from the Sun’s position. For further accuracy, subtract the value derived from the velocity.

<summary>This page explains the complex mathematical steps to calculate the total number of days elapsed since the epoch (Ahargana), accounting for leap months and skipped days, and introduces the formula for finding the Sun's mean position.</summary>
<keywords>Ahargana, Intercalary Month, Omitted Days, Mean Sun, Indian Astronomy, Mathematical Constants</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical procedures for planetary astronomy. Having calculated the total elapsed days (Ahargana) on the previous page, the author now provides the specific multipliers and divisors used to find the mean positions of the Sun, the Moon, and the Moon's apogee.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
->**GANAKARANDINI**<-
<note>The Joy of Calculation</note>

**Explanation:** Place the **total elapsed days** <term>Dyugana</term> in two separate positions. Multiply the bottom position by 3 and divide the result by 503. Subtract the resulting degrees and minutes from the top position of elapsed days. This gives the degrees for the Sun, as well as for the mean positions of Mercury, Venus, and other planets. Add the **previous epoch constants** <term>Dhruva</term> <note>fixed values representing the position of planets at the start of the current era</note> of 11-15-22-3. Multiply the **elapsed years of the era** <term>Sharanabda</term> by 3 and divide by 15; subtract the resulting minutes. Furthermore, multiply the distance by 80 and divide by 50; subtract the resulting seconds to arrive at the **Mean Sun** <term>Madhya Surya</term>.

### For the Moon
<note>original: "Chandrasya"</note>

> *Verse:* Multiply the total elapsed days by thirteen <note>original: "Vishwa-Aditya," referencing the 13 deities or solar forms</note> and place it in two positions; divide the second by twenty-eight (28). Add the resulting degrees to the elapsed days. Then, divide the elapsed days by 1522 <note>original: "shaila-adri-yuga-rupa-nagara"</note> and add those degrees. Thus, the Mean Moon is always found. Always add the epoch constant of 11-8-46-33 <note>original: "rudra-ashta-anga-arnava-sura"</note>. Multiply the elapsed years of the era by three, divide by eight, and subtract those minutes. Subtract the seconds derived from the regional distance to find the Moon.

**Explanation:** Multiply the **total elapsed days** <term>Dyugana</term> by 13 and place the result in two positions. Divide the bottom position by 28. Also, divide the total elapsed days by 1522. Add both these resulting degrees and minutes to the 13-times elapsed days calculated initially to find the Moon's position in **degrees and minutes** <term>Amshadi</term>. Add the previous epoch constant of 11-8-46-33. Multiply the **elapsed years of the era** <term>Sharanabda</term> by 3 and divide by 8; subtract the resulting **minutes** <term>Liptas</term>. Since the regional distance for this location is 80 **yojanas** <note>a traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-15 kilometers</note>, multiply by 80 and divide by 50. Subtract the resulting **seconds** <term>Viliptas</term> to obtain the **Mean Moon** <term>Madhya Chandra</term>.

### For the Moon's Apogee
<note>original: "Chandrocchaku"</note>

> *Verse:* The sum of the degrees and minutes obtained by dividing the total elapsed days by one hundred and sixty-nine <note>original: "nandaih-hutashaih," 9 and 3 squared/combined</note> is the apogee. Always add the epoch constant 9-16-16-19 <note>original: "dwisapta-indu-tara-rashmi"</note>. Multiply the elapsed years by 3 and divide by 96; subtract those minutes to find the apogee. Subtract the seconds calculated from the regional distance.

**Explanation:** <note>The prose for this section follows the verse's logic</note> To find the **Moon's Apogee** <term>Chandroccha</term> <note>the point where the Moon is farthest from the Earth</note>, divide the total elapsed days by 169. Add the epoch constant of 9-16-16-19. Multiply the elapsed years by 3 and divide by 96, then subtract those minutes. Finally, subtract the correction for the regional distance in seconds to determine the precise position of the Moon's apogee.

<summary>This page details the mathematical formulas (Karanas) to calculate the mean positions of the Sun, Moon, and the Moon's apogee by applying multipliers, divisors, and epochal constants to the total elapsed days.</summary>
<keywords>Mean Sun, Mean Moon, Moon's Apogee, Dyugana, Sharanabda, Dhruva, Liptas, Viliptas, planetary constants, astronomical calculation</keywords>

<header>CHAPTER ON MEAN POSITIONS</header>

# Chapter on Mean Positions
<note>original: "Madhya Adhyaya"</note>

**Explanation:** Place the **total elapsed days** <term>Dyugana</term> <note>the number of days passed since the start of the epoch</note> in two separate positions. Divide the first position by 6 to get the degrees, and divide the second position by 60 to get the minutes of arc. Add these two results together to find the movement of the **Moon's apogee** <term>Chandrocca</term> <note>the point where the Moon is farthest from Earth</note>. To this, add the **epoch constant** <term>Dhruva</term> <note>the fixed starting position for this era</note> of 2-17-27-12. Finally, divide the **elapsed years of the era** <term>Trigunabda</term> by 983 and subtract the resulting seconds of arc from your total.

---

## For Rahu
<note>Rahu represents the North Node of the Moon, where its orbit crosses the ecliptic. In Indian astronomy, it is calculated as moving backward (retrograde).</note>

> *Verse:* Multiply the total elapsed days by three and place it in two positions. Divide the second by one thousand and four (1004). This provides the degrees and minutes for the shadow planet.

> *Verse:* The constant is 0-2-30-0. Subtract the calculated value from this **epoch constant** <term>Dhruva</term> to find the **Mean Rahu** <term>Madhya Rahu</term>. Divide the elapsed years of the era by two thousand five hundred and four (2504) and add the resulting degrees. Then, calculate the correction for the **path of travel** <term>Gatyadhwa</term> and subtract the seconds. Subtract the current year from the cycle of 3600; multiply the remainder by 27 and divide by 3150 to find the seconds of arc for the darkness <note>original: "Tamas," a poetic name for Rahu</note>.

**Explanation:** Multiply the **total elapsed days** <term>Dyugana</term> by 3 and place the result in two positions. The first position represents the minutes of arc. Divide the second position by 1004 to find the degrees. Add these two together to get the total movement of Rahu. Subtract this value from the **epoch constant** <term>Dhruva</term> of 0-2-30-0. Then, divide the **elapsed years of the era** <term>Trigunabda</term> by 2504 and add the resulting degrees. Apply the **longitudinal correction** <term>Desantara Samskara</term> <note>adjustment based on the observer's distance from the prime meridian at Ujjain</note>. Finally, subtract the years elapsed from 3600, multiply the remainder by 27, and divide by 3150; subtract these resulting seconds to arrive at the position of Rahu.

---

## Calculation for Mars
<note>original: "Kuja Sadhanam"</note>

> *Verse:* Divide the total elapsed days by two and place the result in two positions. With the multiplier of twenty-one (21), add the degrees and minutes... the epoch constant for Mars is always 0-12-20-46. Divide the elapsed years of the era by five thousand three hundred and fifty (5350)...

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>THE DELIGHT OF PLANETS</header>
<note>original: "Graha Karanjani"</note>

> *Verse:* ...is joined. From the distance traveled in a day, subtract the seconds of arc after dividing by eighteen. In the era of thirty-six hundred <note>3600 years of the Kali Yuga</note>, take the remainder, multiply by eighteen and divide; when these minutes are added to the position, it becomes the **Mean Mars** <term>Madhya Kuja</term>.

**Explanation:** Divide the doubled value by 2 and keep it in two places. If you divide the first part by 40, you get the minutes of arc; if you divide the second part by 20, you get the degrees. If you divide [the original value] by 8 and add it to the doubled value, it becomes the position of **Mars** <term>Kuja</term>. Add the **epoch constant** <term>Dhruva</term> <note>the fixed starting position</note> of 0-12-20-46. Multiply the **years of the era** <term>Karanabda</term> by 3 and divide by 5360; add the resulting degrees. From the **mean motion** <term>Madhya Gati</term>, multiply by 20 and divide by 80, then subtract the resulting seconds of arc.

---

Subtract 3600 from the elapsed years of the **Kali Yuga** <term>Kalyadigata</term>, multiply by 18 and divide by 15. If you add the resulting minutes, it becomes the **Mean Mars corrected for observation** <term>Drik-Samskara Madhya Kuja</term>.

### Calculation for Mercury
<note>original: "Budha Sadhanam"</note>

> *Verse:* The double amount multiplied by four gives the degrees. From the double amount, multiply by six and divide by sixty-one. To this, add the constant 7-1-12-44. For the years of the era, multiply by three and divide by seven hundred and eighty-eight <note>788</note> to find the degrees to be added for **Mercury** <term>Budha</term>. From the daily motion, divide the seconds by eight and subtract.

**Explanation:** Multiply the doubled value by 4 to get the degrees. Multiply the doubled value by 6 and divide by 61 to get the degrees. Add these together to find **Mercury** <term>Budha</term>. Add the **epoch constant** <term>Dhruva</term> of 7-1-12-44. Multiply the **tripled years of the era** <term>Trigunabda</term> and divide by 788; add the resulting degrees and perform the **motion correction** <term>Gati Samskara</term>.

### Calculation for Jupiter
<note>original: "Guru Sadhanam"</note>

> *Verse:* The double amount multiplied by five [results in] the minutes of arc for **Jupiter** <term>Guru</term>. From the double amount, subtract the seconds of arc divided by seventy (70). For Jupiter, five, eight, six, one, zero... <note>The verse cuts off as it lists the constant or divisor</note>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER ON MEAN POSITIONS</header>
<note>original: "Madhya Adhyaya"</note>

...and the **epoch constant** <term>Dhruva</term> <note>the fixed starting position for this calculation</note> should be 5-2-16-20. The minutes of arc and related fractions, obtained by dividing the triple-multiplied years <note>Karanabda</note> by 134, should always be added to **Jupiter** <term>Guru</term>. From the daily motion and the distance traveled, minutes and seconds are calculated; after dividing by the sun's number <note>12</note>, they are subtracted. From the years elapsed since the start of the **Kali Yuga** <term>Kalyadyuna</term>, subtract thirty-six hundred <note>3600</note>. Multiply the remainder by twelve and divide by one hundred and fifty-five <note>155</note>. When these minutes are subtracted, the result is the position of **Jupiter** <term>Vachaspati</term>.

**Explanation:** If you multiply the 'ma' group <note>a specific variable in the calculation</note> by 5 to get minutes, and then subtract the minutes obtained by dividing that same group by 2, you arrive at the position of **Jupiter** <term>Brihaspati</term> in minutes and seconds. The previous **epoch constant** <term>Dhruva</term> of 5-2-16-20 must be added. The minutes and seconds obtained by dividing the triple-multiplied years by 134 are also added. After performing the **longitudinal correction** <term>Deshantara Samskara</term> <note>adjustment for the observer's distance from the prime meridian</note>, subtract 3600 from the elapsed years of the **Kali Yuga**, multiply the remainder by 12 and divide by 155. Subtracting these resulting minutes gives the final position of **Jupiter** <term>Brihaspati</term>.

---

### Calculation for Venus
<note>original: "Shukra Sadhanam"</note>

> *Verse:* Multiply the day-count by eight and keep it in two places. Dividing the first by five gives degrees; dividing the second by sixty-two gives minutes and seconds. To this, add the **epoch constant** <term>Dhruva</term> for **Venus** <term>Kavi</term>, which is 11-9-9-21. For the triple-multiplied years, divide by 1893 to find the degrees. After performing the **longitudinal correction** <term>Deshantara</term>, subtract thirty-six hundred <note>3600</note> from the elapsed years of the **Kali Yuga**. Multiply the remainder by forty-two and divide by one hundred and fifty-six <note>156</note>. Subtracting these minutes quickly gives the **fast anomaly** <term>Shighra</term> <note>the point in the planet's orbit where it appears to move fastest</note> of **Venus** <term>Bhrigu</term>.

**Explanation:** Multiply the **total day-count** <term>Dyuguna</term> by 8 and keep the result in two copies. Dividing the first copy by 5 gives the position in degrees; dividing the second copy by 62 gives the position in minutes. When these are added, it becomes the position of **Venus** <term>Shukra</term>. The **epoch constant** <term>Dhruva</term> of 11-9-9-21 must be added. Divide the triple-multiplied years by 1893 and subtract the resulting degrees. After applying the **longitudinal correction** <term>Deshantara Samskara</term>, subtract 3600 from the elapsed years of the **Kali Yuga**, multiply the remainder by 42 and divide by 156. Subtracting these minutes gives the **Venus Fast Anomaly** <term>Shukra Shighra</term>.

---

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header># GANAKA RANJANI
<note>original: "Ganaka Ranjani" — translates to "Delight of the Mathematician"</note></header>

**Verse:** The **epoch constant** <term>Dhruva</term> <note>the fixed starting position for a planet at the beginning of a calculation period</note> is three-eighteen-forty-nine-thirty-six (3-18-49-36). For **Saturn** <term>Shani</term>, add the minutes obtained by dividing the triple-multiplied years <note>Karanabda</note> by two hundred (200). To find the change based on daily motion, divide the arc-seconds by eighty (80). From the years elapsed in the **Kali Yuga** <term>Kalyabda</term> <note>the current era in the Hindu calendar</note>, subtract thirty-six hundred (3600). Multiply the remainder by fifty-six (56) and divide by one hundred and fifty (150). Adding these minutes and seconds gives the position of **Saturn** <term>Shani</term>.

**Explanation:** If you multiply the <term>Drik-guna</term> <note>an observational factor used as a multiplier</note> by 2, you get the minutes and seconds. Also, divide the <term>Drik-guna</term> by 150 to get further minutes. Adding these results gives the position of **Saturn** <term>Shani</term> in minutes. The previous **epoch constant** <term>Dhruva</term> of 3-18-49-36 must be added. Then, add the minutes and seconds derived from dividing the triple-multiplied years by 200. Next, perform the **longitudinal correction** <term>Deshantara Samskara</term> <note>adjustment for the observer's specific distance from the prime meridian, usually Ujjain</note>. From the elapsed years of the **Kali Yuga**, subtract 3600, multiply the remainder by 56, and divide by 150. Adding these resulting minutes and seconds provides the **mean position of Saturn** <term>Madhya Shani</term>.

---

### Determination of Corrections
<note>original: "Samskara Nirnayah"</note>

**Verse:** Five corrections <term>Samskaras</term> must always be performed for the **mean planets** <term>Madhya Khetas</term>: first, the **epoch constant** <term>Purva Dhruva</term>; second, the **triple-year adjustment** <term>Trigunabda</term>; third, the **longitudinal correction** <term>Deshantara</term>; fourth, the **correction for seeds** <term>Bija Phalam</term> <note>also called the observational or Drik correction</note>; and fifth, the **arc-correction** <term>Bahoh Phalam</term> <note>the correction for the planet's anomaly or eccentricity</note>. These should be done in order.

**Explanation:** Five specific corrections must be applied to the mean position of a planet:

(1) The **Epoch Constant** <term>Purva Dhruva</term>
(2) The **Triple-year Adjustment** <term>Trigunabda</term>
(3) The **Longitudinal Correction** <term>Deshantara</term>
(4) The **Observational Correction** <term>Drik-samskara</term>
(5) The **Correction for Anomaly** <term>Bhuja-phala Samskara</term> <note>an adjustment based on the planet's orbital position</note>.

**Verse:** For the **lunar apogee** <term>Chandrocha</term> and the **ascending node** <term>Rahu</term>, and for the Moon and Sun during eclipses, these corrections are essential for determining the precise timing and positions. This is the established rule.

---

### 5. Correction for Anomaly
<note>original: "Bhuja-phala Samskarah" — this refers to the sine-correction used to find the true position from the mean position</note>

**Verse:** Three-four-five (345), twenty-seven (27), eight-seven-eight (878), six-seven-eight (678), four (4), four-three-three (433), five-two-four (524), three-six-seven (367), and two-zero (20) are the values in order... 
<gloss>8</gloss> <gloss>67</gloss> <gloss>4</gloss> <gloss>524</gloss> <gloss>367</gloss>
<note>The verse uses numerical mnemonics like "Gaja" (Elephant=8) and "Veda" (4) to list mathematical constants used in the sine tables for planetary corrections.</note>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header># THE CHAPTER ON MEAN POSITIONS
<note>original: "Madhya-adhyaya"</note></header>

->**The Chapter on Mean Positions**<-

**Verse:** Divide the value known as the **Sun's equation of the center** <term>Bahu-phala</term> <note>a correction used to account for the elliptical shape of an orbit, turning a "mean" or average position into a "true" position</note> separately by the unique **divisors** <term>Haraka</term> assigned to each planet. Apply the resulting minutes and seconds to the planets in the same manner as prescribed for the Sun's true position.
<note>original Sanskrit: "Sūryabāhuphalasaṃjñaṃ grahāṇāṃ hārakaiḥ pṛthak | vibhajya labdha liptādi kuryātsūrya sphuṭoktavat ||"</note>

**Explanation:** To calculate the correction for the planets, divide the Sun's equation of the center by these specific divisors: **361** for the Sun, **27** for the Moon, **687** for Mars, **88** for Mercury, **4332** for Jupiter, **224** for Venus, and **10766** for Saturn. If the result is to be added to the Sun's position, add it to all the other planets as well. If it is to be subtracted from the Sun, subtract it from all the planets. The results are expressed in **minutes of arc** <term>Lipta</term> and **seconds of arc** <term>Vilipta</term>.

---

->Mean motions for the Sun and other planets.<-

> **Verse:** The daily motion of the Sun in minutes and seconds is fifty-nine <note>original: nava-śarā (9 and 5 = 59)</note> and eight <note>original: gajāḥ (8)</note>. For the Moon, it is seven hundred and ninety <note>original: śūnya-nandāśvi (0, 9, 2 = 290? likely 790 based on context)</note> and thirty-five. For Mars, the motion is thirty-one <note>original: kṣoṇī-vahnayo (1, 3 = 31)</note> and forty-six <note>original: gālivvo (46)</note>. For Mercury, two hundred and forty-five <note>original: bāṇa-jinā (5, 24 = 245)</note> and thirty-two. For Jupiter, it is five <note>original: pañca</note> minutes and zero seconds. For Venus, ninety-six <note>original: aṅga-navā (6, 9 = 96)</note> and eight. For Saturn, the son of the Sun, it is two minutes and zero seconds. For the Moon's Ascending Node <note>Rahu</note>, it is three and eleven. These motions were determined by the ancient teachers and the great sages.

These are the "mean" motions <note>Madhyagatulu: average speeds</note> for the Sun and other planets. However, for the "true" planets <note>Spuṭa-grahamulu: the planets as actually observed in the sky</note>, there are eight distinct types of motions <note>Aṣṭavidhagatulu: eight variations of planetary velocity including retrograde, slow, and fast motion</note>. I shall now provide their details.

<summary>This page lists technical tables for planetary apogees and equations, followed by a verse detailing the daily mean motions of the planets as calculated by ancient Indian astronomers.</summary>
<keywords>Mean Motion, Apogee, Mandoccalu, Sun, Moon, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn, Rahu, Ketu, Astronomy Tables</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of planetary astronomy from the "Chapter on Mean Positions." While the previous page provided the quantitative data for daily motion rates and orbital corrections, this page introduces the qualitative classification of planetary speeds (the eight types of motion) and explains how these motions change based on a planet's distance from the Sun.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>The Astronomer’s Delight <note>original: Gaṇaka Rañjani</note></header>

## The Eight Types of Motion

> **Verse:** Retrograde, starting retrograde, leaving retrograde, slow, very slow, mean; as well as fast and very fast—these are the eight-fold motions of the planets. <note>original: vakrā-anuvakrā vikalā-mandā manda-tarā-samā | tathā śīghrā-atiśīghrākhya grahāṇām-aṣṭadhā gatiḥ ||</note>

**Interpretation:** **Vakra**—known as retrograde motion; **Anuvakra**—the beginning of retrograde motion; **Vikala**—the abandonment or exit from retrograde motion; **Manda**—slow motion; **Mandatara**—exceedingly slow motion; **Sama**—mean or average motion. **Tatha**—likewise; **Shighra**—fast motion; **Atishighra**—exceedingly fast motion. **Ashtavidha**—these eight types; **Gati**—of motions; **Jneya**—should be understood. <note>In ancient Indian astronomy, planets do not actually move backward; "retrograde" (vakra) describes the optical illusion where a planet appears to move backward against the stars because of the Earth's own movement in its orbit.</note>

## Regarding Direct Motion

> **Verse:** Among these, very fast, fast, slow, very slow, and mean—these five are known as direct motion <term>Rjvi<gloss>straight or forward motion</gloss></term>. I shall now explain with care how planets, through the power of their various movements, daily reach a state where their observed positions match the calculations. <note>original: tatrātiśīghra śīghrākhya mandā mandatara samā | ṛjvīti pañcadhā jñeyā yā vakrā sānuvakritā | tattadgativaśān-nityam yathā dṛktulyatāṃ grahāḥ | prayānti-tatpravakṣyāmi sphuṭīkaraṇam-ādarāt ||</note>

**Interpretation:** **Rjvi**—regarding direct motion; **Tatra**—likewise; **Atishighra**—exceedingly fast; **Shighrakhya**—named fast; **Manda**—very slow <note>The Telugu commentary here slightly swaps the intensity of 'manda' and 'mandatara' compared to common usage, but the intent remains a hierarchy of slowness.</note>; **Mandatara**—slow; **Sama**—mean; **Panchadha**—these five types of motion; **Jneya**—are to be known. **Yavakra**—that which is retrograde; **Asanuvakrita**—that which is direct; **Tattat-gativashat**—by the power of those respective motions; **Nityam**—always; **Grahah**—the planets; **Drktulyatam**—the state where observation matches calculation <note>original: dṛktulyatva</note>; **Yatha**—in which manner; **Prayanti**—they attain; **Tat**—that; **Sphutikaranam**—method of correction <note>The process of turning a 'mean' position into a 'true' observed position.</note>; **Adharat**—with care; **Pravakshyami**—I am telling you.

## A Simple Method for the Eight Types of Motion

> **Verse:** Conjoined with the Sun and others, fast in the second; mean in the third, slow in the fourth; retrograde in the fifth and sixth, highly retrograde in the seventh; in the ninth or tenth their motion is tortuous; in the eleventh or twelfth, they become fast and exceedingly fast in sequence. <note>original: sūryamuktādiryaṃte śīghragāśca-dvitīyake | samaṃ tṛtīyake-yānti mandagatyāś-caturthake | vakra pañcama ṣaṣṭheke ativakroṣṭha-saptame | navame daśame-vāpi eteṣāṃ-kuṭilā gatiḥ | ekādaśe dvādaśevā śīghrātī-śīghratarā kramāt ||</note>

**Interpretation:** This is how you should understand it:
1. A planet joined with the Sun is "extinguished" <note>astamaya: invisible due to the Sun's glare</note>.
2. When it leaves the Sun, it "rises" <note>udaya</note> and moves at a **fast** speed.
3. In the third position, it moves at a **mean** (average) speed.
4. In the fourth, it moves at a **slow** speed.
5. In the fifth, it is in a **steady** state.
6. In the sixth, it is **retrograde**.
7. In the seventh, it is **retrograde**.
8. In the eighth, it is **highly retrograde**.
9. In the ninth, it returns to **direct** motion.
10. In the tenth, it has a **tortuous** or irregular motion.
11. In the eleventh, it moves at a **fast** speed.
12. In the twelfth, it moves at an **exceedingly fast** speed.

<summary>This page defines the eight qualitative types of planetary motion (such as retrograde and fast) and explains how a planet's speed changes relative to its angular distance from the Sun.</summary>
<keywords>eight motions, Vakra, Rjvi, planetary speed, retrograde, direct motion, Ganaka Ranjani, Indian astronomy</keywords>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Positions</header>

> **Verse:** Rahu always moves in a retrograde direction; the Moon and the Sun are always fast-moving. Cruel planets in retrograde are exceedingly harsh, while gentle planets in retrograde are highly auspicious. <note>original: vakragāmī-sadā-rāhuḥ śīghragau-candra-bhāskarau | krūra-vakrā-mahā-krūrā saumya-vakrā-suśodhanāḥ ||</note>

**Interpretation:** Rahu and Ketu <note>the North and South Lunar Nodes</note> are always considered retrograde <term>vakra<gloss>backward-moving</gloss></term>. The Moon and the Sun never move in retrograde; they always move in a direct, forward path <term>rju-gati<gloss>straight motion</gloss></term>.

> **Verse:** For a birth occurring before midday, subtract the time from the half-day duration. For a birth after midday, use the elapsed time in units of Ghatis and Vighatis. Take the remainder from the half-day calculation and add or subtract it based on the desired time. <note>original: madhyāhnāt-pūrva-janmani ghaṭī-tyāharad-ardha-śodhite | madhyāhnāt-parato-janma ghaṭikā-vighaṭīṣu-ca | dinārdha-śodhitaṃ śeṣa miṣyakālā-yutāyutam ||</note>

**Explanation:** For those born before midday, the time of birth in <term>Ghatikas<gloss>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</gloss></term> must be subtracted from the half-day duration to determine the elapsed time.

> **Verse:** Multiply the desired time (past or future) by the planetary speed and divide by sixty. The resulting value should be added or subtracted to find the instantaneous position of the planet. For Rahu, this rule is applied in reverse, as per the consensus of the scriptures. <note>original: gata-gamyeṣṭa-nāḍī-ghnī gatiṣ-ṣaṣṭi-vibhājitā | phalena-hīna-yuktā-cau dṛg-grahās-tātkāliko-bhavet | rāhor-vyatyayam-evaṃ-ca iti-śāstram-anummatam ||</note>

**Explanation:** To find the **instantaneous position** <term>tātkālika-graha<gloss>the precise position of a planet at a specific moment</gloss></term>, take the time interval (past or future) and multiply it by the mean speed for mean positions, or by the true speed for true positions. Divide the result by 60.
- If the time is in the past, subtract the result.
- If the time is in the future, add the result.
However, if a planet is in retrograde motion, the rule is reversed: add for past time and subtract for future time.

->**Regarding the Apogees** <note>original: adha mandoccāni - the points where planets are furthest from the Earth and move at their slowest</note><-

> **Verse:** The fixed position for the **Sun’s** apogee is 2-17-16-36 <note>Signs-Degrees-Minutes-Seconds</note>. For **Mars**, it is 4-10-2-20. For **Mercury**, the position is 7-10-26-33. For **Jupiter**, the apogee constant is 5-1-20-24. For **Venus**, it is 2-9-51-12. For **Saturn**, the son of the Sun, the value is 7-26-37-32. <note>The Sanskrit verse uses a numerical coding system to list these astronomical coordinates.</note>

The meaning of these values is clear.

<header>->Ganaka Ranjani<-</header>

### Now, the Correction of the Annual Apogees

> **Verse:** Using the values 517, 980, 544, 222, 374, and 597... add the resulting fruits to the positions of the planets to determine their true apogees. <note>original: śailendu-bāṇaiḥ 517 gaganāṣṭa-nandaiḥ 980 kṛtābdhi-bāṇaiḥ... rākṣāt-tat-phala-spoccha-kalāsu-yuñjāt ||</note>

**Explanation:** Take the <term>Dhruva-abda<gloss>the number of years elapsed since the epoch</gloss></term> and multiply or divide by these specific constants: 517 for the **Sun**, 980 for **Mars**, 544 for **Mercury**, 222 for **Jupiter**, 374 for **Venus**, and 597 for **Saturn**. By adding the resulting values to their respective positions, one obtains the <term>Sphuta Mandocha<gloss>the true longitude of the apogee, the point where a planet moves slowest</gloss></term> for the Sun and the other planets. These true apogees are then used to calculate the <term>Manda-sphuta<gloss>the corrected planetary position considering the slow equation</gloss></term>.

> **Verse:** I, Gopalakrishna Siddhanti of the Bharadwaja lineage, have composed this "Chapter on Mean Positions." <note>original: kārtāntikendra-candrasyāṃ bhāradvāja-sagotrīṇāṃ | gopālakṛṣṇa-siddhāntī madhyadhyāyo mayākṛtaḥ ||</note>

Thus concludes the First Chapter, the "Chapter on Mean Positions," composed by **Ralabandi Gopalakrishna Siddhanti**, the beloved disciple of the venerable **Brahmasri Mukkapaati Venkataramayya Siddhanti** and **Subbaraya Siddhanti**.

# CHAPTER ON TRUE POSITIONS
### The Beginning

---

> **Verse:** We worship day and night the single-tusked God, who is like a sun to the lotus-face of Parvati, and who grants many boons to his devotees. <note>original: agajānana-padmārkaṃ gajānanam-aharniśaṃ | aneka-dantaṃ-bhaktānā meka-dantam-upāsmahe || This is a traditional prayer to Lord Ganesha, the remover of obstacles.</note>

> **Verse:** The anomaly is the apogee minus the planet’s position. If the anomaly is in an odd quadrant, it is the elapsed portion; if in an even quadrant, it is the remaining portion. The <term>Bhuja<gloss>sine</gloss></term> and <term>Koti<gloss>cosine</gloss></term> are then derived. <note>original: spoccagraho-naṃ-kendraṃ-syā... bhujatrijyona-koṭijaṃ ||</note>

**Explanation:** When the position of a planet is subtracted from its own <term>Manda<gloss>apogee</gloss></term>, the result is known as the <term>Manda Kendra<gloss>the mean anomaly</gloss></term>. 
1. If this anomaly falls within an **odd quadrant** <term>Oja-pada<gloss>1st or 3rd quarters of the circle</gloss></term>, it is called the "elapsed sine" <term>Gata-bhuja</term>.
2. If it falls within an **even quadrant** <term>Yugma-pada<gloss>2nd or 4th quarters of the circle</gloss></term>, it is called the "remaining sine" <term>Gamya-bhuja</term>.

After calculating the **Bhuja** (the base side of the triangle) as will be explained hereafter, if you subtract that **Bhuja** from 3 signs <note>90 degrees</note>, the result is the **Koti** <term>Koti<gloss>the perpendicular side of the triangle, or cosine</gloss></term>.

<summary>This page marks the transition from the calculation of mean planetary positions to true positions. It provides mathematical constants for correcting planetary apogees and introduces the fundamental trigonometric concepts of Kendra (anomaly), Bhuja (sine), and Koti (cosine).</summary>

<meta>This page begins the "Chapter on True Positions" (Sphuta-adhyaya). It follows the previous page which concluded the calculations for Mean Positions and defined the values for planetary apogees.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER ON TRUE POSITIONS</header>

### The Method for Calculating the Bhuja
<note>The term *Bhuja* refers to the base or the sine-arc of a quadrant, used here to find the planet's position within its 90-degree sector.</note>

> **Verse:** If the anomaly is less than three signs, that value itself is the *Bhuja*. If it is greater than three, subtract it from six signs (the half-circle). If it is greater than six, subtract six from it. If it is greater than nine, subtract it from twelve signs (the full circle). <note>original: "kendreti-bhālve-tu-tadeva bāhu... gagaṇād-viśodhyam"</note>

**Explanation:** When you subtract the position of the <term>Mandocha<gloss>apogee; the point where the planet is farthest from Earth</gloss></term> from the planet’s current position, the remainder is called the <term>Kendra<gloss>anomaly; the angular distance from the apogee</gloss></term>. 
*   **The Odd Quadrants:** From 0 to 3 signs is the first <term>Ojapada<gloss>odd quadrant</gloss></term>. From 6 to 9 signs is the third odd quadrant.
*   **The Even Quadrants:** From 3 to 6 signs is the second <term>Yugmapada<gloss>even quadrant</gloss></term>. From 9 to 12 signs is the fourth even quadrant.

In an odd quadrant, we use the "elapsed" *Bhuja*. In an even quadrant, we use the "remaining" *Bhuja*. 

To find the <term>Koti<gloss>the perpendicular or cosine-segment</gloss></term>, subtract the *Bhuja* from 3 signs (90 degrees). Specifically:
1. If the anomaly is less than 3 signs, the anomaly itself is the *Bhuja*.
2. If it is greater than 3 but less than 6, subtract the anomaly from 6 signs to get the *Bhuja*.
3. If it is greater than 6 but less than 9, subtract 6 signs from the anomaly to get the *Bhuja*.
4. If it is greater than 9 but less than 12, subtract the anomaly from 12 signs to get the *Bhuja*.

Once you have the *Bhuja*, subtracting it from 3 signs gives you the *Koti*. This is the standard method for determining these coordinates.

### Practical Example

**Sun's Position:** 1 Sign, 2 Degrees, 3 Minutes, 41 Seconds.
**Sun's Apogee:** 2 Signs, 17 Degrees, 17 Minutes, 23 Seconds.
**Anomaly (Kendra):** 0 Signs, 24 Degrees, 13 Minutes, 45 Seconds.

Because this value is less than 3 signs, this value itself is the **Bhuja**. Since it falls in the first quadrant (an odd quadrant), it is the "elapsed" *Bhuja* of 0-24-13-45. Subtracting this from 3 signs (3-0-0-0) results in a **Koti** of 2-5-46-15. This is how the calculation is performed.

### Calculation of the Correction for Slow Motion
<note>Known as *Manda-sphuta-sadhanam*, this calculates the correction needed because planets do not move at a constant speed in perfect circles.</note>

> **Verse:** Multiply the degrees and other units by 180 and subtract... multiply by the result... divide by 60 to find the minutes... add to the previous result to find the Sine... this yields the result for the Sun's correction. <note>original: "bhāgādi-bhūdhṛti-guṇaṃ viśodhya-phalena hatvā... bhujā-phalaṃ-syāt"</note>

**Explanation:** Convert the *Bhuja* into degrees. Subtract these degrees from 180 and treat the remainder as the "fruit." Take the degrees, minutes, and seconds of the *Bhuja* and multiply them by either the "four-fold circular" or "nine-fold circular" factors <note>referring to specific trigonometric table constants used in this system</note>. Divide the lower units by 60 and carry them up to the next unit. When you add this to the degrees of the *Bhuja*, you obtain the <term>Bhujajya<gloss>the Sine of the Bhuja</gloss></term>. By dividing this Sine by the specific divisors for each planet, you arrive at the <term>Bhuja-phala<gloss>the equation of center; the correction value</gloss></term>.

<summary>This page defines the mathematical rules for calculating the Bhuja (sine-arc) and Koti (cosine-arc) based on a planet's anomaly, providing a practical example using the Sun's position. It also introduces the formula for calculating the equation of center (Manda-phala) to determine a planet's true position.</summary>
<keywords>Bhuja, Koti, Kendra, Anomaly, Mandocha, Apogee, Ojapada, Yugmapada, Manda-sphuta, Equation of Center, Indian Astronomy</keywords>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>GANAKA RANJANI <note>The name of the treatise; translates to "The Mathematician’s Delight"</note></header>

**Vocabulary:** Mean differences, Sun, Moon, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn, True difference, Method for calculating slow-motion true tables.

### Mean Differences (Madhya-bhedas)

> **Verse:** For the Sun, the value is four-five-three-four (4534); for the Moon, eyes-nine-one (991); for Mars, elephants-nine (89); for Mercury, seven-three-one-one (3131); for Jupiter, nails-space-space-one (1800); for Venus, three-zero-three-five (5303); for Saturn, four-four-nine-eight (8944). These are the values the wise sages call the Mean Differences. <note>The Sanskrit verse uses symbolic words for numbers: "Netra" (eyes) for 2, "Danti" (elephants) for 8, "Nakha" (nails) for 20. The translator has synchronized these with the numerical digits provided in the text.</note>

**Explanation:** The mean differences are as follows:
*   **Sun:** 4534
*   **Moon:** 991
*   **Mars:** 89
*   **Mercury:** 3131
*   **Jupiter:** 1800
*   **Venus:** 5303
*   **Saturn:** 8944

### Determining True Difference via the Sine-Arc (Bhuja-phala)

> **Verse:** For the Sun (Bhaskara), ten (10); for the Moon, one-and-two-eyes (21); for Mars, three-and-five (53); for Mercury, two-and-five (25); for Jupiter, two-and-six (26); for Venus, twelve (12); for Saturn, three-and-four (34). Divide the result of the <term>Bhujajya<gloss>the sine of the anomaly</gloss></term> by these values to find the true difference.

**Explanation:** By dividing the *Bhujajya* (sine-arc) by these specific divisors—10 for the Sun, 21 for the Moon, 53 for Mars, 25 for Mercury, 26 for Jupiter, 12 for Venus, and 34 for Saturn—you obtain a quotient. Subtract this quotient from the respective Mean Difference listed above; the remaining value is the <term>Sphuta-bheda<gloss>True Difference</gloss></term>.

### Method for Calculating Slow-Motion (Manda) True Tables

### Calculating the True Difference for the Sun

**Difference:** The Mean Difference for the Sun is 4534. <note>The text notes "Suryo-yuga-agni..." referencing the numerical code from the first verse.</note>

> **Verse:** For the Sun, divide by ten; for the Moon, twenty-one...

**Explanation:** Divide the Sun's *Bhujajya* by 10, and the Moon's *Bhujajya* by 21. After obtaining these quotients and subtracting them from the mean differences, if the True Difference is not yet refined, certain corrections must be applied to the *Bhujajya* itself.

### Scriptural Rule for the Sun’s Correction:

> **Verse:** From zero to ten degrees, the calculation is fully refined by the difference. From ten to thirty degrees, half of the difference is used. From thirty to forty-five degrees, use the result divided by three. From forty-five to fifty degrees, there is no change. Beyond that, for the <term>Koti<gloss>the cosine or perpendicular segment</gloss></term>, a negative correction (subtraction) must be applied to the difference.

<sig>3</sig>

## Mercury’s Specific Calculation

> **Verse:** Apply the <term>Cosine<gloss>Kotijya</gloss></term> correction as you would for the Moon, and refine it by a factor of twelve. Using the number thirty (30) as a boundary, the portions are designated as positive. If the value exceeds this, perform a subtraction; this variation in the Sine is known as the result. <note>original: "Koṭijyā candrasaṃskāraṃ dvādaśāntaṃca saṃskaret | khādiśūnyāgni 30 varma stubhāgāstu dhanasaṃjñikāḥ | tathādhike-ruṇakuryāt bhedamyajyā phalasmṛtaṃ"</note>

**Explanation:** Depending on the <term>Anomaly<gloss>Kendra; the angular distance of a planet from its furthest point from Earth</gloss></term>, the following corrections must be added to or subtracted from the "Difference" (orbital constant):
*   From **0 to 9 degrees**: Add the full Cosine value to the Difference.
*   From **10 to 19 degrees**: Add half the Cosine value.
*   From **20 to 29 degrees**: Add one-third of the Cosine value.
*   From **30 to 49 degrees**: No Cosine correction is needed; the standard Sine calculation produces the **True Difference**.
*   From **50 to 90 degrees**: Subtract one-sixth of the Cosine value from the Difference to find the True Difference.

Additionally, divide the Cosine by 12 (as is done for the Moon's calculations). For the first 3 degrees of the result, this value is added (*positive*); beyond that, it must be subtracted (*negative*).

### Direct Observation Table <note>This table provides reference values for Mercury's Sine (Bhuja) and Cosine (Kotijya) at 10-degree intervals.</note>

| Sine Arc | Cosine | Difference | Correction Applied |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1 Sine | 139 Cosine | 69-31 Difference | 4584-30 (Full) |
| 10 Sine | 1700 Cosine | 66-43 Difference | 4397-43 (Half) |
| 20 Sine | 3200 Cosine | 62-15 Difference | 4234-49 (1/3rd) |
| 30 Sine | 4500 Cosine | 56-12 Difference | 4024-0 (None) |
| 40 Sine | 5603 Cosine | 50-37 Difference | 3937-0 (None) |
| 50 Sine | 6500 Cosine | 42-43 Difference | 3877-0 (1/6th Sub.) |
| 60 Sine | 7300 Cosine | 33-36 Difference | 3719-0 (1/6th Sub.) |
| 70 Sine | 7700 Cosine | 23-45 Difference | 3746-0 (1/6th Sub.) |
| 80 Sine | 8000 Cosine | 12-6 Difference | 37-32 (1/6th Sub.) |
| 90 Sine | 8100 Cosine | 0-0 Difference | 5724-0 (1/6th Sub.) |

->Following this same method for the Moon.<-

**Explanation:** By dividing the <term>Sine Arc<gloss>Bhujajya</gloss></term> by the **True Difference**, one obtains the <term>Sine Equation<gloss>Bhujaphala</gloss></term>. This equation is added (*positive*) if the Anomaly is in the first six signs (Aries through Virgo) and subtracted (*negative*) if it is in the latter six signs (Libra through Pisces).

### Method for Applying Sine and Cosine Equation Corrections

> **Verse:** The Sine Equation is positive or negative depending on whether the Anomaly is in the Aries or Libra half of the zodiac. Similarly, the Cosine Equation is positive or negative depending on whether the Anomaly is in the Cancer or Capricorn quadrants. <note>original: "bhujāphalaṃ dhanaruṇaṃ syāt meṣajūkādikeṃdrage | koṭijyādiphalṃ keṃdre svarṇaṃkarkimṛgādige"</note>

**Explanation:** If the Anomaly is within the first 6 signs (0-180°), it is considered "Aries-starting" (<term>Meshadi</term>). If it is between 6 and 12 signs (180-360°), it is "Libra-starting" (<term>Tuladi</term>). When the Anomaly is Aries-starting...

It also introduces the general rules for when planetary corrections should be treated as positive or negative values based on their zodiacal quadrant.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for planetary corrections. While the previous page focused on Mercury's unique adjustments, this page explains the general application of "positive" (additive) and "negative" (subtractive) corrections based on the planet's position relative to the zodiac signs, followed by a worked example for the Sun.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>Ganakaranjani <note>Delight of Calculators</note></header>

The <term>Sine Result<gloss>Bhujaphalam; the correction derived from the sine of the planet's anomaly</gloss></term> is additive. In the quadrant starting from Libra <note>Tula-adikendra</note>, the Sine result is negative and must therefore be subtracted from the planetary positions. 

Regarding the <term>Cosine Anomaly<gloss>Koti-kendra</gloss></term>: from 3 signs to 9 signs is the "Cancer-onwards" quadrant. From 9 signs to 3 signs is the "Capricorn-onwards" quadrant. For the "Cancer-onwards" quadrant, the <term>Velocity Result<gloss>Gatiphalam; the correction to the planet's daily speed</gloss></term> is positive. It should be understood that for the "Capricorn-onwards" quadrant, the Velocity result is negative.

->Direct Demonstration<-

**Anomaly <note>Kendra</note>:** 0 [signs] - 24 [degrees] - 13 [minutes] - 42 [seconds]. 
The **Sine <note>Bhuja</note>** is the same: 0-24-13-42. 
The **Cosine <note>Koti</note>** is 9-5-46-14. 

Searching the Sine degrees in the <term>Mathematical Tables<gloss>Nighantu</gloss></term>, the remainder is 115-46. For the Sine degree of 13, the result is 15-46. Multiplying these by the **Rule of Four Operations** <note>Chathushprakri-nyayana; a specific algorithmic method for interpolation or proportional adjustment</note>, the products are 37105, 1114, 36, 1014, and 4. Dividing 1014 by 60, the quotient is added to the previous figure; then multiplying by the "mean," we get 31313. Dividing this by 60 gives a quotient of 525-25. Adding this to the previous degree figure, the total is 37732-25, which becomes the **True Sine <note>Bhujajya</note>**.

Taking this True Sine and dividing by 60 for the Sun, the quotient is 322. Subtracting 322 from the Sun’s <term>Divisor<gloss>Cheda</gloss></term> of 4534, we get 4212, which is the <term>True Difference<gloss>Sphuta-bheda</gloss></term>.

Following the procedure to be described hereafter, the **True Cosine <note>Kotijya</note>** obtained is 60-36. For the Sun, because the Anomaly is less than 30 degrees, the value of 1-52 is positive. Adding this to the True Difference of 4212, we get a refined True Difference of 4265. 

By dividing the True Sine (37732-25) by this, the quotient for degrees is 0. Multiplying the remainder by 60 gives 2263982. Dividing this by the True Difference, the quotient for **minutes <note>liptas</note>** is 54. Multiplying the remainder (3047) by 60 and dividing by the True Difference gives the **seconds <note>viliptas</note>**. These values (0-54-15), when added to the Sun (since it is in the Aries-onwards quadrant), result in the **True Sun <note>Sphuta-Suryudu</note>** position of 1 [sign] - 23 [degrees] - 54 [minutes] - 6 [seconds].

->Procedure for Calculating the Cosine Result<-

> **Verse:** Divide the Cosine degrees and parts by thirteen (13). Subtract the resulting degrees from seventeen (17). Take the thirteen-fold Cosine portions and multiply them by the remaining degrees using the Rule of Four. Divide by sixty (60), and always add the result to the previous figure. Dividing this by the Cosine-difference yields the Velocity Result. <note>original: "koṭibhāgādirāméndu 13 bhaktābhāgādijaṃ phalaṃ | saptaindu 17 hīnabhāgādi sthāpyakoṭitrayodaśā | aṃśabhāgādigunitā ṣaṣṭhyāptaṃ praphalayuksadā | koṭijyābheda bhaktaṃ tu labdhaṃgatiphalaṃbhavet"</note>

**Explanation:** Convert the Cosine signs and degrees into total degrees and divide by 13. Subtract the resulting degrees from 17 degrees. Using the remaining degrees and the "thirteen-fold Cosine portions," multiply according to the **Rule of Four Operations**. Divide the result by 60 to find the "mean" value. Divide by 60 again and add it to the previous degree figure to obtain the **True Cosine <note>Kotijya</note>**. By dividing that True Cosine by their respective differences...

It introduces the formula for calculating the "Velocity Result" using a specific divisor of thirteen.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for planetary corrections. Building on the previous page's discussion of anomaly (kendra), it now details the calculation of "True Motion" (spashtagati) and provides the specific numerical constants (bhedas) for each planet required for these trigonometric adjustments.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header># Chapter on True Positions <note>Spashtadhyayah; the section of an astronomical treatise dedicated to calculating the actual, observable positions and speeds of planets as opposed to their theoretical average positions.</note></header>

When divided, the result is the <term>Velocity Result<gloss>Gatiphalam; the correction applied to a planet's average daily speed</gloss></term>. By applying this velocity result to the <term>Anomaly<gloss>Kendra</gloss></term>, and adjusting the <term>Mean Motion<gloss>Madhyagati</gloss></term> based on whether the planet is in the <term>Cancer-onwards<gloss>Karkadi</gloss></term> or <term>Capricorn-onwards<gloss>Makaradi</gloss></term> quadrants, the <term>True Motions<gloss>Spashtagatis</gloss></term> are obtained. For example, if the position is in the Cancer-onwards quadrant, the value must be subtracted from the mean motion to find the true motion. In this manner, the <term>Slow True Motions<gloss>Manda-spashtagatis</gloss></term> are established for all planets.

## The Differences <note>Bhedas; specific numerical constants or divisors used in planetary equations.</note>

> *Verse:*
> *The Sun’s factor is thirty (30); the Moon’s is the treasury of arts <note>original: "kalanidhi"; a poetic term for the number 1, but here referring to specific lunar constants</note>; for Mars it is fifteen (15).*
> *Mercury’s is fifteen (15) <note>original: "panchendu"; five and ten</note>; Jupiter’s is the earth and the oceans <note>astronomical code for 41</note>; Venus is the same.*
> *Saturn is thirty-five (35) <note>original: "panchagni"; five and three</note>; these are the refined differences derived for the calculated motion.*
> *Dividing by the Cosine, the result in minutes becomes the Velocity Result.*

## Direct Demonstration

When the <term>Cosine<gloss>Koti</gloss></term> of 9 [signs] - 5 [degrees] - 42 [minutes] - 08 [seconds] is converted, it becomes 9. When this is divided by 3, the result is 3-3-33 in degrees. Subtracting this from the 12 parts of the <term>Thirteenth-Division<gloss>Trayodashamsha</gloss></term>, the remainder is 8-56-27. Taking 11-12 according to the **Rule of Four Operations**, and multiplying it by the thirteenth-division degree of 1-8, then dividing by 28 and adding the results in order, the <term>Cosine Sine<gloss>Kotijya</gloss></term> comes to 1.0-3. Taking the **Sun's Difference** of 30 and dividing, we get 11 minutes and some seconds. Since the Anomaly is in one sign, we subtract this velocity result of 3-18 from the Sun's [mean] motion of 59-8, resulting in a **True Motion** of 52-2.

I shall now write an example to easily obtain the decimal values as mentioned previously.

## Direct Demonstration

Subtract the <term>Sine degrees<gloss>Bhuja-bhagadi</gloss></term> from 90. Multiply the remaining degrees by the original Sine degrees. For the remaining positions (not including degrees), divide by 20 and add to the total to find the <term>Sine<gloss>Bhujajya</gloss></term>. By dividing this Sine by the **True Differences**, the resulting values will be the respective <term>Sine Results<gloss>Bhujaphalam</gloss></term> for each planet.

**Differences:** For the **Sun** 54-34; for the **Moon** 18-51; for the **Mars** 17-0; for the **Mercury** 59-28; for the **Jupiter** 5-00; for the **Venus** 3-03; for the **Saturn** 2-54. These are the **Mean Differences**.

Using the Sine as a base, divide the Sine by 10 for the Sun, 2 for the Moon, 5 for Mars, 5 for Mercury, 3 for Jupiter, 2 for Venus, and 2 for Saturn. Subtract the resulting quotient from their respective Mean Differences to obtain the **True Differences**. Dividing by these True Differences yields the <term>Slow Sine Result<gloss>Manda-bhujaphalam</gloss></term>.

<meta>This page continues the specific mathematical procedures for planetary "True Motion" (Spashta Gati) begun on the previous page, then transitions into calculating the Precession of the Equinoxes (Ayanamsha) and the Ascensional Difference (Chara), which is used to determine the length of the day based on geographic location.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
<header>Delight of the Calculator <note>Ganakaranjani; the title of the treatise.</note></header>

### True Motion <note>Spashta Gati; the corrected speed of a planet.</note>

Divide the <term>cosine degrees<gloss>kotibhaga</gloss></term> by 17. Subtract the result from 13. With the remaining degrees, multiply the <term>thirteenth part of the cosine<gloss>koti-trayodashamsha</gloss></term> using the <term>four-step method<gloss>chatur-prati-nyayana</gloss></term>. Divide the lower values by 60 and add them to the top to obtain the <term>cosine<gloss>kotijya</gloss></term>. 

Divide this cosine value by 30 for the Sun, 1 for the Moon, 10 for Mars, 11 for Mercury, 18 for Jupiter, 35 for Venus, and 24 for Saturn. The resulting minutes are their respective <term>results of slow motion correction<gloss>manda-gati-phalam</gloss></term>. This correction should be applied as an addition or subtraction depending on whether the planet is in the <term>Cancer-onwards<gloss>karkadi</gloss></term> or <term>Capricorn-onwards<gloss>makaradi</gloss></term> quadrants. By performing this calculation, the planets’ <term>true slow motions<gloss>manda-spashtas</gloss></term> are determined.

### Calculation of the Precession of the Equinoxes <note>Ayanamsha-sadhanam; the method for calculating the shift in the celestial equator relative to the fixed stars over time.</note>

> *Verse:*
> *Subtract sixty-squared <gloss>3600</gloss> from the years passed in the Kali Era.*
> *Subtract the result from the "directions-moon" <note>original: "ashanka"; 110</note> and divide by sixty for the Precession.*

**Interpretation:** Subtract 3600 from the current <term>Kali Era years<gloss>kalyadyabda</gloss></term>. Write the remainder in two places. Divide the lower value by 10. Subtract the resulting minutes from the upper value. Divide that total by 60; the result is the <term>Precession of the Equinoxes<gloss>ayanamsha</gloss></term> in degrees.

### A Simple Method for Calculating Precession.

### Calculation of the Ascensional Difference <note>Chara-sadhanam; calculating the difference between a 12-hour day and the actual length of the day due to latitude and the Sun's declination.</note>

> *Verse:*
> *Subtract the "earth-eyes-oceans" <gloss>421</gloss> from the Shaka Era year.*
> *Multiply by three and divide by "sky-mountains-Ashvins" <gloss>200</gloss> for the Precession.*
> *Multiply the equinoctial shadow by twenty; subtract the "eight-limbs" <note>numerical code</note> to find the middle.*
> *Divide by three for the final; this is the Ascensional Difference of one's own land.*

**Interpretation:** Multiply the <term>local equinoctial shadow<gloss>svadesha-vishuvacchaya</gloss></term> (calculated as 3-36) by 20; this is the <term>initial ascensional difference<gloss>adi-chara</gloss></term>. Divide this initial value by 4; subtract the result from the initial value to find the <term>middle ascensional difference<gloss>madhya-chara</gloss></term>. Divide the initial value by 37 to find the <term>final ascensional difference<gloss>antya-chara</gloss></term>. Halving these results gives the <term>half-ascensional differences<gloss>charardhas</gloss></term>.

### Ascensional Differences <note>Charalu</note>

> *Verse:*
> *Hands-mountains <gloss>72</gloss>, arrows-hands <gloss>54</gloss>, and Vedas-eyes <gloss>24</gloss> are the Ascensional Differences in order.*

### Segments of Ascensional Difference <note>Chara-khandalu</note>

> *Verse:*
> *Six-Rama <gloss>36</gloss>, Cow-Ashvins <gloss>29</gloss>, and Cancer <note>original: "karka"; 12</note> are the three segments of the Ascensional Difference.*

### Calculation of the Length of the Day <note>Ahassu-sadhanam</note>

> *Verse:*
> *Multiply the "anomaly of the Sun with precession" by the segments of the Ascensional Difference...*
> *Multiply by the start of the ascensional difference in order...*

<meta>This page follows the calculation of the Precession of the Equinoxes (Ayanamsha) and moves into the calculation of the Ascensional Difference (Chara), which is used to determine the exact length of the day and night for a specific location. It then provides a general formula for the daily duration of all planets.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>### Chapter on Clarification <note>original: "Spashtadhyayah"; the chapter concerning the correction of planetary positions.</note></header>

> *Multiply the first segment by one, then multiply the middle segment by the remainder.* 
> *Combine the first and middle segments and multiply the final segment by the remaining degrees.*
> *Divide each by sixty repeatedly; the resulting difference is added to the previous values.*
> *Leaving aside the remainder of the degrees, divide everything by sixty.*
> *Add the result to the sign position to find the breath-units of ascensional difference.*
> *In the north, the ascensional difference is added; in the south, it is subtracted.*
> *By doing this, one finds the length of the day; subtract this from sixty to find the length of the night.*

**Interpretation:** By adding the <term>precession of the equinoxes<gloss>ayanamsha</gloss></term> to the <term>true sun<gloss>spashta-ravi</gloss></term>, you obtain the <term>tropical sun<gloss>sayana-ravi</gloss></term>. To find the <term>ascensional difference<gloss>bhujasadhana</gloss></term> for this tropical sun: if the sign position is zero, multiply the degrees and minutes individually by the <term>initial difference segment<gloss>adi-chara</gloss></term>. If the sign position is one, place the initial segment in the sign position and multiply the degrees by the <term>middle difference segment<gloss>madhya-chara</term>. If the sign position is two, add the initial and middle segments together, place them in the sign position, and multiply the degrees by the <term>final difference segment<gloss>antya-chara</gloss></term>. 

Divide these by 60, add the results to the degrees, and then divide the degrees by 30. The resulting value is added to the <term>segmental differences<gloss>charakhandas</gloss></term> to find the <term>ascensional difference in seconds<gloss>chara-vinadis</gloss></term>. Divide these seconds by 60; the quotient represents <term>ghadiyas<gloss>units of 24 minutes</gloss></term> and the remainder represents <term>vighadiyas<gloss>units of 24 seconds</gloss></term>. 

If the tropical sun is within the first six signs (Aries to Virgo), it is considered <term>northern declination<gloss>meshadigunaka</gloss></term> and the value is positive. If the tropical sun is within the latter six signs (Libra to Pisces), it is considered <term>southern declination<gloss>yamyam</gloss></term> and the value is negative. For a northern declination, add these units to 30; for a southern declination, subtract them from 30. This result is the <term>length of the day<gloss>aha-pramana</gloss></term>. Half of this value is <term>midday<gloss>dinardha</gloss></term>. Subtract the length of the day from 60 to find the <term>length of the night<gloss>ratri-pramana</gloss></term>.

### Calculation of the Day-Length for the Sun and all other Planets.

> **Verse:**
> *Multiply the planet's motion by the rising breath-units and divide by 10,800.*
> *Add the result to the 21,600 sidereal breath-units to find the individual units of the day and night.*

**Interpretation:** Multiply the <term>true motion<gloss>spashta-gati</gloss></term> of a planet by the <term>rising time in breath-units<gloss>udaya-prana</gloss></term> for its specific location. Divide this by 10,800 <note>original: "khakhashtaika"; numerical code for 10,800</note>. Add the resulting quotient to the <term>sidereal breath-units of the celestial circle<gloss>chakrasavah</gloss></term>, which is 21,600. The sum represents the <term>total day and night units<gloss>ahoratra-asavah</gloss></term>. Dividing this by 60 gives the total duration of the day and night for any planet from the Sun to Saturn.

It also provides a mathematical formula to determine the daily cycle duration for all major planets based on their motion and rising times.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical astronomical calculations from the previous page, specifically moving from the length of the day to the primary components of the Indian almanac (Panchangam): the Tithi, Nakshatra, and Yoga.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>THE DELIGHT OF MATHEMATICS <note>original: "Ganita Ranjani"</note></header>

# Direct Observation
True daily motion of the Sun: 60 - 26. True daily motion of the Moon: 808 - 15 minutes. The <term>standard swan-measure<gloss>hamsa-pramana</gloss></term> is 330 <term>seconds of arc<gloss>viliptas</gloss></term>. When multiplied by 6, this equals 1,980 <term>breath-units<gloss>asuvas</gloss></term> <note>One breath-unit or 'asu' equals four seconds of time.</note>. The resulting calculation for these breath-units is 67-35 for the Sun and 90-16 for the Moon. By adding these breath-units to 21,600 and dividing by 360, the <term>duration of the day and night<gloss>ahoratra-pramana</gloss></term> is found to be 60-10 for the Sun and 62-37 for the Moon. Similarly, for the higher motions, by adding the <term>precession of the equinoxes<gloss>ayanamshas</gloss></term> as before and determining the result through the <term>ascensional difference<gloss>charardha</gloss></term>, the day's length can be calculated just as it was for the Sun.

# Determination of the Lunar Day <note>original: "Tithi Sadhanamu"</note>
> **Verse:** *Subtract the Sun’s position from the Moon’s and divide the degrees by twelve <note>original: "karachandramah," where hands (2) and moon (1) signify the number 12</note>; these are the lunar days. Multiply the past and remaining portions by sixty and divide by the difference in motion; the result is added to or subtracted from midday.*

**Interpretation:** When the position of the <term>true Sun<gloss>sphuta-surya</gloss></term> is subtracted from that of the <term>true Moon<gloss>sphuta-chandra</gloss></term>, it is called the <term>Moon-without-Sun<gloss>vyarkendu</gloss></term>. Convert those "Moon-without-Sun" degrees and divide by 12; the result is the number of elapsed <term>lunar days<gloss>tithis</gloss></term>. If the remainder is less than 33 <note>This likely refers to a specific arc-minute threshold used in this specific school of astronomy</note>, it is considered the "past" portion of the current lunar day; if it is more, it is the "remaining" portion. Multiply the past portion by 60, and again by 60, and divide by the difference between the Moon’s motion and the Sun’s motion (expressed in minutes). This yields the <term>ghadiyas<gloss>units of 24 minutes</gloss></term> and <term>vighadiyas<gloss>units of 24 seconds</gloss></term>. To find the remaining time, subtract the remainder from 12, convert to minutes, and divide by the relative motion of the Sun and Moon <note>original: "vyarkendu-gati"</note> multiplied by 60. If the time has passed midday, subtract it; if it is yet to come, add it. This provides the exact time for the lunar day.

# Determination of the Lunar Mansion <note>original: "Nakshatra Sadhanamu"</note>
> **Verse:** *Multiply the Moon’s degrees and minutes by three and divide by eighty <note>original: "shunya-abhira," representing 80</note> to find the mansions. Multiply the past and future portions by sixty, then by three, and divide by the Moon’s motion. The resulting units of time are to be adjusted relative to midday.*

**Interpretation:** Convert the position of the <term>true Moon<gloss>sphuta-chandra</gloss></term> into degrees. Multiply those degrees, minutes, and seconds by 3 and divide by 80; the result is the number of elapsed <term>lunar mansions<gloss>nakshatras</gloss></term>. The remainder of the 80 units indicates the portion "passed." The part remaining is the "future" portion. If calculating the past portion, convert it to minutes as is. If calculating the future portion, subtract the remainder from 80, multiply by 60, and add the minutes. To find the time in <term>ghadiyas</term> and <term>vighadiyas</term>, divide this by the Moon’s true motion multiplied by 3. Based on whether the time is past or future relative to midday, perform the necessary adjustments.

# Determination of the Luni-Solar Yoga <note>original: "Yoga Sadhanamu"</note>
> **Verse:** *Combine the degrees of the Sun and Moon. Multiply the minutes by three and divide by eighty <note>original: "kha-abdhi," where space (0) and oceans (8) signify 80</note> to find the Yogas. Multiply the past and future portions by sixty and divide by the combined motion of both multiplied by three. The units of time are added or subtracted from the half-day marker to identify the upcoming or past Yoga.*
