<meta>This page continues the technical calculations of the almanac, transitioning from general solar and lunar positions to the specific calculation of the Tithi (lunar day) and Karana (half-lunar day). It provides a worked example for a Sunday in the month of Jyeshtha.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER ON TRUE POSITIONS <note>original: "Sphuta Adhyayah"</note></header>

Take the positions of the <term>true Sun<gloss>sphuta-surya</gloss></term> and the <term>true Moon<gloss>sphuta-chandra</gloss></term>, calculate the difference in degrees, and convert the degrees and minutes by multiplying and dividing by 60. By determining the elapsed and remaining portions, one should adjust these within the <term>day-and-night cycle<gloss>ahoratra</gloss></term> to find the <term>time-units<gloss>ghatikas</gloss></term>.

## Common Year, month of Jyeshtha, Waxing Fortnight, 1st [Lunar Day], Sunday.

**True Sun** 2. 4-26-27 [motion] 66-48. **True Moon** 6-24-5-38 [motion] 720.44. **Moon-minus-Sun <note>original: "Vyaktamshu," a variation of Vyarkendu</note>** 4-20.3-11 [motion] 643-58. When the degrees of the Moon-minus-Sun are calculated, they equal 140. Dividing this by 12, the result is the 12th lunar day <note>original: "Dvadashi"</note> which has passed.

The remainder is 8. Subtracting this from 12, the balance is 4. Multiplying this by 60 gives 240. Converting this into <term>seconds of arc<gloss>viliptas</gloss></term> gives 12,048. Dividing this by the motion of the Moon-minus-Sun (643), the result is 18-44. Converting this again to seconds of arc and dividing gives the <term>time-measure<gloss>ghadiya-vighadiya</gloss></term> of 18-24. Adding this to the <term>length of the day<gloss>ahassu</gloss></term> (26-18), the total <term>lunar day duration<gloss>tithi-ghadiyas</gloss></term> is 44-42. In this same manner, by dividing the motion of the <term>constellation<gloss>nakshatra</gloss></term> and the <term>lunar-solar yoga<gloss>yoga</gloss></term> by 60, and dividing the results, the time-units for the Nakshatra and Yoga are obtained.

## DETERMINATION OF THE HALF-LUNAR DAY <note>original: "Karana Sadhanamu"</note>

> **Verse:** *Double the number of the lunar day, subtract one for the past day, and determine the remainder. Through the portions of the upcoming day, the remaining counts are the Karanas starting with Bava. Adding the durations of the previous and current lunar days and halving them gives the time-units of the Karana.*

**Interpretation:** To find the required <term>half-lunar day<gloss>karana</gloss></term>, double the number of the Tithi and subtract one if it is the past Tithi. Subtract similarly for the upcoming Tithi. The remainder after dividing by 60 indicates the Karana, starting with <term>Bava<gloss>bava-adi karana</gloss></term>. By adding the time-units of that Tithi and the previous Tithi and halving the sum, the time-units of the Karana are found. If the sum exceeds 60, subtract 60 and halve the remainder. If the Tithi’s time-units fall within the day’s duration, the Karana units are set accordingly.

> **Verse:** *The Karanas in the dark fortnight are: Shakuni in the second half of the fourteenth day, Chatushpada in the first half of the new moon day, Nagava in the second half [of the new moon], and Kimstughna in the first half of the first day of the waxing fortnight.*

**Interpretation:** The <term>Shakuni<gloss>shakuni</gloss></term> Karana occurs in the second half of the fourteenth day of the dark fortnight; <term>Chatushpada<gloss>chatushpada</gloss></term> in the first half of the New Moon day <note>Amavasya</note>; <term>Nagava<gloss>nagava</gloss></term> in the second half of the New Moon; and <term>Kimstughna<gloss>kimstughna</gloss></term> in the first half of the first day of the waxing fortnight. These are <note>The letter 'స్థి' (sthi) is written as a subscript correction under 'సి' (si)</note> **fixed half-days** <note>original: "sthirakaranalu"</note> and they do not require these mathematical calculations.

<summary>This page details the mathematical steps to calculate the duration of Tithis, Nakshatras, and Yogas, featuring a worked example for a Sunday in Jyeshtha. ### Method for Determining the Remaining Time of Lunar Days and Constellations.

> Verse: Multiply the measure of the lunar day and constellation by sixty. Divide that by the movement [of the planets]; the result will be the remaining hours and minutes. <note>original Sanskrit: "Khatarkas tithi nakshatra parimana kala hataah | Tad yogatya vibhaktashchai deshya nadi vinadikah ||" Note: 'Khatarka' refers to the number 60.</note>

**Meaning:** The measure of a <term>lunar day<gloss>tithi</gloss></term> is 720, the measure of a <term>constellation<gloss>nakshatra</gloss></term> is 800, and the measure of a <term>lunar-solar conjunction<gloss>yoga</gloss></term> is 800. Multiply these by 60.
- For the **Lunar Day**: Divide by the motion of the Moon-minus-Sun <note>original: "vyarkendu gati"</note>.
- For the **Constellation**: Divide by the motion of the Moon.
- For the **Yoga**: Divide by the combined motions of the Sun and Moon.
The results obtained will be the <term>remaining time<gloss>eshya ghatika</gloss></term> in traditional units of hours and minutes.

# Chapter on True Positions <note>original: "Sphuta Adhyaya"</note>

### On Calculating the Lunar Day <note>original: "Tithi Tappuku"</note>

> Verse: Add the time units of the previous day and the following day together; half of that sum is said to be the duration of the lunar day, constellation, or alignment for that specific day. <note>original Sanskrit: "Yaddinasya tu purvedyuh paredyughatikayutam | tadardham taddinam nadya tithiruktasvayogake ||"</note>

Explanation: To find the duration for a <term>tithi</term> <note>a lunar day</note>, <term>nakshatra</term> <note>star constellation</note>, or <term>yoga</term> <note>astronomical alignment</note>, one must add the <term>ghatikas</term> <note>a traditional unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> of the preceding day and the following day. When this total is halved, it results in the <term>ghatikas</term> representing the duration of the middle day's lunar day, constellation, or alignment.

# Ganaka Ranjani <note>The Mathematician's Delight</note>

> Verse: For Anuradha, Dhanishta, and Shravana, the value is 10. For Mula, it is 56. For Ashlesha, 32. For Rohini, 40. For Purva Bhadrapada, 16. For Ashwini, 20. For Ardra and Hasta, 21. For Uttara Phalguni and Shatabhisha, 18. For Bharani, Uttara Bhadrapada, and Purvashadha, it is 24. For Pushya, Purva Phalguni, Chitra, and Uttarashadha, the value is 20. <note>original Sanskrit: "anūrādhādhanistā ca śravanaṃ daśamaṃ tathā... uttarāṣāḍhaviṃśatiḥ"</note> — *Partially clear.*

> Verse: Take the poisonous points <note>visha-nadis</note>, multiply them by eight, and divide by sixty. The resulting time units <note>ghatikas</note> should be added to the previous constellation to find the poisonous period.

**Another Method:**

> Verse: Subtract the duration of the previous constellation from 60 and add the current constellation's duration. Multiply this total duration by the specific poison-measure of that constellation, then divide by 60. Add the result to the end-time of the previous constellation.

**Explanation:** Subtract the <term>ghatikas</term> <note>a unit of 24 minutes</note> of the previous constellation from 60, and add the duration of the current constellation. This gives the total duration from the start to the end of the current constellation. Multiply this total by the "poisonous measure" <note>visha pramana</note> specific to that star, divide the product by 60, and add the resulting time to the end-time of the previous star.

---

> Verse: If the result exceeds 60, the period occurs during the day; if it is less than the day's duration, it occurs during the night of the previous day. If the value exceeds the day's measure, it falls in the night of that day. <note>This verse describes how to determine the solar timing of the inauspicious period.</note>

**Explanation:** If the total calculated from the previous constellation's end-time exceeds 60, that <term>tyajyam</term> <note>inauspicious time to be avoided</note> occurs during the daytime of the current day. If the value is less than 60 but more than the duration of the day, it occurs during the night. If the total does not even reach the duration of the day, the <term>tyajyam</term> occurred during the previous day. For calculating the night-time <term>tyajyam</term>, add 4 to the night duration, then subtract the night's measure to find the remaining <term>tyajyam</term>. Dividing the total duration of the constellation or <term>tithi</term> <note>lunar day</note> by 15 also yields the duration of the <term>tyajyam</term> period.

### Calculation of the Nectar Constants <note>original: "Amrita Dhruva Sadhanam"</note>

> Verse: Starting from Ashwini, the fixed nectar points are determined. To find the Nectar Point <note>Amrita Dhruva</note>, take the Poison Point <note>Visha Dhruva</note> and add 12 to it. If the sum exceeds the limit, subtract 12... <note>original Sanskrit: "aśvaniviṣṭadhṛvo henato mṛtadhṛvasya... saṃbhavanti vedabāhu arasaṃyateṣudhyaho"</note>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Calculations</header>

# Chapter on Calculations <note>original Sanskrit: "Sphuta Adhyaya." This chapter deals with the "true" or "corrected" positions of celestial bodies and time units.</note>

Explanation: If one removes the poisonous time units <note>Visha Ghadikas</note> from the constellation of **Ashwini**, the remainder of Ashwini becomes nectar-like <note>Amritatva; auspicious</note>. If it falls within the poisonous units of **Mrigashira**, it also attains nectar-like qualities. In the constellation of **Mula**, if 12 units are refined, it becomes nectar-like. For the remaining constellations, if they are aligned with their respective constants <note>Dhruvas</note>, they also attain nectar-like qualities.

## Inauspicious Times of the Week: Rahu Kalam

**Verse: The Moon, Saturn, the son of Bhrigu (Venus), Mercury, Jupiter, Mars, and the Sun; the Rahu Kalam is declared to increase by three-quarters in this order.**
<note>original Sanskrit: "vidhu ssauri bhṛgusuta jña jīvobhābhāskarāḥ | tripā datraṇa vṛddhi syā drāhu kālamudāhṛtaṃ ||"</note>

Explanation: On Monday at 3 3/4 <note>ghatikas</note>, Saturday at 20 1/4, Friday at 11 1/4, Wednesday at 5 1/4, Thursday at 14 1/4, Tuesday at 22 1/4, and Sunday at 26 1/4—starting from these times during these days, a period of 4 <term>ghatikas</term> <note>a unit of 24 minutes; 4 ghatikas equals 1 hour and 36 minutes</note> is known as **Rahu Kalam**, which must be avoided for auspicious works.

## Inauspicious Ascendants <note>Lagna Tyajyam</note>

**Verse: In Aries, Taurus, Sagittarius, and Virgo, it is the Serpent (Bhujanga). In Pisces, Capricorn, and Cancer, and at the end of Scorpio, it is Rahu. In Gemini, Aquarius, Libra, and in the middle of Leo, it is the Vulture (Gridhra). These are considered by all sages to be avoided in all works.**
<note>original Sanskrit: "aja vṛṣa bha dhanuḥ kanyakādau bhujaṃgaḥ | jhaṣamakara kuḷīre vṛścikānte carāhuḥ | midhuna ghaṭa tulānāṃ siṃha madhyetu gradhraḥ | sakalamunimitaṃ syā tsarvakāryeṣu varjaṃ ||"</note>

Explanation: For the <term>Lagnas</term> <note>Ascendant signs</note> of Aries, Taurus, Sagittarius, and Virgo, the first 8 ghatikas are called "Bhujanga" <note>Serpent</note> and must be avoided. For the Lagnas of Gemini, Cancer, Scorpio, and Capricorn, the final 8 ghatikas are "Rahu" and must be avoided. For Gemini, Aquarius, Libra, and Leo Lagnas, the middle portion of the duration is called "Gridhra" <note>Vulture</note>. These times are unsuitable for all ceremonies and rituals.

## Determining the Mathematical Constant <note>Dhruva Sadhana</note>

**Verse: Multiply the desired elapsed quarters (padas) by ten and divide by three to find the Dhruva and its components.**
<note>original Sanskrit: "iṣṭāpūrva navāṃśa kādaśa guṇahatātrvaptānādyadhṛvāḥ ||"</note>

Explanation: Multiply the elapsed quarters <note>padas</note> of a constellation by 10 and divide by 3; the result provides the degrees of the **Dhruva** <note>a fixed mathematical point or constant</note>. Multiply the remainder by 60 and divide by 3 to find the arc-minutes <note>lipras</note>.
**Example:** For the third quarter of the constellation **Mula**, the elapsed quarters are 2. Thus, 2 multiplied by 10 is 20. Dividing 20 by 3 gives a result of 6-40-0 <note>6 degrees, 40 minutes</note>. When converted into the zodiacal sign format, the third quarter's remainder/position is recorded as Sign 4, 6° 40' 0".

## Calculating the Entry of the Sun and Other Planets into Stellar Quarters

**Verse: If the planet's position is less than the Dhruva, it is upcoming (eshya). If the planet's position is greater than the Dhruva, it is past (gata). Divide the difference in arc-seconds by the planet's daily motion to find the time of entry.**
<note>original Sanskrit: "dhṛvasyahīnaṃ bhuvaneṣyamāṇaṃ grahasyahīnaṃ dhṛvayātisaṃjñā | tayorviliptā svagativibha ktā horādyamadhe na ga tai rvya yo jyaṃ ||"</note>

Explanation: If the planet’s position is less than the calculated **Dhruva**, it is an "upcoming entry" <note>eshya</note>. If the planet's position is greater than the Dhruva, it is a "past entry" <note>gata</note>. Therefore, subtract the planet's position from the Dhruva (or vice versa), multiply by 60 to convert to arc-minutes <note>lipras</note>, and divide by the planet’s specific "true motion" <note>sphuta-gati</note> to find the day of entry. Multiply the remainder by 60 and divide by the true motion to find the <term>ghatikas</term>, and multiply the subsequent remainder by 60 to find...

These refined values, obtained by multiplying and dividing the past portions, are used for the purpose of identifying the entry <note>the entry of a planet into a specific division of space or time</note> and performing mathematical corrections. This allows for the calculation of the planetary entrance.

> **Verse:** The planetary positions, starting from the degrees, are multiplied by the "fast" factors and divided by the constant 4,400... The resulting value is known as the "Rashi" <note>zodiac sign</note> factor.

# Correction of Mars and Other Planets
# Beginning

---

### Method of Determining the Fast Conjunction <note>Shighra</note>

> **Verse:** For Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn, the Sun is known as the "Fast Conjunction" <term>Shighrocca</term>. For Mercury and Venus, the Mean Sun is the "Middle Planet," and they possess their own "Fast Conjunction" points.

Explanation: For the planets Mars <note>Kuja</note>, Jupiter <note>Guru</note>, and Saturn <note>Shani</note>, the Sun acts as the **Shighrocca** (the point of fast motion or conjunction). For Mercury <note>Budha</note> and Venus <note>Shukra</note>, the Sun is considered the "Middle Planet" <note>Mean Planet</note>, and their specific "Fast Conjunctions" are calculated accordingly.

### Calculation of the Anomaly <term>Kendra Sadhana</term>

> **Verse:** Subtracting the planet from the "Fast Conjunction" point is called the Variable Anomaly <term>Chala Kendra</term>. If this exceeds six signs, it should be subtracted from the full circle. If the Anomaly begins with Aries, it is a "Positive Sign" for the Sine; if it begins with Libra, the Sine is calculated based on the remainder.

Explanation: When you subtract the planet's position from its "Fast Conjunction" point <note>Shighrocca</note>, the result is called the **Chala Kendra** (Variable Anomaly). If this Anomaly is within the signs starting from Aries <note>Mesha</note>, it is treated as a positive calculation for the "Arm" <term>Bhuja</term> (the sine of the arc). If the Anomaly exceeds 6 signs (entering Libra/Tula), one must determine the Sine by subtracting from the total circle. Once the "Arm Sine" <note>Bhujajya</note> is determined, if you subtract that from 3 signs (90 degrees), the result is the "Perpendicular" or "Complementary Sine" <term>Koti</term>.

> **Verse:** If the Anomaly is less than three signs, that itself is the "Arm" <note>Bahu/Bhuja</note>. If it is greater than the "Fast Conjunction" point, subtract it from the full circle... If it exceeds six signs, subtract six signs; if it exceeds nine signs, subtract it from the full circle <note>the 12 signs of the zodiac</note>.

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>True Positions of Mars and Others <note>original: "Kujadula Sphutam"</note></header>

# Determination of the Half-Sine and Sine Segments

> **Verse:** The sines are established—30 and 5 [5-30]; then seven parts [7-0 or 8-0] <note>The verse uses 'Saptamsha' (seven), but the commentary interprets the calculation as leading to 8-0</note>; and the 'Yugara' [4-0] minutes are the true values.

**Explanation:** When the anomaly <term>Kendra; the angular distance of a planet from its perihelion or aphelion</term> is at the first zodiacal sign position, the first sine value is 5-30. If the anomaly is at the second zodiacal sign position, the second sine value is 8-0. If the anomaly is at the third zodiacal sign position, the third sine value is 4-0. In this manner, the necessary sine values are generated.

## Procedure for Calculating the True Sine

> **Verse:** From the divided degrees, double the degrees; then quadruple the degrees and subtract.

**Explanation:** When calculating the first sine based on the anomaly, whatever the degrees are, those degrees are subtracted from the first sine value of 5-30 to obtain the "True Sine." When the second sine is required based on the anomaly, one should multiply by the "seven parts" (7-0) and then "double the degrees" (multiply the degrees below the sign by two) and subtract that from the value to get the second True Sine. When the third sine is required, one should take the third sine value of 4-0 and (following the rule "quadruple the degrees and subtract") multiply the lower degrees by four. To perform the subtraction from 4-0, one should borrow to make it 3-60 <note>borrowing 1 unit/60 minutes to allow subtraction of minutes</note> and then subtract to find the True Sine. This is how the sines for the Base <term>Bhuja; the side of a right triangle or the sine segment</term> and Perpendicular <term>Koti; the adjacent side or cosine segment</term> are determined.

## Calculation of the Base-Sine and Perpendicular-Sine

> **Verse:** Multiply the sine minutes and seconds; multiply the degrees and minutes by four; with the middle sine and sixty, divide by sixty and sixty, and add them in order.

**Explanation:** To calculate the desired sine for either the Base or the Perpendicular, first determine the Base and Perpendicular of the anomaly. Taking the sine according to the zodiacal sign position of the Base and Perpendicular, place the minutes and seconds of the sine and the minutes and seconds of the Perpendicular degrees according to the "Rule of Four" <note>likely a specific mathematical proportion or quadrant rule</note>. Multiply them by their respective degrees and minutes, divide by 60, and add the result to the middle value. Divide that by 60 again, add it to the degree position, divide by 60 once more, and finally add it to the number in the zodiacal sign position.

> **Verse:** If the zodiacal sign position is zero, designate it as zero. For one sign, divide by three; for twelve, add five.

**Explanation:** If the zodiacal sign position of the Base or Perpendicular is zero, the resulting value should also have zero added to it. According to the rule "for one sign, divide by three," if the sign position is 1, then 1 and 3 are added to make 4; that 4 is then added to the result. According to the rule "for twelve, add five," <note>original: "dvadasapancabhiyutam"</note> if the sign position is 2, then 5 is added to make 7; this 7 is added to the number in the sign position to get the total result.

<summary>This page outlines the trigonometric algorithms for calculating the true sine values (Sphuta Jiva) of planets like Mars, using specific coefficients and adjustment rules for different quadrants (Bhuja and Koti).</summary>
<keywords>Sine segments, Khanda Jiva, Mars, Sphuta, Bhuja, Koti, Kendra, Jyotisha, Indian trigonometry</keywords>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>->The Mathematician’s Delight<- <note>original: "Ganaka Ranjani"</note></header>

### Regarding Interpolation

> **Verse:** Now I shall describe the interpolation, including the seconds and minutes, the minutes and degrees, and the signs and parts. Let the wise one, discerning in the slowness of intellect, divide the whole by eighty. <note>original: "ashta-vibhagena" (by eighty)</note>

**Explanation:** Add the signs into the degrees; add the degrees into the minutes; add the minutes into the seconds. Dividing the total by 70 <note>The Telugu commentary says 70, though the Sanskrit verse specifies 80; such variations are common in regional astronomical traditions</note> results in the <term>Bhuja-jya; the sine of the base</term> and the <term>Koti-jya; the sine of the perpendicular</term>.

### Mean Difference for Determining the Base-Result of Mars and Others

> **Verse:** The difference for Mars's slow-correction <note>original: "manda-sphuta"</note> is divided by sixty (60). For Mercury, it is always twenty-one (21) for the variable-correction. For Jupiter, the difference is divided by four hundred and forty-four (444). The diameter for Venus is eleven (11) for the variable-correction. The difference for Saturn is divided by sixteen (16). Apply these to the minutes of the base and perpendicular to find the true correction.

**Explanation:** Dividing the <term>Manda-sphuta bheda; the difference in the planet's position after the first correction for its elliptical orbit</term> of Mars by 60 yields the variable-correction in minutes and seconds. For Mercury, the factor is 21. For Jupiter, dividing the difference by 444 yields the variable-difference. For Venus, it is 11 minutes. For Saturn, dividing the difference by 16 yields the variable-difference. By applying the corrections of the sine and cosine to these variable-differences, one obtains the <term>Sphuta Shighra Bheda; the true corrected variable difference</term>.

### Correction of the Variable Differences

> **Verse:** These values for Mars and the others are combined with the parts of the base; combined or subtracted with the perpendicular sines, they become the most accurate.

**Explanation:** For the five planets starting with Mars, divide the base (Bhuja) by two and add it to the difference. By either adding or subtracting the perpendicular (Koti-jya), the "True Difference" is obtained.

### Correction of the Perpendicular Component

> **Verse:** If the perpendicular is positive, add it; if the perpendicular is negative, subtract it. One must always know the positive or negative quality based on the position of the anomaly in the odd or even quadrants. Add one-eighth of Mars's base-sine for the true correction.

**Explanation:** The perpendicular <note>original: "Koti-jya"</note> is positive if the <term>Kendra; the angular distance or anomaly</term> is within the first 3 signs. From 3 signs to 6 signs, it is negative. From 6 signs to 9 signs, it is negative. From 9 signs to 12 signs, it is positive. For a positive...

<meta>This page continues the specific mathematical procedures for the "five planets" (Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn) introduced on the previous page. It details the refinement of their "True Positions" by moving from the "Slow Correction" (Equation of Center) to the "Fast Correction" (Equation of Conjunction), and then calculating daily motion, declination, and latitude.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>->True Positions of Mars and the Others<-</header>

This is combined with the difference. When the negative side is subtracted from the difference, it becomes the <term>Sphuta-shighra-bheda; the true difference for the fast-correction</term>. For the base specifically, when divided by the <term>Bhuja-phala; the base-result</term>, the minutes and other values are added. This yields the true difference.

### Correction for the Fast-Base-Result
<note>Shighra-bhuja-phala Samskaram</note>

> **Verse:** Multiply the degrees of the sine of the base by sixty to find the minutes, and multiply again to find the seconds. Divide this by the true fast-difference. If the <term>Shighra-kendra; fast-anomaly or center</term> is in the signs starting with Aries, the result is added; if it is in the signs starting with Libra, the result is subtracted from the <term>Manda-samskara-graha; the planet already corrected for its elliptical orbit</term>. This yields the true position. <note>original Sanskrit: "liptikrta-bahujya sphuta-shighra-bheda-bhajitaiphalam..."</note>

**Explanation:** Take the base-sine degrees, multiply by 60 to get minutes, add any existing minutes; multiply by 60 again to get seconds and add any existing seconds. Divide this total by the <term>Shighra-bheda; the fast-correction difference</term> (also converted to seconds). The resulting value in degrees and minutes is the <term>Bhuja-phala; result of the base</term>. If the <term>Shighra-kendra; the angular distance from the sun</term> is between Aries and Virgo, add this to the previously corrected planet. If it is between Libra and Pisces, subtract it. This results in the final <term>Sphuta; true longitude</term>.

### Calculating the True Fast-Motion of Mars and Others
<note>Kujadinam Shighra-sphuta-gati-sadhanam</note>

> **Verse:** Take one-twelfth of the fast-result in degrees and subtract it from the mean diameter. Multiply the difference between the fast-motion and the true slow-motion by the diameter using the rule of four, and divide by their respective fast-differences. Subtract the result from the fast-motion to find the true motion. If the result is greater than the motion, the planet is in <term>Vakra-gati; retrograde motion</term>. <note>original Sanskrit: "vyasam-shighra-phalansha-arkai..."</note>

**Explanation:** Take the fast-base-result, divide it by 12, and subtract that quotient from the mean diameter. To find the "center-motion," subtract the <term>Manda-sphuta-gati; the planet's daily motion after the first correction</term> from its <term>Shighra-gati; the daily motion of its fast-anomaly</term>. Multiply this center-motion by the diameter (after the fast-correction adjustment) and divide by the planet’s specific fast-difference. Subtracting this from the fast-motion gives the true daily motion. However, if this calculated result is greater than the fast-motion itself, the planet is moving backward in the sky, which we call <term>Vakra; retrograde motion</term>.

### Calculation of Declination
<note>Kranti-sadhanam</note>

> **Verse:** Divide the sine of the base by seventy-six (76). Subtract that result from four hundred and forty-three (443). Divide the sine of the base by the remaining value to find the declination in degrees. <note>original Sanskrit: "bhujajya-shastra-turaga bhajatala..."</note>

**Explanation:** Divide the <term>Bhuja-jya; the sine of the planet's longitude</term> by 76. Take that result and subtract it from 443. Then, divide the original sine of the base by this new remainder. The result provides the <term>Kranti; declination</term>, which is the planet's angular distance north or south of the celestial equator, expressed in degrees.

### Calculation of Latitude
<note>Vikshepa-sadhanam</note>

> **Verse:** Divide the sine of the base of the slow-correction by one thousand and three (1003). Subtract the quotient from thirty-eight (38). Divide the sine of the base by the remaining amount to find the latitude for the Moon and the other planets. <note>original Sanskrit: "bhujajya-lochana-rupa-shunye-ndugam..."</note>

**Explanation:** Divide the <term>Manda-bhuja-jya; the sine of the planet's base after the first correction</term> by 1003. Subtract the resulting value from 38. Finally, divide the sine of the base by this remainder. This produces the <term>Vikshepa; celestial latitude</term>—the planet's distance from the ecliptic—for the Moon and the other visible planets.

<header>Gaṇakarañjani (The Delighter of Calculators)</header>

**Vocabulary:** Mars and others, Calculation of true positions, Fast apex, Slow apex, Calculation of diameter portions, Differences in slow-true positions, Mathematics.

**Explanation:** The calculation of the true positions for Mars and the other planets follows the same method described in the chapter on True Positions (*Sphuta-adhyaya*).

> **Verse:** Dividing the degrees of the cosine by thirteen, the result is in degrees, etc. <note>original Sanskrit: "koṭibhāgādirāmendu bhaktābhāgādijaṃphalam"</note>

With this and another verse, I have explained the calculation of the <term>Sphuta-gati; the true daily motion</term> of the planets; that same method should be applied here. For Mars and the other planets, the true position is determined through two <term>Shighra-samskaras; corrections for the "fast" or solar-related anomaly</term>.

> **Verse:** For the star-planets, the fast-apex is the Sun; for others, the planets themselves are the fast-apex. <note>original Sanskrit: "tārāgrahakarmyōśśīghra śīghrōccamitārēgrahaḥ"</note>

**Explanation:** For Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn, the Sun acts as the <term>Shighrochcha; the fast apex or point of conjunction</term>. For Mercury and Venus, the Sun’s position at the end of the planet’s own calculation is considered the fast apex.

Before finding the <term>Bhuja; the sine-component</term> and <term>Koti; the cosine-component</term> to calculate the positions, I will first provide the <term>Mandochchas; the slow apices or apogees</term> of the planets.

> **Verse:** Nineteen (19), two hundred and twenty (220), one hundred and seventy (170), eighty (80), two hundred and forty (240). <note>This verse uses a numerical code system to provide the longitudes of planetary apogees.</note>

**Explanation:** The apogees are as follows:
*   **Mars:** 4 Signs, 9 Degrees, 0 Minutes, 0 Seconds
*   **Jupiter:** 5 Signs, 20 Degrees, 0 Minutes, 0 Seconds
*   **Venus:** 2 Signs, 20 Degrees, 0 Minutes, 0 Seconds
*   **Saturn:** 8 Signs, 0 Degrees, 0 Minutes, 0 Seconds
Just as we calculated the true position of Mars earlier using specific factors, we now perform the <term>Manda-sphuta; the slow-correction for the elliptical orbit</term>. The components of the <term>Bhuja</term> and <term>Koti</term> should be derived as previously explained.

### Calculation of Diameter Portions for Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn
<note>Vyasamsha-sadhanam</note>

> **Verse:** The differences for Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn are multiplied by four, three, and seven... <note>original Sanskrit: "kujajīvaśanibhedā yugaragni 3 nagatāhatāḥ..."</note>

### Mean Differences for Mars and Others
<note>Kujadulaku Madhyabhedalu</note>

> **Verse:** Fifty-four (54), one hundred (100), three hundred and twenty (320)... the differences are corrected by the cosine. <note>original Sanskrit: "śaravedāḥ 54 khakhendavaḥ 100 dvyangākāśakhadantāḥ 320..."</note>

**Explanation:** The differences are: Mars: 45; Mercury: 100; Jupiter: 57; Venus: 320; Saturn: 63. According to these values, if the <term>Manda-koti-jyartha; the cosine value of the slow anomaly</term> of the respective planet is positive, it must be added; if it is negative, it must be subtracted. These are the <term>Manda-sphuta-bhedas; the slow-true differences</term> for Mars and the others. If the <term>Manda-karma; the process for slow-correction</term> is applied to Mars and the others just as it is for the Sun and Moon, their true slow motions will be obtained.

### Calculation of Diameters for Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn

> **Verse:** The differences for Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn are multiplied by four, three, and seven. <note>original Sanskrit: "kujajīvaśanibhedā yugāgni nagatāhatāḥ"</note>

**Explanation:** In the phrase "multiplied by four, three, and seven," it means multiply the difference for Mars by 4, Jupiter by 3, and Saturn by 7. Then, these should be divided by the values 15 for Mars, 7 for Jupiter, and 6 for Saturn. This calculates the <term>Vyasa; the planetary diameter or orbital range</term>.

**Explanation:** For Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn, the corrected divisors of half the <term>Manda-koti; the cosine of the slow anomaly</term> are calculated. Specifically, for Jupiter, multiply by 3; for Saturn, multiply by 2; for Mars, multiply by 10. Then, divide the result by 10 for Mars, 3 for Jupiter, and 7 for Saturn. The resulting values are their respective <term>Vyasas; diameters or base values</term>. These are the diameters for Mercury and Jupiter as well.

> **Verse:** Those values combined with the fast-sine and corrected by half the cosine result in the <term>Shighra-cheda; the fast divisor or hypotenuse</term>. <note>original Sanskrit: "taddhostrīvaṃśayutā śīghra cedāḥ koṭyardha saṃskṛtāḥ"</note>

**Explanation:** Subtract their respective <term>Shighrochcha; fast apex</term> from the <term>Manda-sphuta; slow-corrected true planets</term> to find the <term>Kendra; anomaly or center</term>. From this, calculate the <term>Bhuja; sine-component</term> and <term>Koti; cosine-component</term> to obtain the <term>Bhujajya; sine</term> and <term>Kotijya; cosine</term>. Divide their respective sine values by 3; add the result to their diameters. For the cosine values, if the result is positive, add it; if it is negative, subtract it. This result yields their <term>Shighra-cheda; fast divisors</term>. (2) Dividing the fast-sine by the fast divisor according to the previous rules yields the <term>Shighra-bhuja-phala; the correction for the fast sine</term>. By applying this as a positive or negative correction to the slow-corrected planet, the <term>Sphuta; true position</term> is obtained.

## CALCULATION OF TRUE DAILY MOTION

> **Verse:** Subtracting the fast correction in degrees divided by twelve (12) from the diameter, multiply by the difference in motion of the planet and its fast-apex. Divide by the divisor and subtract from the fast-apex motion to find the <term>Shighra-gati; fast daily motion</term>. <note>original Sanskrit: "vyāsaṃ śīghraphalāṃśārkā 12 bhāgonaṃ grahaśīghrayoḥ | gatyantaraghna chedā ptaṃ tyaktvā śīghragatergatiḥ"</note>

**Explanation:** Divide the fast-sine correction by 12. Subtract the result from their respective diameters. Take the difference between the fast-apex motion and the slow-corrected motion. Apply the <term>Chatush-shashti-nyaya; the rule of sixty-four</term> to this difference and divide by the fast divisor. Subtract the resulting value from the <term>Shighrochcha-gati; the daily motion of the fast-apex</term> to find the <term>Sphuta-gati; the true daily motion</term>. If the resulting correction is greater than the motion of the fast-apex, and the fast-apex motion is subtracted from it, the result is <term>Vakra-gati; retrograde motion</term>. Because there is a slight discrepancy in this, we provide another method for calculating the true daily motion.

> **Verse:** Subtract the slow motion from the fast motion, multiply by the diameter, and divide by the fast divisor. Take that result and multiply by ten. Divide by sixty-four or sixty-five. If the result is very high, the motion is reversed or retrograde. <note>original Sanskrit: "mandagatyā nitā śīghra bhuktyā vyāsa hatā hṛtāḥ | śīghra chedena tallabdhaṃ phalabhāgbhyo daśāhatā | catuṣṣaṣṭi pañcaṣaṣṭhai rā ptaṃ tyaktvā śīghragatergatiḥ | yadā phalamanalpañce dvakrabhuktirviparyayāt"</note>

**Explanation:** Subtract the slow-true motion from their respective fast motions. Multiply by the diameter and divide by the fast divisor. Take the result and find the <term>Bhogyakhanda; the tabular sine difference</term> for the fast-sine correction in signs and degrees. Using this sine difference, multiply the previous result by sixty-four...

### TRUE POSITIONS OF VENUS AND SATURN DEMONSTRATED

The mean position of the Sun is 4-7-37-0 <note>This notation refers to Signs-Degrees-Minutes-Seconds</note>. Adding this to the <term>Shighrochcha; fast apex</term> of Venus 11-7-10-9 results in:
The <term>Manda-kendra; slow anomaly</term> is 1-17-32-0. The <term>Bhujajya; sine</term> is 5-48 and the <term>Kotijya; cosine</term> is 5-27. Since the cosine is positive, having reasoned and applied the positive/negative corrections to the <term>Manda-cheda; slow divisor</term>, the corrected divisor for half the cosine is 32-44. Following the previous rules, dividing by this divisor yields a negative <term>Bhuja-phala; sine correction</term> of 1-6-33. Subtracting this gives the <term>Manda-sphuta; slow-corrected</term> Venus as 8-6-25-27. Because the Sun is the governing body for Venus, multiply the <term>Ravi-gati; Sun's motion</term> of 59-8 by the cosine and divide by the slow divisor. The result 0-59, being a positive cosine, is subtracted to find the <term>Manda-sphuta-gati; slow-corrected motion</term> of 58-9.

Subtracting the fast apex from the slow-corrected Venus gives the <term>Shighra-kendra; fast anomaly</term> of 4-25-38-18. Its sine is 4-13 and its cosine is 7-0. Dividing the sine by 36 yields 1-4. Adding this to the <term>Vyasa; diameter</term> gives 12-4. In this case, because it is a negative cosine, subtract the cosine to find the <term>Shighra-cheda; fast divisor</term> of 5-24. Dividing the sine by this divisor yields the sine correction in signs etc. as 1-16-51-10. Subtracting this yields the <term>Shighra-sphuta; true</term> position of Venus as 2-18-34-17. The fast motion is 79-4. Subtracting the slow-corrected motion gives the <term>Kendra-gati; anomaly motion</term> of 72-54. Multiply this by the mean diameter of Venus 11 and divide by the fast divisor to get 75-34. The sine correction in signs is 1-16-51-10. Since this is in the 1st sign position, the <term>Sphuta-khanda; true segment</term> is 3-13. By this, multiply the 75-34 by the four-fold sine-multiplier, add the middle, process the value, add on top, multiply by 7, and divide by 16. The result is 57-10. Subtracting this from the fast motion of 56-8 results in the True Motion of 38-58.

The corrected divisor for half the cosine is 48-17. The positive sine correction is 0-7-7. The slow-corrected Saturn is 2-0-56-56. The negative cosine correction is 0-16. The positive sine is 7-31 and the positive cosine is 3-13. The fast divisor is 44-25. The sine correction is 6-3-13. The true Saturn is 2-7-0-9. The quotient obtained is 12-29. This is the <term>Shighra-bhuja-phala; correction for the fast anomaly</term> converted into signs and degrees. Multiply this by 4-25, increase the magnitude, then multiply again by 4 and divide by 15. The resulting value in minutes and seconds is 14-7. Subtracting this from the <term>Shighra-gati; fast motion</term> results in a <term>Sphuta-gati; true daily motion</term> of 5-1. This is the standard procedure.

I shall now describe another method for determining the <term>Sphuta; true position</term> by calculating the <term>Kendra; anomaly</term> using the <term>Manda; slow</term> and <term>Shighra; fast</term> table-indices.

## Simplified Method for True Position

> Verse: Subtract the planet from the slow apex, or the fast apex from the planet, to determine what is called the <term>Kendra; anomaly</term>. If the result is greater than six signs, or if it exceeds three signs, calculate the <term>Bhuja; base sine</term> by subtracting it from the half-circle or the full circle. The <term>Manda-bhuja; slow base</term> is thus found; for the <term>Shighra; fast anomaly</term>, if it exceeds six signs, subtract it from the full circle to find the base.
> <note>original: "గ్రహంమృదూచ్చాధ ధనంచలోచ్చా త్త్యక్త్వావశేషం కథయం త్తిక కేంద్రం..."</note>

**Explanation:** The <term>Kendra; anomaly</term> is found by subtracting the planet's position from the <term>Mandochcha; slow apex</term> or the <term>Shighrochcha; fast apex</term>. If it is a <term>Manda-kendra; slow anomaly</term>, and the value is within 3 signs, that value itself is the <term>Bhuja; base</term>. If it is between 3 and 6 signs, subtract it from 6 signs to get the base. If it is between 6 and 9 signs, subtract 6 signs from it. If it is between 9 and 12 signs, subtract it from 12 signs to find the <term>Manda-bhuja</term>. For the <term>Shighra; fast anomaly</term>, if it is within 6 signs, that value is the base; if it is above 6 signs, subtract it from 12 signs to find the <term>Shighra-bhuja</term>. <note>This process reduces any angle to a value between 0 and 90 degrees for use with sine tables.</note>

## Calculating Slow and Fast Table Indices

> Verse: Divide the degrees of that base by fifteen; the quotient is the <term>Padaka; table-index</term> for both the slow and fast corrections. Multiply the remainder by the difference between the current and next table values, then divide by fifteen to find the proportional result. Add or subtract this from the table entry depending on whether the values are increasing or decreasing.
> <note>original: "తద్బాహుభాగాః పవనై గిర్వి భక్తా మాందేచశీఘ్రేచ ఫలం పదస్యాత్..."</note>

**Explanation:** Convert the <term>Manda-bhuja</term> and <term>Shighra-bhuja</term> into degrees and divide by 15. The quotient gives the <term>Padaka; table-index</term>. Take the remainder (including the minutes) and multiply it by the difference between the current table entry and the next one. Divide this by 15. If the values in the table are increasing, add this result to the current entry; if they are decreasing, subtract it. This final result is the <term>Bhuja-phala; correction value</term>.

## Correction of the Base Result

> Verse: Apply the resulting <term>Bhuja-phala</term> in minutes to the slow apex or the fast apex respectively. Perform these corrections for the celestial bodies. Add the correction if the anomaly is in the half-circle starting with Aries, and subtract if it is in the half-circle starting with Libra.
> <note>original: "కలాదికంబాహుఫలంసమేతం మృదూచ్చ వంశీఘ్రసమన్వితంచ..."</note>

<note>This final verse instructs the calculator on the directional application of the corrections based on which "side" of the zodiac the planet currently occupies.</note>

It explains how to reduce a planet's anomaly to a base sine (bhuja) and interpolate between table entries provided in 15-degree increments.</summary>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>GANAKA RANJANI</header>

Thus, the <term>Bhujaphala; the correction derived from the base sine</term> should be divided by 60. The resulting degrees and minutes should be applied to the planets already corrected by the <term>Shighra-manda-samskara; the combination of fast and slow anomalies</term>. If the <term>Kendra; anomaly</term> begins with Aries (the first six signs), the value is <term>Dhana; positive/additive</term>. If the anomaly begins with Libra (the latter six signs), it is <term>Runa; negative/subtractive</term>.

- For all other calculations, the <term>Ravi-bhujaphala; solar correction</term> must be applied to arrive at the <term>Sphuta; true position</term>.

> Verse: Divide the <term>Bhujaphala; base correction</term> by twenty-seven <note>original: "శైలలోచనైః" (shailalochanaiḥ) — literally "mountains (7) and eyes (2)," signifying the number 27</note>. The resulting minutes should be applied to the Moon in the same manner as the planets.
> <note>original: "భుజాఫలకలాభానొ భాజితా శైలలోచనైః । ఫలంకలాసుమధ్యేందో సంస్కుర్యా త్తి గ్రహ స్తివత్ ॥"</note>

**Explanation:** Through this process of <term>Manda-samskara; slow correction</term> <note>This correction accounts for the eccentricity of the orbit</note>, the Sun and the Moon reach their true positions.

### True Position of Mars and Other Planets

> Verse: For Mars and the other planets, to find the true position, first apply half of the <term>Shighra-phala; fast correction</term>. Then apply half of the <term>Manda-phala; slow correction</term>. Next, apply the full slow correction to the mean planet, and finally apply the entire fast correction. By doing this, the celestial bodies reach their true positions.
> <note>original: "కుజాది కానాంతు పది స్ఫుటత్వం మధ్యేవిధాయా సుఫలార్ధమా దౌ । తతై్రవిమాందస్య ఫలస్యచార్ధం మధ్యేపునమాం దఫలంసమస్తం । శీఘ్రంఫలం సర్వమి హైవకుర్యా దేవం కృతేతేఖచరాస్స్ఫుటాస్యుః ॥"</note>

**Explanation:** For the five planets starting with Mars (Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn), one must:
1. Apply half of the fast correction.
2. Apply half of the slow correction.
3. Apply the full slow correction to the <term>Madhya-graha; mean planet</term>.
4. Apply the full fast correction.
Through these four steps, the true positions of Mars and the others are established.

### Calculating the True Daily Motion

> Verse: Multiply the <term>Madhya-gati; mean daily motion</term> by ten and divide by fifty-six <note>original: "శరైగిర్విభక్తా" — 'shara' (5) and 'giri' (7), though the Telugu text interprets the math as 56</note>. The resulting value in seconds is the correction. Add this to the motion if the anomaly is in the signs starting from Cancer, and subtract if it is in the signs starting from Capricorn. This is the prescribed <term>Manda-gati; slow motion</term>.
> <note>original: "మధ్యేగతిస్తత్త్వ ద శాంతరఘ్నో । శరైగిర్విభక్తా వికలాః ఫలంతత్ । స్వర్ణం గతౌకర్కిమృగాదికేంద్రే । కుర్యాదియం మందగతి ప్రదిష్టాత్ ॥"</note>

**Explanation:** To find the true daily motion, multiply the mean motions by the difference of ten and divide by 56. If the anomaly is a <term>Karkadi-kendra; Cancer-beginning anomaly</term>, add the result to the motion. If it is a <term>Makaradi-kendra; Capricorn-beginning anomaly</term>, subtract it from the mean motion to arrive at the <term>Sphuta-gati; true daily motion</term>.

<vocab>Bhujaphala: Equation of the center/Base sine correction, Samskara: Correction/Processing, Sphuta-graha: True planetary position, Kujadi-pancha-graha: The five planets (Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn), Manda-gati: Slow or corrected daily motion, Kendra: Anomaly/Angular distance from apex</vocab>

It explains the four-step correction process for the star-planets and the method for adjusting daily motion based on the planet's zodiacal position.</summary>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>True Position of Mars and Others.</header>

# Method for Calculating the Fast Motion of Mars and Others.

> Verse: Subtract the corrected slow motion from the motion of the fast apex; this is called the <term>Kendra-gati; motion of the anomaly</term>. Multiply this by the difference between the current and next segment and divide the product by five. The resulting <term>Viliptas; seconds of arc</term> should be added to or subtracted from the <term>Manda-bhukti; slow daily motion</term>. If the correction exceeds the motion, the result is <term>Vakra-gati; retrograde motion</term>.
> <note>original: "శ్లో|| స్వమందగత్యాసుచలోచ్చభుక్తిం | తాంతద్విధః కేంద్రగతిం వదంతి స్వైషాంగతైష్వాంతర నిఘ్నమధ్యై గ్విభాజయే దా ప ఫలం విలిప్తానికుర్యాద్ధనరణ నిజమందభుక్తౌ | ద్గమ్య పదే తత్ప్రద కాధికో నే స్ఫుటగతి స్పాదధికేశోధ్యా | తదం తరం వక్రగతి ప్రదిష్టాః ||"</note>

**Explanation:** Subtract the <term>Mandasphutagati; corrected slow motion</term> from the <term>Shighrochagati; motion of the fast apex</term> to obtain the <term>Kendragati; motion of the anomaly</term>. Multiply that motion by the difference between the segments of the fast correction and divide by 5. The resulting seconds of arc should be added (if the difference is positive) or subtracted (if negative) from the slow daily motion. This provides the <term>Sphutagati; true daily motion</term>. If the fast motion correction causes the value to be subtracted beyond the planet's current motion, it is recognized as <term>Vakra; retrograde motion</term> <note>Retrograde motion refers to the optical illusion where a planet appears to move backward in the sky as seen from Earth.</note>.

# Chapter on the Nodes <note>original: "Pata Adhyayam". This chapter deals with the lunar nodes and the points where the paths of the Sun and Moon intersect, relevant for predicting eclipses and specific astrological moments.</note>

<margin>Verse</margin> Glory to the Sun, the soul of the universe, the natural ornament of the sky, who is adorned with a garland of a thousand golden rays. <note>original: "Jayati jagati prasū... savitā"</note>

### The Origin of Vyaipra and Vaipra <note>These refer to Vyatipata and Vaidhriti, which are specific astronomical conjunctions where the declinations of the Sun and Moon are equal.</note>

<margin>Verse</margin> When the *Vriddhi* yoga is present, it is called *Vyatipata*; when the *Brahma* yoga is present, it is called *Vaidhriti*. From the *Vriddhi* yoga comes the name *Vyaipra*, and from the *Brahma* yoga comes *Vaipra*.

### Times When the Node Conjunction Cannot Occur

<margin>Verse</margin> When the Sun is situated in Gemini, Cancer, Sagittarius, or Capricorn, the state of equilibrium <note>declination parity</note> does not occur; thus, the *Pata* <note>the astronomical conjunction</note> is not possible.

<margin>Verse</margin> Also, when the Moon's node <note>Rahu</note> is in Libra or Virgo, and the Sun is in Sagittarius, Capricorn, or Taurus, or in Gemini or Cancer, the *Pata* conjunction will certainly not occur.

<margin>Explanation</margin> During the months of Gemini, Cancer, Sagittarius, and Capricorn, there is an absence of the *Pata*. When the node <note>Rahu</note> is in Libra or Virgo, there is also an absence of the *Pata*.

### Calculating the Precession of Equinoxes <note>original: "Ayanamsha Sadhanam". Ayanamsha is the angular distance between the tropical and sidereal zodiacs.</note>

<margin>Verse</margin> From the current Shaka era year, subtract the era of the eyes and the earth <note>421</note>... 
<insert>multiply by 3 and divide by 200 to find the degrees of precession.</insert>

<margin>Explanation</margin> Subtract 421 from the Shalivahana Shaka year, multiply the result by 3, and divide by 200; the result will be the degrees of precession <note>Ayanamsha</note>.

<header>The Delight of Mathematicians <note>original: "Ganaka Ranjani"</note></header>

### Explanation of the Directions

<margin>Verse</margin> Beginning with Aries, the direction is North; beginning with Libra, it is South. From the start of Cancer begins the Southern path; from the start of Capricorn begins the Northern path. <note>original: "Meṣādi uttara diśā..."</note>

**Meaning:** From Aries <note>the first sign of the zodiac</note> onward is the Northern direction. From Libra onward is the Southern direction. From Cancer begins the *Dakshinayana* <note>the sun's six-month southern journey or the period from the summer solstice to the winter solstice</note>. From Capricorn begins the *Uttarayana* <note>the sun's six-month northern journey or the period from the winter solstice to the summer solstice</note>.

---

### The Purpose of the Solstices

<margin>Verse</margin> The purpose mentioned here for the two solstices <note>Ayanas</note> is the movement of declination. For the North and South directions, the purpose is the movement of the celestial lights.

**Meaning:** The purpose of the solstices <note>original: "Ayanamulaku"</note> is to determine the motion of declination <note>original: "Kranti-gati"; the angular distance of a body north or south of the celestial equator</note>. The purpose of the directions is to understand the motion of the celestial light.

---

### Calculation of Declination and Celestial Latitude

<margin>Verse</margin> Note the true positions of the planets, the Sun, the Moon, and Rahu <note>the North Node of the Moon</note>. Add the degrees of the precession of the equinoxes <note>original: "Ayanamsha"</note> to these three. By subtracting Rahu from the Moon, one finds the celestial latitude <note>original: "Vikshepa"</note> through the center. From the base <note>Bhujajya</note> and the complement <note>Kotijya</note>...

**Meaning:** First, record the true positions <note>original: "Sphuta-gati"</note> of the planets—the Sun, the Moon, and Rahu. To these three, add the degrees of the *Ayanamsha* <note>the shifting of the equinoxes over time</note> to calculate the declinations of the Sun and Moon. To find the celestial latitude of the Moon <note>its distance north or south of the Sun's path</note>, subtract the position of Rahu from the Moon's position. By using the "base" <note>original: "Bhuja"; the sine of an arc</note> derived from the Moon’s center (including Rahu) and the Sun and Moon (including Ayanamsha), and by subtracting that base from 3 zodiac signs <note>90 degrees</note>, the "complement" <note>original: "Koti"; the cosine of an arc</note> is obtained. In this manner, one must calculate the Sine and Cosine values.

---

### The Method for Calculating the Sine of the Base

<margin>Verse</margin> Subtract from 180 degrees, multiply the result, and divide by 60... the result is the minutes of the arc. Adding this to the previous result yields the Sine of the base. <note>original: "Bhujajya"</note>

The detailed explanation for this has been written in the "Chapter on True Positions" <note>original: "Sphuta Adhyayah"</note>; please refer to it there.

---

### Calculation of the Sine of the Complement

<margin>Verse</margin> Divide the degrees of the complement <note>original: "Koti"</note> by 13. Subtract 17 from the resulting degrees... the complement of thirteen degrees...

<header>Chapter on the Nodes <note>original: "Pātādhyāyaḥ"</note></header>

# Chapter on the Nodes

Multiplied by sixty (60) and always adding the resulting product; when divided by the divisor of the cosine <note>term: "Koti-jya" (the cosine of an angle in Indian trigonometry)</note>, the result of the planetary motion <note>term: "Gati-phalam" (the correction for the daily motion of a planet)</note> is obtained.

We have already written the first half of this verse in the Chapter on True Positions <note>original: "Sphutadhyaya"</note>. By dividing the result of the cosine by the cosine's divisor, the motion-result is obtained.

->Determination of Declination<-

> Verse: The sine <note>term: "Bhujajya" (the sine of an angle in Indian trigonometry)</note> divided by 76 gives a certain quotient; subtract that from 443. When the sine is divided by that remainder, the declination <note>term: "Kranti" (the angular distance of a body north or south of the celestial equator)</note> is obtained in degrees and minutes.

**Meaning:** When the sine <note>original: "Bhujajya"</note> of the Sun, Moon, or other planets is divided by 76, the resulting quotient should be subtracted from 443. By dividing the sine by the remaining balance, the declination is obtained in degrees and other units.

->Determination of Celestial Latitude<-

> Verse: The sine <note>original: "Bhujajya"</note> divided by 1012 <note>The Sanskrit verse uses the word-numeral "Lochana-rupa-shunya-indu" meaning 2, 1, 0, 1</note> gives a result. Subtract that quotient from 38 <note>word-numeral: "ashta-agni" (8 and 3)</note>. Dividing the sine by that remainder gives the celestial latitude <note>term: "Vikshepa" (the angular distance of a planet from the ecliptic)</note> in minutes.

**Meaning:** When the sine of the mean anomaly minus the node <note>original: "Patona-kendra-bhujajya"</note> is divided by 1012, the resulting quotient is subtracted from 38. By dividing the sine by the remaining balance, the celestial latitude for the Moon and other planets is obtained, starting from minutes <note>original: "liptadi"</note>.

->Determination of the Difference between Declination and Latitude<-

> Verse: One hundred and seventy-one (171) is the divisor for declination; eight hundred and ninety (890) <note>word-numeral: "shunya-ananda-ashta" (0, 9, 8)</note> is the divisor for celestial latitude. These are the mean divisors. Divide the sine by four hundred (400) and subtract it from the first; divide the sine by one hundred (100) and subtract it from the second. When the cosine is divided by these true divisors, the results are the differences in declination and celestial latitude.

**Meaning:** The mean divisor for declination differences is 171, and the mean divisor for celestial latitude differences is 890. These are the central divisors. Divide the sine by 400; the resulting minutes and seconds <note>original: "lipta-vilipta"</note> are to be subtracted from the mean declination divisor of 171. Then, divide the sine by 100 and subtract the result from the mean celestial latitude divisor of 890. When the cosine is divided by this "true arc" <note>the adjusted divisor</note>, the difference in celestial latitude is obtained; and by dividing the sine by its respective adjusted divisor, the difference in declination is obtained.

While the previous page provided the trigonometric constants for calculating the declination and celestial latitude of the Sun and Moon, this page focuses on the directional logic of these coordinates and the arithmetic formulas required to determine the exact moment of "declination equality" (Kranti-samya), an event of significant astrological importance.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>The Joy of the Calculator <note>original: "Ganaka Ranjani"</note></header>

### Explanation of the Directions

> Verse: Having written down the positions of the celestial latitude <note>term: "Vikshepa" (angular distance from the ecliptic)</note> of the Sun and Moon, add the position if the latitudes are in the same direction. If the directions are different, the latitude is subtracted from the Moon’s position. The equality of the Sun and Moon's declination is then observed through this vision.

**Meaning:** Once the celestial latitudes of the Sun and the Moon are known, if they are in the same direction (both North or both South), they should be added together. If they are in different directions, subtract the latitude from the Moon's position. If the Moon's declination <note>term: "Kranti" (angular distance from the celestial equator)</note> is removed within the latitude, the correction for the intersection point <note>note: This refers to a specific adjustment for the lunar nodes</note> is performed according to its specific rules. In this way, the point where the Sun and Moon have equal declination should be observed.

### Now, the Calculation of the Time Intervals

> Verse: Whether for the Sun <note>original: "Arka"</note> or the Moon <note>original: "Himarashmi," literally 'cold-rayed'</note>, find which declination is greater. Divide the remainder by five (5), then [adjust by] a seventh (7th) part. This yields the desired units of time—minutes and seconds—for the time of equal declination.

**Meaning:** Take the declination of the Sun and the declination of the Moon. Subtract the smaller value from whichever is greater. Divide that remainder by five (5). Take the resulting quotient, divide it by seven (7), and subtract that value from the previous result. This calculation gives the past or future units of time <note>term: "Ghatikas" (a traditional Indian unit of time equal to 24 minutes)</note> and their fractions <note>term: "Vinadikas" (24 seconds)</note> required to find the exact moment when the declinations of the two bodies are equal.

### Calculating Planetary Positions at a Desired Time

> Verse: The number of past or future units of time <note>original: "Ghatika"</note> should be multiplied by the motion of the planet. Divide the result by sixty (60). The result should be applied to the Sun and Moon as a correction, or in reverse as the case may be.

**Meaning:** Multiply the past or future units of time by the daily motion of the planets. Divide the resulting product by 60. Taking the past or future status into account, apply this correction to the positions of the Sun and Moon. By applying the logic used for the calculations of Rahu <note>note: Rahu represents the North Node of the Moon; this "logic" refers to its retrograde motion or its role in eclipses</note>, one should determine the equality of declinations.

### Past and Future Intervals

> Verse: When the two declinations are moving from an odd quadrant <note>term: "Oja-pada" (the 1st or 3rd quadrants of the zodiacal circle)</note>... the Sun's declination is past or future... likewise, the equality is seen...

---

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions from the "Chapter on the Nodes" (Pātādhyāyaḥ). <page-num>45</page-num>
<header>Chapter on the Nodes. 45</header>

**Meaning:** In an odd quadrant <note>term: "Oja-pada" (the 1st and 3rd quadrants of the zodiacal circle)</note>, if the Moon's declination <note>term: "Kranti" (angular distance from the celestial equator)</note> is greater than the Sun's declination, the node is "past" <note>original: "Gatam"</note>. If the Moon's declination in an odd quadrant is less than the Sun's declination, the node is "future" <note>original: "Aishyam"</note>. In an even quadrant <note>term: "Yugma-pada" (the 2nd and 4th quadrants)</note>, if the Moon's declination is greater than the Sun's, it is "future"; if it is less, it should be understood as "past."

## Calculation of the Motion of Declination

> Verse: Multiply the difference in declination by the [daily] motion,
> And divide by sixty; let this be done separately for each.
> <note>original Sanskrit: "Krantyantara sankhya gatim hatamcha / Shashtya prishkteshcha prithak-prithas-syat"</note>

**Meaning:** Multiply the difference in declination by the true daily motions of the Sun and the Moon and divide the result by 60. The resulting values will be the specific motions of declination for the Sun and the Moon respectively.

> Verse: Subtract Rahu’s motion from the Moon’s motion, multiply by the difference in latitude,
> And divide by sixty; then, according to the direction of the Moon and its latitude,
> Either add or subtract the Sun’s motion to find the Moon’s [adjusted] motion.
> <note>original Sanskrit: "Rahurgatirnighnashashi gaticha kshepantaré sankhya hatam hatamcha / Vikshepachandraikadishiyutachcha shashi gatirbhanuvihinamaptam"</note>

**Meaning:** Subtract the motion of Rahu <note>note: Rahu represents the ascending lunar node, which moves retrograde</note> from the Moon's daily motion. Multiply this by the difference in celestial latitude <note>term: "Vikshepa"</note> and divide by 60 to find the motion of latitude. If the latitude and the Moon are in the same direction, these two motions should be added together; if they are in different directions, the result of their subtraction is the final value.

## Calculation of the Solar and Lunar Discs

> Verse: Multiply the daily motion of the Sun and Moon by eleven (11) and ten (10) [respectively];
> Divide by twenty (20) and two hundred forty-seven (247),
> To find the measurement of their discs in minutes of arc.
> <note>original Sanskrit: "Ekadasha 11 dasha 10 bhyasta ravindvorbhukti liptikah / Nakhai 20 saptarnavayamai 247 rbhakta manakalastayoh"</note>

**Meaning:** Multiply the Sun's daily motion by 11 and the Moon's daily motion by 10. Divide the Sun's result by 20 and the Moon's result by 247. The resulting values are the diameters of the solar and lunar discs <note>term: "Bimba" (the visible disc of a celestial body)</note> in minutes of arc.

## An Easy Method

> Verse: For a quick calculation of the Sun’s disc, use half the daily motion and divide by six (6).
> For the Moon’s disc, add ten (10) to the daily motion and divide by twenty-five (25).
> <note>original Sanskrit: "Shadbhyo 6 tatkalagatyartham raverbimbasya sadhanam / Chandra bhuktirdasha 10 yutam tatva 25 ptam vidhubimbajah"</note>

## Calculation of the Divisor

> Verse: If the result of the difference in motions is divided by the square of sixty (3600),
> The resulting figure yields the half-duration of the event in units of time.
> By adjusting the time of the weekday with this, the moments of contact and separation are known.
> <note>original Sanskrit: "Gatyantaragunena phalitam yadi shashti vargam 3600 chedam / Kritapta phalitamsthityardhanadyah..."</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating the timing and duration of a node (Pāta) from the previous page. While page 45 focused on determining if a node was "past" or "future" based on declination, this page provides the specific formulas for finding the "Middle Time," "Contact Time," and "Exit Time," concluding the chapter.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>Ganaka Ranjani <note>The title translates to "The Delighter of Calculators" or "The Mathematician's Joy."</note></header>

**Meaning:** Subtract the Sun’s motion in declination from the Moon’s pure motion in declination <note>term: "Kranti-gati" (the rate of change in angular distance from the celestial equator)</note>. Multiply the result by 60 and add the latitude <note>term: "Vikshepa" (celestial latitude)</note>; this is the result. Divide this result by 3600 to obtain the half-duration <note>term: "Sthityardha" (literally 'half-duration,' refers to half the time the node's influence lasts)</note>. If you apply this half-duration to the time when the declinations were equal at midday, you obtain the **Middle Time** <note>term: "Madhya-kala"</note>. Keep this time in two places: subtract the half-duration from the first to get the **Contact Time** <note>term: "Sparsha-kala" (the beginning of the phenomenon)</note>, and add it to the second to get the **Exit Time** <note>term: "Nirgamana-kala" (the end of the phenomenon)</note>.

### Another Method for Calculating Half-Duration

> Verse: Multiply half the sum of the discs by sixty and divide by the divisor;
> The half-duration should then be added to or subtracted from the middle time.
> <note>original Sanskrit: "Bimbayogardhashashti taditamharabhajitam / Sthityardhassyanmadhyakalasahitam viyatanchatatu"</note>

**Meaning:** Add the sizes of the solar and lunar discs together <note>term: "Bimba" (the visible disc or diameter of the celestial body)</note> and take half of that sum. This is the "Half-sum of the Discs." Multiply this value by 60 and divide it by the divisor <note>term: "Hara" (a specific numerical divisor calculated for the day's planetary positions)</note> to find the half-duration.

### Determination of the Timing of the Node

> Verse: At eight hundred (800) ghatikas <note>term: "Ghatika" (a unit of time equal to 24 minutes)</note> before the entry of the vessel,
> And at fourteen (14) for the exit, the rotation of the node occurs.
> <note>original Sanskrit: "Bhabdhimatanga 800 ghatikaparepatrapraveshake / Nirgamemanu 14 sankhyacha patrabhramanevacha"</note>

**Meaning:** Approximately 800 ghatikas from the start of the node’s entry, the node "takes birth." From that entry time, the exit time is established at 14 ghatikas. There is never any variation or special increase/decrease in this sequence; these phenomena arise from the rays of the ecliptic <note>term: "Kranti-mandala" (the circle of the ecliptic)</note>.

> Verse: Just as a very sharp needle tip pricks,
> Creating a full, red drop, such is the precise determination of that time.
> <note>original Sanskrit: "Suchyagrenasutikshnena yavadvidyutitetatha / Raktabindushchapushtashcha tavatkala sunirnayah"</note>

> Verse: This Chapter on the Nodes was composed by me, Gopalakrishna Siddhanti,
> Of the Dwaradwaja lineage, focusing on the lunar center.
> <note>original Sanskrit: "Karanti kendramchandrasya dvaradvajasagotrena / Gopalakrishnasiddhanti patadhyayomayakrita"</note>

This concludes the Chapter on the Nodes <note>original: "Pātādhyāyaḥ"</note>, composed by Rallabandi Gopalakrishna Siddhanti, the dear disciple of Brahmasri Subbaraya Siddhanti—who was a master of the eighteen ancient systems <note>term: "Ashtadasa Siddhanti" (one who knows the 18 classical Indian astronomical treatises)</note>—and student of Brahmasri Bukkapati Venkataramanayya Siddhanti. <meta>catchword: none</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Nodes</header>

I am now providing a simple example for calculating the slow-motion correction factors <note>term: "Mānda-padakas" (tabular values or results used to correct a planet's position based on its elliptical orbit, specifically the "slow" or "mānda" anomaly)</note> for the seven planets, beginning with the Sun.

**Verse:** Subtract the degrees from 180, multiply by the degrees, and divide the result by 60... <note>original Sanskrit: "bhāgādighātyī 180 viśodhyāpalenahatyā bhāgādiśūnya ripu 60 bhājitalabdhi..." This verse provides a shorthand mathematical formula for approximating the sine of an angle (Bhujajyā) used in Indian astronomy.</note>

**Meaning:** I have already explained the method of calculation in the Chapter on True Slow Motion <note>term: "Mānda-sphuṭādhyāya"</note>. I am now providing easy examples for calculating the slow-motion factors for the seven planets, as well as the factors for declination and celestial latitude <note>term: "Krānti-vikṣepa-padakas"</note>.

### Calculation of the Slow-Motion Factors

If the longitude of the "arm" <note>term: "Bhuja" (the angular distance of a planet from the nearest cardinal point, used to find its sine)</note> is 0 signs and 10 degrees, we subtract 10 from 180 to get 170. Multiplying 170 by the 10 degrees gives 1700, which is the Sine of the Arm <note>term: "Bhujajyā"</note>. For Mars <note>Telugu: "Kuju"</note>, the Mean Divisor <note>term: "Madhya-cheda"</note> is 856. Dividing the Sine of the Arm (1700) by 24 and subtracting the result from the Mean Divisor gives a True Divisor <note>term: "Sphuṭa-cheda"</note> of 838. Dividing [the Sine] by this gives a result of 2, 3, 30; this is the correction factor for Mars at 10 degrees. By dividing this Sine of the Arm by the respective True Divisors of each planet, the correction factor at 10 degrees can be found for all. For Mercury <note>Telugu: "Budha"</note>, the Sine of the Arm is 1700 and the Mean Divisor is 2137. Dividing the Sine by 24 gives 71; subtracting this from the Mean Divisor gives a True Divisor of 2066. Dividing [the Sine] by this gives 0 degrees, 49 minutes, and 17 seconds. This is Mercury's correction factor for 10 degrees.

### For Declination

Dividing this same Sine of the Arm (1700) by 76 gives a result of 22, 22, 8. Subtracting this from 443 leaves a remainder of 420, 38. Converting the Sine of the Arm into minutes and dividing gives 4, 2, 50; this is the Declination Factor <note>term: "Krānti-patakam"</note> for 10 degrees. Dividing the Sine of the Arm (1700) by 1012 gives 1, 39; subtracting this from 38 gives 36, 21. Dividing the Sine of the Arm by this gives a result in minutes of 46, 50; this is the Latitude Factor <note>term: "Vikṣepa-patakam"</note> for 10 degrees.

-> Grahakañjanī: Chapter on Nodes, Chapter on Memory. <-

# Chapter on Auxiliary Calculations
## The Beginning.

---

->The Calculation of Intercalary Months and Omitted Months.<-

<term>Verse:</term> Multiply the lunar revolutions <note>original: "Chandra-bhagaṇa"</note> by thirteen (13), subtract the solar revolutions <note>original: "Tigmakara-parjaya" (revolutions of the sun)</note>, and from the remainder, the intercalary months <note>term: "Adhimāsa" (the extra months added to a lunar calendar to synchronize it with the solar year)</note> are born. By finding the difference between the lunar revolutions and the solar revolutions, one determines the omitted days <note>term: "Kṣaya-tithi" (days deleted from the calendar when a lunar day is longer than a solar day)</note> in a Great Age <note>term: "Mahāyuga"</note>.

<term>Meaning:</term> If the lunar revolutions are multiplied by 13 and the solar revolutions are subtracted from that product, the remainder indicates the number of intercalary months <note>term: "Adhimāsa"</note> in a Great Age <note>term: "Mahāyuga"</note>. By adding the solar revolutions to the lunar days <note>term: "Chāndra-tithi"</note> and subtracting the total number of planetary revolutions, the remainder provides the omitted days <note>term: "Kṣaya-tithi"</note> in a Yuga. In other words, to find the intercalary months for a Great Age or from the beginning of the present era <note>term: "Kalpādi"</note>, one must multiply the lunar revolutions by 13 and subtract the solar revolutions. To find the omitted days, one must combine the lunar days with solar revolutions and subtract the total number of orbital rotations of the Sun and Moon.

->The Calculation of Days of Anomaly.<-

<term>Verse:</term> The difference between the revolutions of two planets in a Great Age tells us the number of times those two planets will meet <note>term: "Yoga" (conjunction)</note>. For those who understand the calculations of the orbits, the difference between the revolutions of a planet and its highest or lowest points <note>term: "Nīcoccha-paryaya" (apogee and perigee)</note> determines the revolutions of its "center" or anomaly <note>term: "Kendra"</note>.

<term>Commentary:</term> The difference between the total revolutions of two planets in a single Great Age <note>term: "Yuga"</note> is equal to the number of conjunctions <note>term: "Yoga"</note> that occur between them in that period. By dividing the total terrestrial days <note>term: "Bhū-divasa" (civil days)</note> in a Yuga by that number of conjunctions, one finds the duration of time that passes between each consecutive meeting of those two planets. To find the revolutions of a planet's "fast anomaly" <note>term: "Śīghra-kendra"</note>, subtract the planet's own revolutions from the revolutions of its "fast-moving" point <note>term: "Śīghra-bhagaṇa"</note>. By dividing the total terrestrial days of the Age by these anomaly revolutions, one arrives at the specific "Anomaly Days" <note>term: "Kendra-dina"</note> for that planet.

<vocab>Gaṇita Rañjini (Title of the work), Upakaraṇādhyāya (Chapter on Auxiliary Calculations), Adhimāsa (Intercalary month), Kṣaya-māsa (Omitted month), Chandra-bhagaṇa (Lunar revolutions), Ravi-bhagaṇa (Solar revolutions), Kendra-dinādi (Days of anomaly/mean center), Śloka (Verse), Vyākhyāna (Commentary)</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the mathematical formulas for calculating intercalary months (Adhimāsa) and omitted days (Kṣaya-tithi) within a Great Age (Mahāyuga). It also provides the method for determining the timing of planetary conjunctions and the cycle of a planet's anomaly (Kendra).</summary>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Planetary Nodes</header>

In this context, the calculation of eclipses is discussed.

<term>Verse:</term> Multiply the sum of the revolutions of the Sun and Rahu <note>original: "Āditya-Rāhu bhagaṇa-aikya"; Rahu is the north lunar node, essential for eclipse calculations</note> by two (2); this result becomes the count of syzygies <note>term: "Parva-gaṇa" (the points in time, such as New Moon or Full Moon, where an eclipse can occur)</note> in a cycle of Brahma. By dividing the total number of civil days <note>term: "Bhū-divasa" (terrestrial days or solar days)</note> by that count, the result is the duration of civil days between syzygies <note>term: "Sāvana-dināni"</note>.

<term>Commentary:</term> In a Great Age <note>term: "Yuga"</note>, the sum of the revolutions of the Sun and Rahu multiplied by two equals the count of syzygies <note>original: "Parva-gaṇa"</note> in a cycle of Brahma. By dividing the total civil days of the Yuga by that number, one obtains the number of civil days <note>term: "Sāvana-dināni"</note> that must pass between the possibility of two eclipses. From this, one should understand that the number of eclipses that occur will equal the number of these syzygy periods. How is this so? Some solar eclipses occur at night, and some lunar eclipses occur during the day <note>note: these would be invisible to the observer</note>; therefore, the local calculators must determine their visibility through calculation. Eclipses occurring below the local horizon are visible in other regions. One should know the count of eclipses for their own location accordingly.

<term>Meaning:</term> If you take the revolutions of the Sun and Rahu, add them together, and multiply by two, it becomes the total count of syzygies <note>original: "Brahma-ādi-parvagaṇa"</note>. Dividing this total syzygy count by the total civil days gives the number of civil days in each period. This is how one identifies the number of these sacred eclipse-juncture days <note>term: "Parva-dinamu"</note>.

# Chapter on Planetary Combat
### The Beginning.

---

### Calculating the Time of Conjunction.

<term>Verse:</term> When a planet with slower motion <note>original: "Svalpa-bhukti"</note> is at a higher degree of longitude than a planet with faster motion, the conjunction <note>term: "Yoga" (here referring to planetary combat or alignment)</note> has already passed <note>original: "Bhūta"</note>. If the faster planet is at a lower degree, the conjunction is yet to come <note>original: "Gamya"</note>. One should calculate based on whether the event is past or future. If both planets are in retrograde <note>term: "Vakra" (appearing to move backward)</note>, the one with the higher speed is considered "faster." If the planet with the higher speed has a greater longitude, the conjunction is in the past; if it has a lesser longitude, the conjunction is in the future.

<sig>7</sig>

<vocab>Pātādhyāya (Chapter on Nodes), Āditya-Rāhu bhagaṇa-aikya (Sum of Sun and Rahu revolutions), Brahma-ādi-parvagaṇa (The count of syzygies starting from Brahma), Sāvana-dināni (Civil days), Graha Yuddhādhyāya (Chapter on Planetary Combat), Yoga-kāla-sādhanam (Calculating the time of conjunction)</vocab>

<summary>This page provides a mathematical formula for determining the frequency of eclipses based on solar and lunar node revolutions and introduces the rules for "Planetary Combat." It explains how to calculate whether a planetary conjunction has already occurred or is yet to happen based on the planets' respective speeds and longitudes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for astronomical calculations from page 49.
