<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>Delighter of Astronomers (Ganakaranjani)</header>

> When one planet is retrograde and two are in direct motion, if the degree of the planet is greater [the calculation changes]. If both are in direct motion, the conjunction is future or past based on their degrees.

1. **Commentary:** Among two planets, if the planet with <term>slow motion</term> <note>original: "alpagati"</note> has a lower degree and the planet with the higher speed has a higher degree, it should be understood that this conjunction belongs to the past <note>original: "bhūtakāla"; meaning the planets have already passed each other</note>.

2. Among two planets moving in <term>direct motion</term> <note>original: "savyagati"; the standard forward movement of planets through the zodiac</note>, if the planet with <term>fast motion</term> <note>original: "shighragati"</note> has a higher degree than the slower one, the conjunction <note>original: "yogamu"</note> is past. If it is lower, the conjunction is understood as future <note>original: "gamya"; yet to happen</note>.

3. If those two planets are <term>retrograding</term> <note>original: "vakra"; appearing to move backward in the sky</note>, and the planet with the faster motion has a higher degree than the slower one, then the conjunction is future. If it is lower, know that the conjunction is in the past.

4. When a retrograding planet is involved, if the planet in direct motion has a higher degree, the conjunction is past. If the <term>retrograding planet has the higher degree</term>, the conjunction is future. In this way, the time of conjunction is determined roughly.

->Calculation of the Timing of Conjunction on the Same Longitude<-

> Verse: By the difference in motion for those moving in the same direction, or by the sum of motions for those in different directions, the days between planets are found through proportion.

1. **Commentary:** Convert the difference in degrees between the two planets into <term>arc-minutes</term> <note>original: "lipta"; a unit of angular measurement where 60 minutes equal 1 degree</note>. Multiply these separately by their respective <term>true daily motions</term> <note>original: "sphuṭagati"</note>. Whether the planets are in direct or retrograde motion, divide the result by the difference of their two speeds. The resulting value tells you the days, etc., that have passed or are yet to come until the conjunction.

2. If both planets are retrograding, one must divide by the <term>difference in speeds</term> <note>original: "gatyantara"</note>.

3. If one planet is retrograding and the other is in <term>direct motion</term> <note>original: "rujugati"</note>, one must divide by the sum of the true daily motions of the two planets. The resulting arc-minutes should be <unclear>subtracted</unclear> <note>likely "తీసివేయవలెను" (subtract)</note> from the planets' positions if the conjunction is in the past, or added if the conjunction is in the future.

<header>Chapter on Planetary War <note>original: "Graha Yuddha Adhyayam"; this refers to the astronomical phenomenon of two planets appearing very close together in the sky</note></header>

# Calculation of the Daily Timing Adjustment

> **Verse:** The timing of the result of the days is to be calculated and multiplied by the <term>true motion</term> <note>original: "sphuta-gati"; the actual speed of a planet as seen from Earth</note>. Divide this by sixty (60). The resulting <term>minutes of arc</term> <note>original: "liptas"</note> should be subtracted if the event is past, or added if it is yet to come. For a <term>retrograde planet</term> <note>original: "vakra-graha"; a planet appearing to move backward</note>, the addition and subtraction of the minutes for past and future are performed inversely. If one planet is retrograde, the minutes of the direct motion and retrograde motion are added or subtracted accordingly to achieve the <term>equal longitude</term> <note>original: "samakala"; the point where two planets reach the exact same degree</note>.

1. According to this daily result, multiply the respective <term>true motions</term> <note>original: "sphuta-gatulu"</note> of the planets and divide by 60. If the resulting <term>minutes of arc</term> <note>original: "liptadi"</note> represent a past event, subtract them; if they represent a future event, add them. By doing this, the planets reach <term>equal longitude</term> <note>original: "samakala grahalu"</note>.

2. If both planets are in <term>retrograde motion</term> <note>original: "vakrinchi-unna"</note>, and the result is past, add the minutes; if the result is future, subtract them to find the equal longitude.

3. If one planet is retrograde and the other is in <term>direct motion</term> <note>original: "rujugati"</note>, and the result is past, subtract from the planet in direct motion and add to the planet in retrograde motion. Through this, they reach equal longitude.

# Calculation of the Planetary Disks

> **Verse:** The numbers six (6), eleven (11), twenty (20), twelve (12), and twenty-two (22) should be multiplied by ten (10). Divide these by the respective <term>speed divisors</term> <note>original: "shighra-cheda"</note> to find the <term>diameters of the disks</term> <note>original: "bimbani"</note> starting from Mars.

**Commentary:** As previously described, determine the positions. Using the <term>speed divisor of the true center</term> <note>original: "shighra-kendra sphuta-shighra-cheda"</note>, take the following values and divide them by the respective speed divisors: 60 for Mars, 110 for Mercury, 200 for Jupiter, 120 for Venus, and 220 for Saturn. The results will be the <term>diameters of the planetary disks in minutes of arc</term> <note>original: "liptadi bimbalu"</note>.

# Calculation of Celestial Latitude

**Nodes** <note>original: "Patalu"; the points where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic</note>: Four (4), Two (2), Eight (8), Six (6), Ten (10), Ten (10) — these are the portions of the nodes <note>original: "huta-ashva-pata-amshah"</note>.

**Commentary:** The positions of the <term>Nodes</term> <note>original: "pata"</note> in signs and degrees are:
- Mars: 1 sign, 10°
- Mercury: 0 signs, 20°
- Jupiter: 2 signs, 20°
- Venus: 2 signs, 0°
- Saturn: 3 signs, 10°

# Divisors

> **Verse:** Forty-five (45), one hundred (100), ninety-two (92), three hundred and twenty (320), and sixty-three (63) are the <term>divisors</term> <note>original: "chedah"</note> used to refine the <term>latitude distance</term> <note>original: "kotyartha-samskritah"</note>.

<summary>This page details the mathematical corrections needed to determine the exact moment of a planetary conjunction and calculates the visual diameter of the planets and their celestial latitudes.</summary>
<keywords>planetary conjunction, true motion, retrograde motion, planetary disks, celestial latitude, nodes, astronomical divisors, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn</keywords>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>The Delight of the Calculator <note>original: "Ganakaranjani"; a title likely referring to a mathematical or astronomical treatise</note></header>

**Meaning:** Mars: 45; Mercury: 100; Jupiter: 62; Saturn: 320. These are the **mean differences** <note>original: "madhya bhedalu"</note>. If these are mathematically adjusted <note>original: "samskritam cheste"</note> for the purpose of subtraction/alignment, they become the **true differences** <note>original: "sphuta bhedalu"</note>.

->**Manda Corrections**<- <note>Manda refers to the "slow" motion or the equation of the center, correcting for the eccentricity of a planet's orbit</note>

**Verse:** 159, 220, 170, 80, 240. These values are to be used for the Manda calculations for the zodiac signs.

->**Calculation of Celestial Latitude**<- <note>original: "Vikshepa-sadhanam"; refers to calculating the distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</note>

**Verse:** Subtract the <term>node</term> <note>original: "pata"; the point where the planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic</note> from the planet's <term>true anomaly position</term> <note>original: "manda-sphuta"</note>. For the "fast" planets Mercury and Venus, use the <term>Shighra</term> <note>the "fast" anomaly or conjunction position</note>. Multiply the sine of the resulting arc <note>original: "bhuja-jya"</note> by the factors 12, 16, 9, 8, and 6 respectively. This provides the <term>mean latitude minutes</term> <note>original: "madhya-vikshepa-lipta"</note>. Multiply these by the planet's own radius and divide by the <term>Shighra-bheda</term> to find the <term>true latitude minutes</term> <note>original: "sphuta-vikshepa-liptika"</note>. The direction (North or South) of the latitude is determined by the quadrant of the center.

**Meaning:** For the desired days, as previously described, calculate the <term>true anomaly positions</term> <note>original: "manda-sphuta"</note> of Mars and the other planets at the time of conjunction. Multiply the motion by the desired time and divide by 60. By adjusting the daily motion, find the specific positions for Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, and Saturn at that moment. Subtract their respective <term>nodes</term> <note>original: "pata"</note> to find the <term>argument of latitude</term> <note>original: "kendra"</note>. Find the sine of this position <note>original: "jya"</note> and multiply it by 12 for Mars, 8 for Jupiter, and 6 for Saturn. The resulting values are the **mean latitude minutes** <note>original: "madhya vikshepa liptalu"</note> for each. 

For the "fast" positions of Mercury and Venus at the desired time, the results of the true anomaly must be adjusted inversely. Subtract their respective nodes, identify the direction based on the argument of latitude, find the sine of the arc, and multiply by 16 for Mercury and 9 for Venus. These resulting values are the **mean latitudes** for Mercury and Venus.

---

**Verse:** The mean latitude minutes of Mars and the others should be multiplied by their respective orbital radii <note>original: "vyasa"</note> and divided by the <term>Shighra-bheda</term> <note>the divisor derived from the planet's "fast" motion adjustment</note>. The result is the **true latitude** <note>original: "sphuta-vikshepa"</note>, designated as either South or North.

**Meaning:** Take the mean latitudes previously calculated for Mars and the others, multiply them by their respective radii, and divide by their specific **Shighra-bheda** divisors. The resulting value is the true celestial latitude in minutes and seconds.

<summary>This page details the mathematical formulas for calculating the celestial latitude (Vikshepa) of planets, involving specific numerical multipliers for each planet and corrections for their orbital nodes and anomalies.</summary>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Planetary Combat</header>

# Chapter on Planetary Combat <note>original: "Graha Yuddha Adhyāyam"; in Indian astronomy, when two planets appear very close in the sky, it is referred to as a "combat" or "war."</note>

One must understand that if the <term>arm of the anomaly</term> <note>original: "bhuja"; a mathematical value derived from the planet's position</note> is in the latter half of the center, it results in a **Southern Latitude**, and if it is in the former half, it results in a **Northern Latitude**.

> **Verse:** If the latitudes are in the same direction, take the difference between them; if they are in different directions, take their sum. If the distance between the disks is less than the sum of their radii, a "clash" or "piercing" occurs. <note>original Sanskrit: "Vikshepayossamadishorantaram..."</note>

**Meaning:** For <term>Mars and the other planets</term> <note>original: "Kujadulu"</note>, to find the "distance between the disks" <note>original: "Bimbantaramu"</note> in minutes of arc: if both latitudes are in the same direction (both North or both South), subtract the smaller from the larger. If they are in opposite directions, add them together. If the latitude does not exceed the distance between the disks, there is no "combat." However, if the latitude is within that distance, a **Planetary Combat** occurs.

> **Verse:** Regarding Mars and the others, whenever the time of conjunction varies due to their differing speeds, the duration of total obscuration will exceed four *ghatikas*. <note>A *ghatika* is a traditional unit of time equal to 24 minutes; four *ghatikas* equals 1 hour and 36 minutes.</note>

**Meaning:** For Mars and the other planets, whenever there are differences in the timing of their conjunction, the <term>duration of total obscuration</term> <note>original: "vimarda kala"</note> will be greater than four *ghatikas*.

> **Verse:** Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, Mercury, and the Moon—these are situated one above the other in their respective spheres. <note>original Sanskrit: "Mandamarejyabhūputra..." This describes the ancient geocentric model of the cosmos.</note>

**Meaning:** In the mutual conjunctions of <term>Mars and the others</term>, **Mercury** is the lowest. Above Mercury is **Venus**; above him is **Mars**; above him is **Jupiter**; and above him is **Saturn**. The planetary orbits <note>original: "mandalamulu"</note> are arranged in this order. For this reason, you should understand that "combat" or "seizure" <note>original: "grahanamu"; usually meaning eclipse, but here referring to occultation</note> is caused by the planet situated lower in this hierarchy.

## Calculating the Half-Duration

> **Verse:** Subtract the square of the latitude from the square of the sum of the radii, and take the square root. Multiply this by 60 and divide by the difference in the planets' daily motions to find the half-duration.

**Meaning:** Just as in the calculation of eclipses, subtract the square of the <term>latitude</term> <note>original: "vikshepa"</note> from the square of the <term>distance between the disks</term> <note>original: "bimbantara"</note>. Take the square root of the result. Divide that root by the difference in the daily motions <note>original: "bhuktyantaralu"</note> of the two planets to find the **half-duration** <note>original: "sthityardha"; the time from the start of the conjunction to the middle, or from the middle to the end</note> in minutes. If one of the planets is in <term>retrograde</term> <note>original: "vakra"; appearing to move backward in the sky</note>, multiply the root by 60 and divide by the *sum* of their motions instead. If you apply these corrections exactly as you would for solar or lunar eclipses, you will obtain the total accurate duration.

<summary>This page explains how to determine if two planets are in "combat" (conjunction) based on their celestial latitudes and provides the mathematical formula to calculate the duration of such events.</summary>
<keywords>Planetary Combat, Graha Yuddha, Celestial Latitude, Vikshepa, Bimbantara, Half-duration, Sthityardha, planetary hierarchy, astronomical calculation</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical classification of planetary conjunctions, specifically detailing the "Planetary Combat" (Graha Yuddha) mentioned on page 53. Although the page number is marked as 45, the content directly follows the definitions of latitude and disk distance from the previous discussion.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>The Mathematician’s Delight <note>original: "Gaṇaka Ranjani"</note></header>

> **Verse:** Using the tips of an eight-finger staff, the measurement between the eyes and the center of the disk is determined in fingers. The eastern point begins at the edge of the circle’s rotation, followed by the western point. <note>original Sanskrit: "Vadaṣṭāṅguḷayaṣṭragrai..."</note>

**Meaning:** The space representing the distance between the planetary disks <note>original: "Bimbāntara sthalamu"</note> is divided into 60 parts, each becoming a unit known as a "finger" <note>original: "Aṅguḷa"</note>.

1. If the distance between the disks is exactly equal to the sum of the two radii <term>Manekyartha</term> <note>the combined half-diameters of the two planets</note>, it is called **Scraping** <note>original: "Ullekhana"</note>.

2. If the distance between the disks is slightly less or slightly more than the sum of the radii, it is called **Goad-combat** <note>original: "Aṅkuśā-yuddhamu"</note>.

3. If the distance between the disks exceeds 60 minutes <note>original: "liptas"</note>, it is called **Ill-omened Combat** <note>original: "Apasavya yuddhamu"</note>. In these encounters, those planets situated to the North are considered the victors, while those to the South are the defeated. However, Venus <note>original: "Śukruḍu"</note> is unique—it is considered the victor even when situated in the South.

---

->I shall now provide the easy methods for identifying the characteristics<-
->of planetary combat as described in the Siddhanta scriptures.<-

> **Verse:** Piercing, Scraping, Goading, and Crushing, as well as the Ill-omened; these are the types of unions that occur when planets reach the same degree. This combat is described for the sons of the Earth <note>Mars</note> and the others when they are mutually joined. <note>original Sanskrit: "Bhedollekāṅkumardā khyā..."</note>

**Meaning:** This type of conjunction does not apply to the Sun or the Moon; it occurs only among the five planets: Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn. "Combat" occurs when these planets meet. The phrase "mutually joined" means the combat takes place at the moment when the zodiac signs, degrees, and minutes of both planets are equal. In physical terms, "combat" is defined as the disk of a planet positioned higher in the sky being obscured or covered by a disk positioned lower.

### The Five Types of Combat in Siddhanta

> **Verse:** Scraping is defined as the touching of the stars; Piercing is the actual overlapping. Combat is further known as Goading and Crushing when the rays mingle. If the distance is less than a degree, it is called Ill-omened; if the planets are minute and the distance is greater than a degree, it is a simple meeting. I have thus described the fivefold characteristics: Crushing, Meeting, Piercing, Scraping, and Ill-omened. <note>original Sanskrit: "Ullekhantārakāsparśa..."</note>

**Meaning:**
1. **Ray-Crushing Combat** <note>original: "Aṃśuvimarda-yuddhaṃ"</note>
2. **Peak Combat** <note>original: "Kūṭa-yuddhaṃ"</note>
3. **Piercing Combat** <note>original: "Bheda-yuddhaṃ"</note>

<summary>This page defines the specific measurements and categories of "Planetary Combat" (conjunctions), explaining how the distance between planetary disks determines the type of celestial event and which planet is declared the "victor."</summary>
<keywords>Planetary Combat, Graha Yuddha, Ullekhana, Apasavya, Venus victory, Siddhanta, planetary radii, Bimbantara</keywords>

<page-num>55</page-num>

## Chapter on Planetary Combat

1. **Scraping Combat** <note>original: "Ullekhana-yuddhamu"</note>. 5. **Reverse Combat** <note>original: "Apasavya-yuddhamu"</note>. There are five distinct names for these types of planetary encounters.

> **Verse:** When the distance between the two disks in the sky is slightly greater than half the sum of their diameters, the experts call this **Combat of Brilliance** <term>Amshu-yuddha</term> or "Ray-Clashing." <note>original Sanskrit: "Mānayogārdhamityeta..."</note>

**Meaning:** If the distance between the planetary disks <note>original: "Bimbāntaramu"</note> is slightly more than the combined radii <term>Manayogardha</term> <note>half the sum of the diameters of the two planets</note>, it should be understood as the **Combat of Brilliance** <term>Amshu-vimarda-yuddha</term>.

2. **Puzzling Combat** <term>Kuta-yuddha</term>. 
> **Verse:** When two planetary disks come together, and are characterized by being slightly smaller or larger than expected, one should recognize this as **Puzzling Combat**, through which victory and defeat are declared.

**Meaning:** When two planetary disks are joined and there is a slight variation or obscurity in their appearance, it is known as **Puzzling Combat**.

3. **Regarding Piercing** <term>Bheda-vishayam</term>.
> **Verse:** When one disk obscures or overlaps the other, it is described by the name **Piercing** <term>Bheda</term>.

**Meaning:** If one planetary disk covers another, it is called **Piercing Combat**.

4. **Regarding Scraping** <term>Ullekhana-vishayam</term>.
> **Verse:** When a mere touch between the planets occurs, the experts call it **Scraping** <term>Ullekhana</term>.

**Meaning:** The moment the planets seem to touch, it is designated as **Scraping**.

4. **Defining "Touch"** <note>The text repeats the number 4 for this clarification</note>.
> **Verse:** If the distance between the disks is exactly equal to half the sum of their diameters, it is known as **Scraping**. This state determines victory and defeat.

**Meaning:** A **Touch** <term>Sparsha</term> occurs when the distance between the centers of the two disks is exactly equal to the sum of their radii. To calculate the outcome of Scraping, one must be knowledgeable in calculating parallax <term>Lambana</term>, deflection <term>Avanati</term>, and the half-duration of the contact <term>Sthityardha</term>.

**Reverse Conjunction** <term>Apasavya-grahanam</term>.
> **Verse:** If one disk is small and the other is large, and the distance between the two disks is less than one degree <note>original: "bhāga"</note>, it is known as **Reverse Combat** <term>Apasavya</term>, used to determine victory and defeat.

**Meaning:** If one disk appears small while the other appears large, and the distance between these two fighting planets is less than one degree, it must be understood as **Reverse Combat**.

---

### Characteristics of the Fourfold Military Forces

**From the Sun Treatise** <term>Surya Siddhanta</term>
> **Verse:** Regarding the horses and other forces, the eight tasks... the two-footed... the arrows and the bowstrings... the famous movement of the chariot... <note>This verse from the Surya Siddhanta uses military metaphors—horses, chariots, and infantry—to describe the relative strengths and motions of the planets.</note>

**Vocabulary:** 
Planetary Combat <note>Graha Yuddhamu</note>, Scraping Combat <note>Ullekhana-yuddhamu</note>, Reverse Combat <note>Apasavya-yuddhamu</note>, Ray Combat <note>Amshu-yuddhamu</note>, Puzzling Combat <note>Kuta-yuddhamu</note>, Piercing Combat <note>Bheda-yuddhamu</note>, Characteristics of the Fourfold Forces <note>Chaturanga Bala Lakshanam</note>, The Sun Treatise <note>Surya Siddhanta</note>, Victory and Defeat <note>Jayajayamu</note>, Disk/Orb <note>Bimbamu</note>.

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header># Astronomer’s Delight</header>

The Lord of the planetary army, adorned with flags as brilliant as the sun and followed by retreating soldiers, possesses a pure, bright disk. When the time for battle arrives in the sky, the conjunction occurs within the houses of the ascendant planets. <note>original: "khēṭasthai nyādināthaṃ bhāsvatkhātadhvajāḍhyandugatanatabhaṭaṃ..."</note>

->The Simplified Method<-

<margin>Verse</margin> The visibility corrections for latitude and declination <term>Akshayanansha-drikkarma</term> are the swarms of elephants and horses; the celestial latitudes <term>Vikshepa</term> <note>the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</note> are the arrows. The radius <term>Trijya</term> is the twang of the bow. The distances from the earth <term>Manda-shighra-karna</term> <note>referring to the hypotenuse in planetary longitude calculations</note> are the bows themselves. The planet's own daily motion <term>Svabhukti</term> is the movement of the chariots. The corrective factors <term>Bija-samskara</term> <note>literally "seed-refinements," used to align calculations with observations</note> are the generals of the army. The nodes <term>Pata</term> are the flags of the battle. The observed portents and signs are the brave soldiers. The lunar sphere is the battlefield. The diameters of the planets in leagues <note>yojanas</note> and their solid, spherical shapes represent the mass and density of the opposing forces. This battle shines with the power and valor associated with the "Terrible" <term>Bhibhatsa</term> aesthetic.

**Meaning:** In such a battle between the planets, you should understand the following: The visibility corrections <note>original: "akshayanadrikkarmambulu"</note> are the elephants and horses; the latitudes are the arrows; the radius is the sound of the bowstring; the slow and fast distance-vectors are the bows; the planets' own motions are the movement of the chariots; the "seed" corrections are the army generals; the planetary nodes are the flags; the visual portents are the heroic soldiers; and the massive, spherical planetary disks—stretching many leagues in diameter—carry the terrifying power and majesty of a great conflict.

->The Method for Determining Victory and Defeat<-

<margin>Verse</margin> In this battle, by dividing the difference in the "arrows" <note>latitudes</note> by the difference in the planets' daily motions, the interval is found. One must then apply this to the northern or southern positions to determine the outcome.

<margin>Verse</margin> When the planets meet in the celestial sphere <term>Gola</term>, one must look at the interval between the north and the south. The planet situated to the north, or the one with the larger disk, is the certain victor. The planet to the south, or the one with a smaller disk, is said to be the defeated one. If both are equal, the one in the north that is brighter and larger is the winner; even if a planet is in the south, if it is significantly more powerful, it may prevail. However, Venus <term>Bhargava</term> is generally the victor regardless of position.

<margin>Verse</margin> Usually, the northern planet is the winner. However, even in the southern direction, Venus is the victor.

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header># Chapter on Planetary War</header>

**Meaning:** In such an occurring battle, among the two planetary discs positioned north and south on the celestial sphere, the one in the north with the larger disc <term>sthula-bimba</term> <note>the apparent diameter of the planet as seen from Earth</note> is victorious. The one in the south with the smaller disc <term>alpa-bimba</term> suffers defeat. Venus <term>Shukra</term>, usually having a large disc, is often victorious in both directions. However, in an "unnatural" <term>apasavya</term> <note>conjunctions involving retrograde motion or specific irregular movements</note> battle, the planet with the smaller disc is the loser, regardless of which direction it occupies. In conjunctions involving solar obscuration <term>astangata</term>, simple meetings <term>samagama</term>, or "massed" <term>kuta</term> battles, if the positional strengths <term>sthanadi-balas</term> are gentle <term>saumya</term>, victory and defeat do not occur regardless of the planets' positions. I shall now explain the method for determining the strength of planets that have experienced such victory or defeat in battle.

**Meaning:** When such a battle occurs, one must take the difference between the larger and smaller disc shapes as seen at the point of observation. Convert the remainder into minutes <term>virupa</term> and divide them by the difference in the planets' celestial latitudes <term>vikshepantara-lipta</term>. The resulting quotient is added to the planet in the northern direction and subtracted from the one in the south. Regarding the latitude difference: if both latitudes are in the same direction, they are added; if they are in different directions, they are subtracted. This process establishes the true difference in latitude. It should be understood that a planet whose "kinetic hypotenuse" <term>chala-karna</term> <note>the distance from the Earth to the planet</note> is greater than the radius <term>trijya</term> will have a small disc. Conversely, one with a smaller distance will appear to have a large disc.

->The Simplified Method for Determining Victory and Defeat<-

> **Verse:** When two planets are in the same sign and degree in their transit, and the anomaly <term>shighra-kendra</term> of both planets is positioned within ten 10 degrees... <note>original Sanskrit: "Rāśyaṃśasamānagōcarē ubhayagrahāśīghra kēndragē..."</note>

**Meaning:** Considering the technical calculation for victory and defeat in such a battle to be too complex for some, I shall describe a simplified "rule of thumb" method followed by the school of the great teacher **Varahamihira** <note>a 6th-century Indian astronomer and polymath</note>, which is useful for everyone. It is as follows: among two planets occupying the same zodiacal sign and degree, the one with "broad rays" <term>vistara-kirana</term> <note>appearing larger, brighter, or more radiant</note> is the winner. Specifically, a planet whose anomaly <term>shighra-kendra</term> falls between 4 and 10 degrees is considered "broad-rayed." Know that such a planet is situated in the north. The winning planet provides favorable results. There is little need to describe the fate of the loser; even if it is a naturally "benefic" <term>saumya</term> planet, its positive effects are lost, appearing like one who is bent or crushed. [Examples of the calculations for Saturn <term>Shani</term> and Venus <term>Shukra</term> can be found on page 30; the calculations for Mars <term>Kuja</term> and others are written in the Chapter on Longitudes].

Thus ends the Chapter on Planetary War, composed by the venerable Brahmasri Makkapati Venkataramanayya Siddhanti, Subbaraya Siddhanti, and Rallabhandi Gopalakrishnayya Siddhanti.

**Meaning:** By subtracting the planetary revolutions from the total civil days, one obtains the "Savana" days (civil days) specific to that planet. <page-num>64</page-num>
<header>The Treatise of Aryabhata.</header>

# Calculation of the Day-Count <note>original: "Dyugana Sadhanamu"; refers to the total count of days elapsed from a specific epoch to the present date.</note>

> <term>Verse</term> || By adding 3,179 to the Shaka year, the elapsed years of the <term>Kali Yuga</term> <note>The current cosmic age, which traditional Indian astronomy calculates as beginning in 3102 BCE</note> are obtained. Multiply these by the Sun <note>the number 12, for the twelve months of the year</note> and add the elapsed months. Separately, multiply this by the count of intercalary months and divide by the solar months to find the elapsed intercalary months. Add these to the previous sum. Multiply the total by thirty and add the elapsed lunar days <note>tithis</note>. Separately multiply this by the count of omitted days and divide by the total lunar days to find the omitted days <note>Kshaya tithis</note>. Subtracting these from the total lunar days yields the <term>Ahargana</term> <note>total civil days</note> since sunrise at Lanka. The sequence of days begins from Friday <note>Bhargava-vara</note>. ||

**Meaning:** When 3,179 is added to the Shalivahana Shaka year, the result is the number of years elapsed in the Kali Yuga. To find the Day-Count for a desired time: 
1. Multiply the elapsed years of the Kali Yuga by 12 <note>the number of months in a year</note>.
2. Add the number of months that have passed from the start of the year <note>the month of Chaitra</note> to the current date, including any intercalary months that have occurred.
3. Keep this number in two places. Multiply the lower number by the total number of intercalary months in a Great Age and divide by the total solar months in a Great Age. 
4. The result is the number of <term>Adhikamasas</term> <note>intercalary or "leap" months</note>. Add these to the first number to get the total "refined" months. 
5. Multiply this total by 30 <note>the number of days in a lunar month</note> and add the number of lunar days <note>tithis</note> passed in the current month from the first day of the bright fortnight <note>Shukla Pratipat</note> to the desired date. 
6. Again, keep this total in two places. Multiply the lower number by the total number of omitted days in a Great Age and divide by the total lunar days in a Great Age. 
7. The result is the number of <term>Kshaya Tithis</term> <note>omitted lunar days</note>. Subtract these from the upper number. 
The remaining total is the refined group of days known as the **Ahargana**. This represents the total count of solar-based civil days. If you divide this number by 7, the remainder indicates the day of the week, counting from **Friday** as the starting point. 

In certain instances, it may be necessary to add or subtract one day for precise alignment. Because the text states "**Bhargava-vara purvatah**" <note>Starting from Friday</note>, the planetary positions derived from this Day-Count will correspond to the previous week's positions; one must check the weekday and, if necessary, subtract one from the Day-Count to ensure the planetary positions align correctly.

# Demonstration of the Day-Count Calculation.

**Meaning:** For an example date: The year *Sadharna*, the month of *Ashwayuja*, the 1st day of the bright fortnight <note>Shukla 1</note>, a Saturday. 
Shaka Year: 1832. 
Elapsed years of the Kali Yuga: 5,011. 
According to the Aryabhata Siddhanta system, we take the Kali year 5,011 and multiply it by the month-count...

It provides a step-by-step method involving intercalary months and omitted lunar days, starting the count from a Friday.</summary>

<page-num>62</page-num>

-> **The Delight of Calculators** <- <note>original: "Ganaka Ranjani"; the title of the treatise.</note>

...multiplying by **12** gives **60,532** elapsed months. Adding the **6** [current elapsed months] results in **60,538**. Place this value in two positions. Multiply the lower position by the intercalary months constant, **1,593,336**, resulting in **96,422,004,038,888**. Divide this by the solar months in a Yuga—that is, **4,320,000** multiplied by **12**, which is **51,840,000**—to find the obtained quotient of **1,844** intercalary months <note>original: "Adhimasalu"; extra lunar months added to align the lunar calendar with the solar year.</note>. Add these to the upper position to get **62,382**. Multiply this by **30** and add the elapsed lunar days to get **1,896,484**. Place this in two positions. Multiply the lower position by the omitted days constant, **25,082,252**, resulting in **47,640,754,503,198**. Divide this by the total lunar days, **1,603,000,050**, to find the **29,097** omitted days <note>original: "Kshaya tithis"; lunar days that are skipped in the civil calendar to keep pace with the sun.</note>. Subtracting these from the upper position yields the **Day-Count** of **1,867,487** days starting from Friday, as agreed upon in the **Aryabhata** system <note>original: "Aryabhata Sammata Siddhanta"; referring to the school of the 5th-century astronomer Aryabhata.</note>.

### Now, the Calculation of the Mean Planets is Stated.

> <term>Verse</term> <note>original: "Shloka"</note> || The Day-Count, multiplied by the specific planetary revolutions [of a Yuga] and divided by the Earth-days [civil days], yields the revolutions, signs, and other units of the planet. For the Moon’s apogee, add three signs; for the Node [Rahu], subtract the result from the total revolutions and add six signs. ||

**Meaning:** Multiply the previously mentioned Day-Count of the Kalpa <note>a vast cosmic cycle</note> by the number of revolutions assigned to the Sun and other planets in a **Mahayuga** <note>a Great Age of 4,320,000 years</note>. Divide the result by the total civil days <note>original: "Kuvasaramulu"; literally 'earth-days'.</note> stated for a Mahayuga. The resulting quotient represents the completed revolutions. Multiply the remainder by **12**, then **30**, **60**, and **60**, and divide each time by the civil days to find the Signs, Degrees, Minutes, and Seconds <note>original: "Rashi, Bhaga, Lipta, Vilipta"</note> respectively. 

For the **Moon's Apogee** <note>original: "Chandrochha"</note>, discard the revolutions and add **3 signs** to the result. For the **Node** <note>original: "Rahu" or "Pata"</note>, add **6 signs** and subtract the result from the total circular measure to find the position.

**Example Calculation for the Sun:** 
Take the Day-Count of **1,867,487** and multiply it by **4,320,000** [Sun's revolutions]. This equals **8,067,543,840,000** <note>Note: The manuscript's internal math shows 7,907,315,139,000; medieval transcriptions often contain slight variations in digit recording.</note>. Dividing this by the civil days constant of **1,577,917,820,000** gives a quotient of **4,111** revolutions. Multiplying the remainder by **12** and dividing by the civil days gives the position in signs and other units as: **5** signs, **24** degrees, **15** minutes, and **25** seconds. To this, add a fourth-part correction of **18, 4** for the sunrise time, resulting in **5** signs, **24** degrees, **16** minutes, and **43** seconds. After subtracting the longitudinal correction <note>original: "Deshantara Samskaramu"; an adjustment for the observer's distance from the prime meridian, likely Ujjain.</note> of **0** degrees and **9** minutes, and since this planet is the Sun for a Sunday, calculating the daily motion yields the **Sun's position** as: **5, 23, 27, 36** <note>5 signs (Virgo), 23 degrees, 27 minutes, 36 seconds.</note>.

**Example Calculation for Jupiter:**
Multiply the Day-Count **1,867,487** by **364,224** [Jupiter's revolutions] to get **680,183,061,715**. Dividing by the civil days yields Jupiter's mean position as **425** revolutions, **6** signs, **8** degrees, **38** minutes, and **34** seconds. After applying the sunrise correction, it becomes **6, 8, 4, 45**. Applying the longitudinal correction results in **Jupiter's position**: **6, 8, 34, 47** <note>6 signs (Libra), 8 degrees, 34 minutes, 47 seconds.</note>.

### Correction for the Years Since the Beginning of the Kali Era

<note>The following verse is in Sanskrit, transcribed into Telugu script.</note>

> <term>Verse 3</term> || Multiply [the elapsed years of the Kali Era] by **10**. For the Sun and others: divide by **96,523** for the Sun; by **690** for the Moon; by **2,544** for Mars; by **51** for the fast motion of Mercury; by **498** for Jupiter; by **163** for the fast motion of Venus; by **5,500** for Saturn; by **1,074** for Rahu. The result obtained is in minutes of arc <note>original: "liptadyam"</note>, as agreed by the Sun <note>referring to the Surya Siddhanta or solar-based calculation</note>. Add this for Mercury's fast motion, the Sun, and Saturn; subtract for others. If the value exceeds a full revolution, subtract the revolutions.

**Meaning:** Multiply the elapsed years of the Kali Era <note>The current age in Hindu chronology, which began in 3102 BCE.</note> by **10**.
For the Sun: **96,523**
For the Moon: **690**
For Mars: **2,544**
For Mercury’s fast motion <note>original: "Budha-shighram"; refers to the planet's anomaly or its position relative to the sun.</note>: **51**
For Jupiter: **498**
For Venus’s fast motion: **163**
For Saturn: **5,500**
For Rahu <note>The North Node of the Moon, responsible for eclipses.</note>: **1,074**...

> ...multiplied by two, and divided by 11 <note>original: "shashisurya" (Moon-Sun), which corresponds to the number 11 in the Katapayadi system</note>. The result obtained in degrees and fractions is the correction, to be applied like the <term>sine correction</term> <note>original: "bhujaphala"</note>, either as a subtraction or addition to make it equal to the observed position.

In the <term>Kalpajata</term> <note>The number of years elapsed in the current cosmic cycle (Kalpa).</note>, if one subtracts 3,600, and for the remaining 4,800 years—or for the time elapsed beyond that—one divides by 4,800. According to the rule, the remainder up to 2,400 is known as the <term>odd step</term> <note>original: "ojapada"</term>, and anything beyond that is the <term>even step</term> <note>original: "yugmapada"</note>. After determining this, multiply the remainder by two and divide by 11. The resulting value, in degrees etc., should be applied like the sine correction—specifically, it should be added <note>original: "dhanam"</note> during an odd step and subtracted <note>original: "runam"</note> during an even step. This <term>observational correction</term> <note>original: "drik-samskara"</note> arises after the initial calculations for the start of the Kalpa are performed.

One must perform this observational correction to determine the <term>true position</term> <note>original: "sphuta"</note> of the planet. All authorities on astronomical treatises agree on this correction. The rule "The experts of the Kalpa through the 3,600 years" <note>original: "kalpadyajnah khakharasugunai" — a mnemonic for the number 3,600</note> applies here. However, the multipliers and divisors may vary between systems.

### Regarding the Correction for Longitude

> *Verse:* In sequence, Lanka, Ujjain, and the Himalayas lie [on the meridian]. For those living to the east or west of these, the <term>longitude correction</term> <note>original: "deshantara karma"</note> must be performed. Thus, one obtains the timing relative to the actual sunrise at Lanka.

Because these cities—Lanka, Ujjain, Kurukshetra, and the Himalayas—lie on the same <term>prime meridian</term> <note>original: "sama sutra," literally "same string/line"</note>, those living to the east or west of this line must apply the longitude correction. Sunrise is perceived directly and earliest in the regions to the east of the city of Lanka.

> *Verse:* The circumference of the Earth is 3,300 <term>yojanas</term>. Its diameter <note>original: "vismriti"</note> is 1,050 yojanas. By multiplying [the diameter] by the Sun's [constant] and dividing by the result's hypotenuse, the true circumference of the great sphere is found.

In this science, the Earth's circumference <note>original: "paridhi"</note> is 3,300 <term>yojanas</term> <note>An ancient Indian unit of distance, roughly 5 to 9 miles.</note>. The diameter <note>original: "vyasa"</note> of the Earth, as indicated through the center of the globe or its circumference, is 1,050 yojanas. This...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for astronomical mapping. Having discussed the observational corrections for time on the previous page, the text now shifts to calculating the Earth's local circumference and the longitudinal correction (Deshāntara) required to adjust planetary positions from a prime meridian to the observer's specific location.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>The Calculator's Delight</header>

Multiply the number of <term>yojanas</term> <note>A traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly equivalent to 8 or 9 miles.</note> by 12 and divide by the <term>equinoctial hypotenuse</term> <note>original: "vishuwatkarna"; this refers to the hypotenuse of a shadow cast by a 12-digit gnomon at noon during the equinox at a specific location.</note> of one’s own country. The resulting quotient will be the <term>true circumference</term> <note>original: "sphuta-paridhi"; the circumference of the Earth at the observer’s specific latitude.</note>.

**Verse:** *Regarding the location of the Earth's equator and one’s own city | The diagonal distance is known through yojanas | One should multiply the degrees of latitude of each | By 3,300 and divide by 360.*
<gloss>khi 12</gloss> <note>This gloss refers to the multiplier 12 used in the calculation.</note>

**Meaning:** Multiply that true circumference by 3,300 <note>The total circumference of the Earth in yojanas according to this specific system.</note> and divide by 360. The quotient represents the yojanas between the respective cities. Understand these as yojanas measured along the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>original: "karna-gata"; the direct, diagonal distance between two points on the globe.</note>. One must take the difference between the <term>latitude</term> <note>original: "akshāṃsha"</note> of the cities situated on the prime meridian <note>The text refers to the "Earth-line" or "Bhurekha," historically passing through Ujjain.</note> and the latitude of one’s own location. Multiply this difference by the Earth's circumference in yojanas and divide by 360. The quotient reveals the distance in yojanas from the prime meridian to the observer's city, specifically in terms of the east-west direction.

**Verse:** *Subtract the square of the result from the square of the yojanas | Quickly find the square root; that is the difference | Multiply this by the motion of the planet | Divide by the Earth's own circumference | Apply as addition or subtraction for those to the east or west.*

**Meaning:** Subtract the square of those yojanas <note>the latitudinal distance</note> from the previously determined square of the hypotenuse <note>the diagonal distance</note>. Taking the square root of the remainder gives the yojanas specific to the observer's location <note>This uses the Pythagorean theorem to find the base of the triangle, which represents the pure longitudinal distance</note>. Multiply the mean motions of the planets by these yojanas and divide by the true circumference of the Earth. The result will be the correction in minutes and seconds <note>original: "kalādi-phalamu"</note>. This correction must be applied by those residing to the east or west of the prime meridian: those to the east must subtract the value, and those to the west must add it. By performing this <term>longitudinal correction</term> <note>original: "deshāntara saṃskāra"</note> in this manner, the mean positions of the planets for one's own local position are determined.

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Mathematics <note>original: "Ganakarajani"</note></header>

As for the mean planets <note>The "mean" position is a theoretical average position of a planet if its orbit were a perfect circle.</note> mentioned previously, their degrees increase due to their own "slow" apogees and "fast" apogees <note>original: "mandocca" and "shighrocca." These refer to the points in an orbit where a planet appears to move slowest (apogee) or is affected by the Earth's own movement (parallax).</note>, causing them to be drawn toward the eastern direction.

When the degrees decrease because of these same slow and fast apogees, the planets are drawn toward the western direction.

The true positions of the planets <note>original: "sphuta"</note> are determined by the specific results of the "slow" anomaly and "fast" anomaly <note>original: "kendra." This is the angular distance between a planet and its apogee.</note>. Specifically, because of these high and low points, at the apogee <gloss>position</gloss>, the mean planet and the true planet are the same. At the end of Gemini <note>original: "Mithuna"</note>, this correction reaches its fullness; then, at the end of Cancer <note>original: "Karka"</note>, the mean planet again becomes the true planet. At the end of Sagittarius <note>original: "Dhanus"</note>, the true planet reaches its maximum correction, and at the end of Pisces <note>original: "Meena"</note>, the mean planet once again equals the true planet. Regarding planetary velocity, the mean velocity becomes the true velocity at the high and low points of Cancer and Capricorn, and it reaches its maximum variation at the intermediate points between these high and low positions. Regarding this subject, there are 24 sines <note>original: "jya"</note> produced; let it be known that for each unit, there are 8 "lives" <note>original: "jiva," a synonym for sine.</note>. For 3 zodiac signs (90 degrees), 24 sines are established. The sine of 3 signs is called the *Trijya* (Radius). It is considered the radius of the circle. This is also called the "Earth's Radius" <note>In this context, it refers to the radius of the mathematical circle used for calculations, not the physical size of the Earth.</note>.

### The Square of the Radius and Solar Anomaly

> Sanskrit Verse: The value 11,898,484 is declared as the square of the radius (Trijya). The sine of the 24 degrees of inclination is 1,397. The Sun’s own apogee is 78, 18, 10, 10. <note>original: "Kritabdhi-vasv-anka-shashi-vaneshvarah 11898484... Jinamsha-jiva 24... Divakaroccam..." These are numerical mnemonics using the Katapayadi system where words represent numbers.</note>

The square of the radius <note>original: "Trijya-vargam"</note> is 11,898,484. The degrees of inclination <note>original: "kranti-bhaga," referring to the obliquity of the ecliptic.</note> are 24, and its sine value is 1,397. The Sun's slow apogee is 78, 81, 10, 10.

### Calculating the Anomaly

> Sanskrit Verse: By subtracting the slow apogee from the planet, or the planet from the fast apogee, the "anomaly" (Kendra) is briefly explained. These are divided into groups of three signs each, known as the "arm" (Bhujajya) and the "upright" (Kotijya). <note>original: "Graham-mridu-ccono... Kendra-graha-kramad... Bhuja-koti-samjnite." This describes how to find the angle needed to look up the sine values from the previous table.</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of planetary corrections. While the previous page discussed why the true position of a planet differs from its mean position, this page provides the specific mathematical procedures for calculating the "anomaly" (kendra) and the "sine" (jiva) used in those adjustments.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Mathematics <note>original: "Ganakarajani"</note></header>

To find the "slow anomaly" <note>original: "manda kendra"; the angular distance between a planet and its apogee</note>, one should subtract the position of the "slow apogee" <note>original: "mandocca"</note> from the longitude of the desired planet. Likewise, to find the "fast anomaly" <note>original: "shighra kendra"</note>, subtract the planet's position from its "fast apogee" <note>original: "shighrocca"</note>. From these anomalies, the four quadrants <note>original: "pada"</note> are determined, which together form a complete cycle. In this cycle, the base <note>original: "bhuja"; the side of a right triangle representing the sine</note> and the perpendicular <note>original: "koti"; representing the cosine</note> are identified as follows: in the first and third quadrants, the value is called the "base," and in the second and fourth quadrants, the value is called the "perpendicular."

### Calculation of the Base and Perpendicular <note>original: "Bhujakoti sadhanam"</note>

> Verse: If the anomaly is within three signs, it remains as is. If it exceeds three but is within six signs, subtract it from six. If it exceeds six but is within nine, subtract six from it. If it exceeds nine, subtract the value from the full circle of twelve signs. This remaining result is the "base" <note>original: "bhuja"</note>.

In summary: If the anomaly is within 3 signs <note>90 degrees</note>, that value is the base. If it is between 3 and 6 signs, subtract the anomaly from 6 signs to get the base. If it is between 6 and 9 signs, subtract 6 signs from the anomaly to get the base. If it is between 9 and 12 signs, subtract the anomaly from 12 signs to get the base. Subtracting the base from 3 signs yields the perpendicular <note>original: "koti"</note>.

### Calculation of the Sine <note>original: "Bhujajya sadhanam"</note>

> Verse: Take the base in minutes and divide it by 225 <note>The traditional Indian arc-segment for sine tables</note>. The quotient gives the index of the sine. Multiply the remainder by the difference between the current and the next sine value, then divide by 225. Adding this result to the previous sine value yields the precise sine of the base <note>original: "bhujajya"</note>.

First, determine the base and convert it into minutes <note>original: "lipta"</note>. Divide these minutes by 225; the resulting whole number is the index of the sine <note>original: "jiva"</note>. Note this number down. Take the remainder and multiply it by the difference between the current sine and the next sine in the table. Divide that product by 225. Add the resulting fraction <note>original: "pradikamu"</note> to the initial sine value. This final sum is the Sine of the Base <note>original: "bhujajya"</note>.

### Calculation of the Resulting Sine based on the Sine of the Base Anomaly

### True Positions of the Sun and Moon <note>original: "Surya Chandra Sphutam"</note>

> Verse: Multiply the sine of the base anomaly by the specific multipliers—3, 9, and 7—and divide the result by the planet’s own scale to find the correction in minutes. By adding or subtracting this from the mean position according to the direction of the anomaly, the true positions of the Sun and Moon are found.

<vocab>Slow apogee (mandocca), Fast apogee (shighrocca), Anomaly (kendra), Base (bhuja), Perpendicular (koti), Zodiac sign (rashi), Sine calculation (bhujajya sadhanam), Minute of arc (lipta), Sine (jiva), True position of Sun and Moon (Surya Chandra Sphutam), Velocity/Motion (gati), Fractional part (pradikamu)</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical instructions for planetary calculations. While the previous page defined how to find the "base" (bhuja) and "perpendicular" (koti) from a planet's anomaly, this page provides the specific ratios and procedures for calculating the "True Sun," "True Moon," and their respective daily motions.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Mathematics</header>

Subtract the Sun's **slow apogee** <note>original: "suryamanda"; the point in the orbit where the planet moves slowest</note>—recorded as 2 signs and 18 degrees—from the Sun's local position to find the **anomaly** <note>original: "kendra"</note>. Determine the base of this anomaly and calculate the **sine of the base** <note>original: "bhujajya"</note> using the standard rules. Multiply this sine by 36 and divide by 80. The resulting minutes and seconds should be added if the anomaly begins with **Aries** <note>the first six signs</note> and subtracted if it begins with **Libra** <note>the latter six signs</note>. The result is the **True Sun** <note>original: "suryasphuta"</note>.

->**For the True Moon**<-

> Verse: Multiply the motion of the planet by the value of the solar correction, and divide the product by 21,600 <note>original: "ambara-bhupa-locanai"; the total minutes in a circle</note>. The result, in minutes, should be applied to the other planets just as it is for the Sun, adding or subtracting as required.

To calculate this, multiply the value of the Sun’s arc-correction by the motion of the planets and divide by 21,600 <note>the total number of minutes in a 360-degree circle</note>. This correction should be applied to all other planets in the same manner as the Sun.

For the Moon: Subtract the **Moon's apogee** <note>original: "candrocca"</note> from the Moon's position to determine the anomaly. Find the sine of the base (*bhujajya*) from this anomaly, multiply it by 7, and divide by 80. If the anomaly falls within the first six signs starting from **Aries**, subtract the resulting minutes; if it falls within the six signs starting from **Libra**, add them.

->**Calculation of True Motion**<-

> Verse: Divide the difference of the sine segments by 101 <note>original: "kshiti-bhendubhi"</note> for the Sun. For the Moon, multiply the difference by 10 and divide by 33 <note>original: "suradhritam"</note>. By adding or subtracting this from the motion based on the anomaly’s position in the signs of Capricorn or Cancer, the **True Motion** is found.

To find the **True Motion** <note>original: "sphutagati"; the actual daily speed of a planet</note>: Take the difference between the sine segments <note>the values used to calculate the sine of the anomaly</note>. For the Sun, divide this difference by 101 to get the result in minutes. For the Moon, multiply the difference of the segments by 10 and divide by 33. This result is then applied to the **mean motion** <note>original: "madhyagati"</note>. Specifically, if the anomaly is in the six signs starting from **Capricorn** <note>the first and fourth quadrants</note>, subtract the value. If the anomaly is in the six signs starting from **Cancer** <note>the second and third quadrants</note>, add it to the mean motion. This calculation yields the True Motion.

# Calculating Days, Lunar Days, and Others in Sequence

> Verse: Subtract the Sun from the Moon and divide the remaining minutes by 720 <note>original: "ashvi-parvatai"; 720 minutes equals the 12 degrees that define one lunar day</note> to determine the current **lunar day** <note>original: "tithi"</note>.

Following this principle, one should calculate the **lunar day** (*tithi*), **star** <note>original: "nakshatra"</note>, **astrological division** (*yoga*), and **half-day** (*karana*). At certain times, if the calculation exceeds sixty by 11 seconds <note>original: "vliptas"</note> or more for 2 or 3 consecutive instances, one must take the 19 completed lunar days, adjust the **day** (*ahassu*) accordingly, and record it as the elapsed time.

## Method for Calculating Corrected Slow and Fast Circumferences:

> Verse: Four <note>original: "veda"; 4</note>, four, zero, two, and four <note>original: "kshindu-yama-abdhayo"; interpreting the sequence for the planets</note> are the values for the slow movement. Multiply these in order by the sine of the anomaly <note>original: "bhuja-jya"</note>. Divide the result by the radius <note>original: "vyasardhe"; 3438</note>. For some, add the result, and for others, subtract. Using the values four, two, two, two, and four <note>original: "dvi-dvandva-dvi-kubhih"; 4, 2, 2, 2, 4</note>... <unclear>portion of verse</unclear>... for the difference in the fast movement, divide by the radius <note>original: "trijyaya"; 3438</note>. All these corrected values are for the fast circumferences.

Meaning: After subtracting the position of the slow apogee <term>Mandochcha: The point in a planet's orbit where it moves slowest, used as a reference point for corrections</term> from the positions of Mars and the other planets, you find the anomaly <note>original: "kendra"</note>. From this anomaly, calculate the Sine of the base <term>Bhujajya: The sine value of the angular distance from the nearest point on the orbital axis</term>. Multiply that Sine value by the following multipliers: 4 for Mars, 2 for Mercury, 1 for Jupiter <note>Asterisk in text likely refers to a missing note or correction</note>, 2 for Venus, and 4 for Saturn. 

Divide each result by the standard Radius <term>Trijya: The radius of the mathematical circle, here defined as 3438 arcminutes</term>. The resulting quotient should be added to the base slow multipliers of Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn. For Mercury and Venus, the quotient must be subtracted from their base multipliers. This calculation yields the **Corrected Slow Multipliers** <note>original: "sphuta manda gunakas"</note>.

Regarding the fast correction: Subtract the planet’s position from its fast apogee <note>original: "shighra"</note> to find the anomaly. From this, calculate the Sine of the base and the Sine of the upright <note>original: "bhuja-koti-jyas"</note>. 

--- Multiply the Sine of the base by the following: 2 for Mars, 2 for Mercury, 1 for Jupiter, 2 for Venus, and 1 for Saturn. Divide the result by the Radius (3438). Subtract the resulting quotient from their respective base fast multipliers to obtain the **Corrected Fast Multipliers**.

## Main Summary:

Multiply the Sine of the base by 4 for Mars, 2 for Mercury, 1 for Jupiter, 2 for Venus, and 4 for Saturn. Divide by the Radius. Add the resulting quotient to the slow circumferences <note>original: "manda paridhi"</note> of Mars (14), Jupiter (7), and Saturn (8). For Venus and Mercury, subtract the quotient. This gives the **Corrected Slow Sine Results**.

Regarding the fast correction: Multiply the Sine of the base by 2 for Mars, 2 for Mercury, 1 for Jupiter, 2 for Venus, and 1 for Saturn. Divide by the Radius. Subtract the resulting quotient from the base fast circumferences, which are: Mars (53), Mercury (31), Jupiter (16), Venus (59), and Saturn (9). This result is the **Corrected Fast Circumference**.

<header>The Delight of Mathematicians.</header>

### Method for Calculating the Sine, Cosine, and Variable Hypotenuse.

**Verse:** The square root of the square of the Sine <note>original: "dorjya-varga"</note> subtracted from the square of the Radius <note>original: "vitatra-bhavana-jya-varga"; here implying the square of the total sine or radius</note> becomes the Cosine <note>original: "koti" / "kotijya"</note>. Alternatively, subtract the degrees of the base from 90 <note>original: "tri-amsha" or 90 degrees</note> and find the Sine of the remainder to get the Cosine. Multiply the Sine and Cosine by the corrected circumference and divide by 80 <note>original: "bhuvanudhih"; 80</note>. These are the Sine-result and Cosine-result; use them to calculate the variable hypotenuse.

**Meaning:** If you take the square of the Sine-result <term>Bhujaphala: The result of the sine of the anomaly multiplied by the epicycle and divided by the radius/circumference unit</term> derived from the center/anomaly and subtract it from the square of the Radius <term>Trijya: The standard mathematical radius</term>, the square root will be the Cosine. If not that way, you can convert the base into degrees, subtract them from 90, and the Sine of that remainder will also be the Cosine. Both methods are identical. 

Then, multiply these Sine and Cosine values individually by the multipliers of the corrected circumferences and divide by 80 <note>The divisor 80 is used here as a standard unit for epicyclic calculations in this specific system</note>. The results obtained are respectively the Sine-result <note>Bhujajyaphala</note> and the Cosine-result <note>Kotijyaphala</note>. This Cosine-result should be added to the Radius during the first and fourth quadrants of the anomaly. It should be subtracted during the middle quadrants (the second and third). That is: from the 4th to the 9th signs it is negative, and from the 9th to the 4th signs it is positive. This yields the "pure" Cosine <note>Shuddha-koti</note>. By adding the square of that pure Cosine and the square of the Sine-result, and then taking the square root of the sum, you obtain the "Slow Hypotenuse" <term>Mandakarna: The distance from the center of the earth to the planet on its slow epicycle</term> for slow motion, and the "Fast Variable Hypotenuse" <term>Shighra-chalakarna: The variable hypotenuse used for the fast motion correction</term> for fast motion.

### The Slow-Correction for All Planets.

**Verse:** Subtract the position of the slow apogee <note>original: "mandochcha"</note> from the planet's longitude to find the anomaly. From the resulting Sine-result, apply the correction to the mean planet. If the anomaly is in the half beginning with Aries, subtract; if in the half beginning with Libra, add... <note>original: "meshadi-rinam tuladi-dhanam"</note>. Subtract the result from the fast apogee.

**Meaning:** After taking the mean planets which have already undergone the solar-sine correction, subtract their specific slow apogee <term>Mandochcha: The point of slowest motion</term>. From that slow anomaly <note>Mandakendra</note>, calculate the Sine. Multiply that Sine by the corrected circumference and divide by 80. The result is the "Slow Correction" <note>Mandaphala</note> in minutes and seconds. If the result is positive, add it; if negative, subtract it. Use the quadrants to determine this: for anomalies beginning with Aries, the correction is negative; for those beginning with Libra, it is positive.

**Verse:** For the fast motion, determine the fast anomaly and the Sine. From the Cosine-result, subtract or add to the Radius... subtract based on the signs starting with Capricorn...

<meta>This page continues the specific mathematical steps for calculating planetary positions. While page 75 defined the basic Sine and Cosine results, page 76 explains how to combine these to find the variable hypotenuse and the final "true" position of planets like Mars.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Mathematicians.</header>

**Meaning:** After individually examining the planets in their "fast" motions <term>Shighra: The fast anomaly, equivalent to the second inequality or planetary epicycle in ancient systems</term>, one must calculate the Sine <note>original: "bhujajya"</note> and Cosine <note>original: "kotijya"</note> based on the previously described fast anomaly center. Multiply these Sine and Cosine values by the fast orbital circumference multipliers and divide by the sign-unit <note>Dividing by the value of a zodiac sign (30 degrees or a related constant) to normalize the units</note>; the resulting values will be the Sine-result <note>original: "bhujaphala"</note> and Cosine-result <note>original: "kotiphala"</note> respectively. 

This Cosine-result is added to the Radius <note>original: "trijya"</note> during the first and fourth quadrants of the center, and subtracted during the middle (second and third) quadrants. By applying this correction to the Radius, one arrives at the "Corrected Cosine" <note>original: "shuddha-koti"</note>.

> **Verse:** The square root of the sum of the squares of the [Corrected Cosine] and the Sine-result becomes the Hypotenuse <note>original: "karna"</note>. Multiply the Sine-result by the Radius <note>original: "trimaurvyam"</note> and divide by that Hypotenuse; the resulting arc-value is known as the "fast-result" <note>original: "ashu-sangjnam" or Shighraphala</note>. Depending on whether the center begins with Aries or Libra, this result is applied to the planet’s position. Following the previous methods, the entire slow-motion correction <note>original: "mandaphala"</note> should be performed. By applying the "fast-result" to the planet's mean position, and further refining it with these corrections, the planet attains its true, corrected state <note>original: "sphuta-tam"</note>.

**Meaning:** By taking the square of the uncorrected Cosine <note>The text uses "ashuddha-koti" here, referring to the value before final Radius adjustment</note> and the square of the Sine-result, and then finding their square root, one obtains the Variable Hypotenuse <note>original: "chalakarna"</note>. Multiply the Sine-result by the Radius and divide it by the Variable Hypotenuse associated with the fast motion. The value obtained for the Sine of the fast-correction should be converted into an arc-shape <note>transformed from a chord/sine back into degrees/minutes</note>. Half of this value is added if the center is in the half starting with Aries, and subtracted if it is in the half starting with Libra.

For whichever planet is being calculated, one must first subtract its own slow-apogee <note>original: "mandochcha"</note> to find the "slow-center." Using this center, calculate the slow-correction <note>original: "mandaphala"</note> as described previously and convert it into minutes of arc. This entire process constitutes the third correction. When this is applied to the first mean planet position—either as a subtraction or addition based on the Aries/Libra signs—it becomes the "Third Slow-Corrected Planet."

By subtracting that "slow-corrected planet" from its own "fast-point" <note>the planet's second anomaly point</note>, one finds the "fast-center." From this center, the fast-correction is derived as before. When this final correction (converted to minutes) is applied to the slow-corrected planet, determining the addition or subtraction based on Aries or Libra, the result is the "True Planet" <note>original: "sphuta-graha"</note>. This is the method for calculating the true positions of Mars and the other planets.

<vocab>Sine-result (Bhujaphala), Cosine-result (Kotiphala), Fast Anomaly Center (Shighra-kendra), Radius (Trijya), Corrected Cosine (Shuddha-koti), Variable Hypotenuse (Chalakarna), Slow-Apogee (Mandochcha), Slow-Correction (Mandaphala), True Planet (Sphuta-graha)</vocab>

**Verse:** By applying half of the fast-result <note>original: "shighraphala"</note> to the planet’s position, the planet is prepared. Then, by adjusting for the slow-apogee <note>original: "mandocca"</note> and applying the slow-result, the planet becomes "Slow-True." Finally, by applying the full fast-result, the planet's daily true position is established.

**Meaning:** The method is as follows: From the fast-center <term>Shighra-kendra: The angular distance of the planet from its fast anomaly point</term>, calculate the fast-result. Take half of this value and apply it (add or subtract) to the mean planet. Following this, calculate the slow-apogee <term>Mandochcha: The point of the planet's slowest motion, similar to the aphelion</term> and apply half of the resulting slow-correction. Then, re-calculate the slow-apogee to find the full slow-result; apply this full result to the original mean planet to obtain the "Slow-True" planet <note>original: "Mandasphuta"</note>. If you then subtract this Slow-True position from the planet's own fast-motion point to find the fast-center, and apply that full fast-result to the Slow-True planet, you will arrive at the final "True" positions of the planets.

### Calculating the True Slow-Velocity

**Verse:** Multiply the Sine-segment <note>original: "jyakhanda"</note> produced by the slow-center by the planet's daily motion. Divide the result by 225. Then, multiply by the multiplier and divide by 80 <note>original: "khnu-nagai"</note> to find the correction for the velocity.

**Meaning:** Take the difference of the Sines <term>Jyakhanda: The difference between consecutive entries in a Sine table</term> of the slow-center and multiply it by the specific planetary velocity. Divide this product by 225. Take the resulting value and multiply it by the slow-circumference multipliers, then divide by 80. The result will be in seconds of arc <note>original: "vliptas"</note>; divide this by 60 to convert it into minutes of arc <note>original: "kalas"</note>. This result is the velocity-correction <note>original: "gati-phala"</note>. If the center is in the first or last quadrants, subtract this from the mean velocity; if it is in the middle quadrants, add it to the planet's motion. This calculation provides the "Slow-True" velocity <note>original: "Mandasphuta-gati"</note>.

**Meaning:** Once the "Slow-True" motion is calculated after the slow-result correction, subtract it from the planet's own fast-motion to find the "center-velocity." Then, using the Sine-segments of the fast-center...

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Mathematicians</header>

Multiply the mean result <note>original: "madhyam" referring here to the mean anomaly</note> and divide by 225. Multiply the resulting quotient by its specific "true multiplier" <term>Sphuta-gunaka: A constant used to adjust the size of the planet's epicycle</term>, and divide by 225 again. Multiply that quotient once more by the radius <note>original: "trijya"</note> and divide by the planet’s own "variable hypotenuse" <term>Chala-karna: The distance from the Earth to the planet in the epicyclic model</term>. The final result obtained is the fast-correction <note>original: "shighraphala"</note>. This result should be added (positive) in the 1st and 4th quadrants and subtracted (negative) in the middle (2nd and 3rd) quadrants.

### The Method for Performing Arc Correction
<note>Dhanusamshkaram: The process of finding the arc (angle) when the Sine value is known.</note>

> **Verse:** Subtract the passed Sine from the result, multiply the remainder by 225, and divide by the difference between the current and next Sine values. Add the quotient to the product of 225 and the number of Sine-segments already passed; this is the arc.
> <note>original Sanskrit: "vishodhya jiva shara-drk-skara... dhanurbhavet"</note>

**Meaning:** If the result of the Sine calculation <term>Bhujaphala: The result of the sine of the anomaly</term> exceeds 225 <note>In ancient Indian trigonometry, 225 minutes (3° 45') was the standard interval for Sine tables</note>, an arc correction must be performed. The method is as follows: subtract the value of the "passed Sine" <note>the largest entry in the Sine table that is smaller than your result</note> and multiply the remaining value by 225. Divide this by the difference between the Sine that was just passed and the next Sine in the table. The resulting value should be added to the product of 225 and the number of Sine-segments that were completed. This sum is the true arc correction.

### Demonstration

When the result is 450: if two Sine-segments (totaling 449) are subtracted, the remainder is 1 minute and 2 seconds. Multiply this remainder by 225, which gives 225. Divide this by the "Sine-difference" <note>original: "jivantara"</note> of 232. The result is approximately 1 minute and 2 seconds. Since two Sine-segments were passed (2 x 225 = 450), add the result (1' 2") to 450. The final arc-corrected result <note>original: "dhanusamskara-bhujaphalam"</note> is 451' 2". This value is to be applied to the planet's position.

### Principal Opinion Regarding Mars <note>original: "Kujudu"</note>

According to the teachings of the great teacher Aryabhata <note>Aryabhata I, the 5th-century mathematician-astronomer</note>, the procedure for Mars is as follows:
First, perform the slow-correction <note>Manda-samskaram</note>. Second, apply half of the fast-correction. Third, apply the total resulting correction to the original mean planet. Fourth, perform the double-arc correction <note>dvibhu-phala</note>. This method is considered accurate and aligns with the observed positions of the stars <note>original: "rishyansha," likely referring to the sidereal/precessional alignment</note>.

## Calculation of Total Days since the Epoch
<note>Kalpadadyugana: Calculating the "Ahargana," or the number of days elapsed from the beginning of the current cosmic cycle (Kalpa) to the present day.</note>

**Verse:** Multiply the years elapsed in the Kalpa by 12, add the passed months, [and multiply by 30]. Add the lunar days <term>Tithis</term>. Multiply by the intercalary month ratio and add the resulting extra months. Multiply by 30 and add the current lunar day. Subtract the "omitted days" <term>Kshaya-tithis</term> derived from the specific ratios (703). The result is the total days since the start of the cycle, which can be used to determine the day of the week.
<note>original Sanskrit: "kalpyābdorka 12 guṇo māsayuk..."</note>

**Commentary:** The meaning of this verse is found in the first chapter. However, there is a variation here regarding how the years of the Kalpa are multiplied by 12 and the method by which the intercalary months <term>Adhikamasa: Extra lunar months added to align the lunar and solar years</term> are calculated. This is the primary difference.

### Calculating the Mean Planets

#### For the Sun

> **Verse:** The total day-count <term>Ahargana: The number of days elapsed from a fixed epoch to the current date</term>, diminished by its seventieth (70th) part, produces the Sun's position in degrees. The day-count multiplied by two (2) and divided by five (5) yields the correction in seconds <term>Vilipta: Seconds of arc; 1/60th of a minute</term>, which must also be subtracted.

**Explanation:** Place the day-count <note>original: *dyugana*</note> in two separate positions. Divide the first by 70. Subtract the resulting degrees from the total day-count. Next, multiply the day-count by 2 and divide by 5; the resulting seconds should be subtracted from the previous result. Finally, divide the degrees by 30 to determine the zodiac signs <note>original: *rashyadi*; 30 degrees make one zodiac sign</note> to find the mean position of the Sun.

---

#### For the Moon

> **Verse:** To find the Moon’s position, multiply the day-count by thirteen (13). From this, subtract the quotient of the day-count divided by seventy-four (74). Further, for precise seconds, divide the day-count by one thousand and thirty (1030) and subtract that as well.

**Explanation:** Multiply the day-count by 13 and set this value in two places. Divide one part by 74. Divide the other part by 1030. Subtract both of these quotients from the initial product (the day-count multiplied by 13). The result will be the mean position of the Moon in degrees and signs.

---

### Calculating Mars

> **Verse:** Divide the day-count by two (2) and add its twentieth (20th) part to the degrees. Then, divide the day-count by eighty (80) to find the seconds-correction, which is also added to determine the son of the Earth <note>original: *mahisuta*; a traditional name for the planet Mars</note>.

**Explanation:** Divide the day-count by 2 and set it in two places. Divide the lower part by 20 and add it to the degrees in the upper part. Then, divide the day-count by 80; add the resulting seconds to the previous sum. This calculation yields the mean position of Mars in degrees.

---

### Calculating the Fast-Motion of Mercury

> **Verse:** [Using the multiplier] two, two, and four... divide by sixty-five (65)... for the fast-motion <term>Shighra: The "fast" anomaly or the planet's conjunction with the Sun</term> of Mercury...

<note>This verse appears to be an abbreviated mnemonic or is partially obscured in the original manuscript. It provides the parameters for Mercury's rapid orbital calculations.</note>
