<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Mathematicians</header>

**Explanation:** Multiply the day-count <term>Ahargana: The total number of days elapsed since a fixed epoch</term> by four (4) to get the degrees. Next, multiply the day-count by sixty (60) and divide by sixty-five (65); add this result to the previous figure to determine the position of **Mercury’s Fast Apex** <term>Budha-shighrochcha: In Indian astronomy, this represents the position of the mean Sun, used to calculate the longitude of an inferior planet like Mercury</term>.

### Calculation of Jupiter

> **Verse:** Multiply by five <note>original: *shara* is the Sanskrit word for "five," referring to the five arrows of the love-god</note>, then subtract the 344th part of that product. This provides the fixed position of Jupiter <note>original: *Jiva*</note> in minutes and higher units.

**Explanation:** Multiply the day-count by 5 and keep the result in two places. Divide the lower figure by 344. Subtract this quotient from the upper figure. The result is the position of **Jupiter** in minutes <term>Kala: A minute of arc; 1/60th of a degree</term>.

### Calculation of Venus’s Fast Apex

> **Verse:** The day-count is multiplied by eight (8). Separately, divide by five (5) to get degrees and by sixty-two (62) to get minutes. These must be added in sequence to successfully find the **Fast Apex of Venus** <note>original: *Sita-shighrochcha*</note>.

**Explanation:** Multiply the day-count by 8 and set the result in two places. Divide the first by 5 to find the degrees. Divide the second by 62 to find the minutes. Adding these two results together yields the position of Venus’s Fast Apex.

### Calculation of Saturn

> **Verse:** Multiply the day-count by two (2) for minutes. To this, add the quotient of the day-count divided by three hundred and fifteen <note>original: *sharendurama* – Shara (5), Indu (1), Rama (3) read right-to-left as 315</note>.

**Explanation:** Multiply the day-count by 2 and set it in two places. Divide the lower figure by 315. Add the resulting minutes to the upper figure to find the position of **Saturn** in minutes.

### For the Lunar Apogee

> **Verse:** Divide the day-count by nine (9) and also by sixty-one (61) <note>original: *rupa-rasai* – Rupa (1), Rasa (6) as 61</note> to find the position of the **Moon’s High Point** <term>Chandrochcha: The Lunar Apogee, the point where the Moon is farthest from Earth</term> in degrees and minutes.

**Explanation:** Divide the day-count by 9 to get a value in degrees. Divide the day-count by 61 to get a value in minutes. Adding these two together gives the position of the Lunar Apogee in degrees.

# Calculation of Rahu

> **Verse:** Divide the day-count by nineteen (19) <note>original: *navendu* - nine and one</note> to get the degrees, and by forty-four (44) <note>original: *naveda* - here representing 44 in the local numbering system</note> to get the minutes. Subtract this from the full circle. One must add three (3) signs to the lunar apogee for accuracy. For Rahu, subtract the result from six (6) signs <note>original: *bhachakra* - the zodiac wheel</note> to find the correct position.

**Explanation:** By dividing the total day-count <term>Dyugana: The number of days elapsed since the start of the epoch</term> by 19, you obtain the degrees. By dividing the day-count by 44, you obtain the minutes <term>Kala: 1/60th of a degree</term>. Adding these two results together gives the position of Rahu <note>Rahu and Ketu are the "shadow planets" or nodes where eclipses occur</note>. Note that the Lunar Apogee <term>Chandrochcha: The point where the Moon is farthest from Earth</term> requires an addition of 3 signs to be accurate. For Rahu, one must subtract the value from 6 signs to find the true position.

In this manner, the mean positions of the planets <term>Madhya-grahas: The average positions of planets before correcting for their elliptical orbits</term> should be established. 
To achieve observational accuracy <term>Driksiddha: Validated by sight/observation</term> as mentioned previously, one must apply several corrections: the Epochal correction <term>Kalyadi-samskara: Adjustment based on the start of the Kali Yuga</term>, the Observational correction <term>Drik-samskara</term>, the Longitudinal correction <term>Deshantara-samskara: Adjusting for the distance from the prime meridian, usually Ujjain</term>, and the Equation of the Center <term>Bhujaphala-samskara: Correcting for the planet's non-circular path</term>. By doing so, the planets will align with the **Aryabhata Siddhanta** <note>The astronomical system of the 5th-century mathematician Aryabhata</note>.

## The Epochal Correction (Kalyadi)

> **Verse:** Multiply the Kali-era day-count by ten (10). Divide by 4,742 for the Sun; 690 for the Moon; 224 for Mars; 51 for Mercury; 498 for Jupiter; 153 for Venus; 200 for Saturn; 174 for Rahu; and 140 for the Lunar Apogee. Subtract these results from the respective planets, except for Mars, Mercury's fast apex, and Saturn, where they should be added. This aligns the calculation with the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>The most authoritative traditional Indian astronomical text, considered "revealed" by the Sun God</note>.

**Explanation:** Multiply the years elapsed in the Kali Era by 10 and divide by the following specific divisors: 4,742 for the Sun, 690 for the Moon, 224 for Mars, 51 for Mercury, 498 for Jupiter, 153 for Venus, 200 for Saturn, 174 for Rahu, and 140 for the Lunar Apogee. The resulting minutes should be subtracted from the positions of the Sun, Moon, Lunar Apogee, Jupiter, Venus’s apex, and Rahu. However, for Mars, Mercury’s apex, and Saturn, these values must be added. Applying these corrections makes the results equivalent to the Surya Siddhanta system.

## The Observational Correction

In the system of Aryabhata, for the period starting from the beginning of the Kali Yuga <term>Kalyadi: The start of the current cosmic age, traditionally dated to 3102 BCE</term>, besides the usual adjustments, one must also perform the visual correction <term>Druk-samskara: An adjustment applied to calculated positions to ensure they match actual observations in the sky</term>. This subject was addressed in the preceding text; one can understand it by looking there.

### Longitudinal Correction
<term>Desantara-samskara: An adjustment made to planetary positions to account for the geographic distance (longitude) between the observer and the prime meridian, usually Ujjain in ancient Indian astronomy.</term>

> **Verse:** Multiply the distance in yojanas by the motion, and divide by sixty (60).

**Explanation:** Multiply the mean motion by the travel distance of 40 *yojanas* <term>Yojana: A traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</term> and divide by 80. The resulting value, starting from minutes <term>Kala: 1/60th of a degree</term>, must be subtracted because we are located in the eastern region <note>Observers east of the prime meridian subtract this value to align their local time with the meridian time</note>.

### Equation of Center Correction
<term>Bhujaphala-samskara: A correction that accounts for the eccentricity of a planet's orbit, turning the "mean" position into a "true" position.</term>

> **Verse:** Three hundred forty-five (345) <note>original: *pancha-tur-ramaih*</note>... twenty-one (21) <note>original: *murni-netrayoh*</note>...

As I have explained this previously, one should follow that same method.

### Calculation of the Precession of Equinoxes
<term>Ayanamsha-sadhanam: The calculation of the shifting of the equinoxes over time due to the Earth's axial precession.</term>

> **Verse:** Multiply the elapsed years of the Kali age by three (3) and divide by 1000 <note>original: *kha-kha-nabhu*</note>... [the remainder of the calculation involves divisions and comparisons]... the smaller of the two results provides the degrees of precession.

## The Beginning of the Chapter on Subtle True Positions

> **Verse:** For the planets, the degrees of the slow apogee <term>Manda: The "slow" or "slow-motion" point, referring to the planet's apogee used to calculate the Equation of the Center</term> are [as follows]: Twelve (12) <note>original: *naga-arka* - seven and twelve, here specifically 12</note>; Two hundred twenty (220) <note>original: *asva-asva-dasra*</note>; One hundred seventy (170) <note>original: *kha-atyashti*</note>; Eighty (80) <note>original: *kha-ashta*</note>; and Two hundred forty (240) <note>original: *kha-abhdhya-asva*</note>. These are used when the planet is subtracted from its slow apogee to find the anomaly.

**Explanation:** The slow apogee values <term>Manda: Points of correction for planetary orbits</term> are: for **Mars**, 4 signs, 10 degrees, 0 minutes; for **Mercury**, 7 signs, 10 degrees, 0 minutes, 0 seconds; for **Jupiter**, 5 signs, 20 degrees, 0 minutes, 0 seconds; for **Venus**, 2 signs, 20 degrees, 0 minutes, 0 seconds; and for **Saturn**, 9 signs, 0 degrees, 0 minutes, 0 seconds.
As stated in the text <term>Ganitanandamu</term> <note>A classic astronomical treatise</note>, the value is 517 <note>original: *shailendu-banai* - mountains (7), moon (1), arrows (5)</note>. It appears that applying this adjustment to the slow apogee values is a crucial step. However, this does not result in a significant difference in the final calculation.

### The True Calculation of the Manda Adjustment

->For this purpose, I am stating the divisors below.<-

> **Verse:** The slow divisors <term>Manda-chedas: Specific numbers used as divisors to calculate the correction for a planet's elliptical orbit</term> are: Three hundred sixty-three (363) <note>original: *tri-shat-asra*</note>; One hundred fourteen (114) <note>original: *gati-sha*</note>; Five hundred eleven (511) <note>original: *asva-indra-sayakas*</note>; One hundred twenty-four (124) <note>original: *gaja-arka*</note>; One hundred twelve (112) <note>original: *ravi-chandra-asva*</note>; Three hundred sixty-three (363) <note>original: *tri-shat-asra*</note>; and Seventy-four (74) <note>original: *asva-ra-achala*</note>. Multiply the sine of the arc <term>Bhujajya: The sine of the planet's angular distance from its apogee</term> by ten (10) and divide by these respective divisors to find the correction in minutes. This is added or subtracted depending on the quadrant to find the positions of the Sun and Moon.

**Explanation:** Calculate the <term>Bhujajya</term> (sine of the anomaly), multiply that sine by 10, and divide by the following divisors: for the **Sun**, 363; for the **Moon**, 114; for **Mars**, 511; for **Mercury**, 124; for **Jupiter**, 112; for **Venus**, 363; and for **Saturn**, 74. The resulting <term>Manda-phala</term> (the Equation of the Center) is used to correct the positions of the Sun and Moon to find their true longitudes. While the <term>Manda-samskara</term> (the slow correction) for Mars and the other planets is performed in the same way, this correction should be applied only where the <term>Charya-sphutam</term> (the true daily motion or specific correction) is absolutely necessary.

## Method for Calculating the Slow Motion for All Planets

> **Verse:** Multiply the motion by the difference of the sines of the anomaly; divide by two hundred twenty-five (225). Again, multiply by ten (10) and divide by the specific divisor of each planet. The resulting minutes of arc <note>original: *liptā*</note> are applied to the mean motion based on whether the anomaly is in the half-cycle beginning with Cancer or Capricorn. This results in the true motion for the slow-moving planets <term>Manda: Referring to the correction for a planet's elliptical orbit</term> like Mars and the others.

**Explanation:** Multiply the mean motion by the difference of the sines <term>Jya: A chord or sine-like function used in ancient Indian trigonometry</term> of the planet’s anomaly. Divide this by **225**. Multiply the resulting quotient by **10** and then divide by the specific divisor assigned to each planet. The result is the correction in minutes of arc.

### Chapter on the True Position of the Slow Motion

If the calculated correction falls in the half-cycle beginning with **Cancer**, it must be added to the mean motion. If it falls in the half-cycle beginning with **Capricorn**, it must be subtracted. This process yields the true slow motion.

### Circumferences of the Fast Motion

> **Verse:** Five-and-thirty (**35**) <note>original: *pañcāgni*</note> [minutes]. Ten (**10**) minutes. Two-and-fifty (**52**); eight-and-fifty (**58**). For Mars, Venus, and Saturn; the multipliers for the fast motion <term>Śīghra: The "fast" correction, which accounts for the Earth's motion (parallax) relative to the other planets</term> of Mercury and Jupiter: fifteen (**15**) <note>original: *tithi*</note> and three (**3**) minutes added to thirty-one (**31**) and sixteen (**16**).

**Explanation:** For **Mars**, the value is **52**; for **Venus**, it is **58**; and for **Saturn**, it is **60**. By adding the "five-and-thirty" (**35**) minutes, the true circumference <term>Paridhi: The epicycle or circumference used to calculate the planet's deviation from its mean path</term> for **Mars** becomes **52 | 35**; for **Venus**, **58 | 35**; and for **Saturn**, **60 | 35**. For **Mercury**, the value is **31**, and for **Jupiter**, it is **16**. By adding the "fifteen and three" <note>This refers to 15 and 35 minutes respectively</note>, the true circumference for **Mercury** is **31 | 15**, and for **Jupiter**, it is **16 | 35**.

->True Fast Multipliers: For **Mars: 52 | 35**; for **Mercury: 31 | 15**; for **Jupiter: 16 | 35**; for **Venus: 58 | 35**; and for **Saturn: 60 | 35**. These are the true multipliers.<-

### Procedure for the Fast Motion Correction

> **Verse:** Subtract the planet's position from the fast anomaly point. From the sine of the anomaly, find the cosine. Multiply by the multiplier and divide <gloss>by the divisor</gloss>... <note>original: *mu*</note> ...in the half-cycles of Cancer and Capricorn, add or subtract the results of the cosine... find the square of that... <gloss>the result</gloss> ...add the square of the sine... find the square root... the radius... <note>The verse becomes fragmentary here, describing the Pythagorean-style calculation of the hypotenuse (the distance from the Earth to the planet).</note>

The sine correction <note>original: *bhujaphala*</note> multiplied by its own hypotenuse <term>Karna: The distance from the center of the earth to the planet</term> gives the resulting sine. Its arc is the fast correction. This arc, beginning with the signs of the zodiac, is applied to the minutes. When the hypotenuse is applied, the planets become "true" <note>original: *spaṣṭāḥ*</note> within the centers beginning with Aries or Libra.

**Explanation:** One should calculate the *Śaindra* anomaly <term>Śaindra: Referring to the anomaly or center used for correction</term> by investigating the fast correction <note>original: *śīghra*</note>. From that center, one must derive the *Bhuja* <term>Bhuja: The sine of the arc</term> and *Koti* <term>Koti: The cosine of the arc</term>. Multiply these sines and cosines by their respective true multipliers <note>original: *harispuṭaguṇakamulu*</note> and divide by **80**; the resulting quotients are the *Bhujajyāphala* and *Koṭijyāphala*. 

If the center begins with the sign of Cancer, the *Koṭijyāphala* should be subtracted from the radius <term>Trijya: The standard radius of the calculation circle</term>. If it begins with Capricorn, it should be added to the radius; this results in the "pure cosine" <note>original: *śuddha-koṭi*</note>. The square of this pure cosine and the square of the sine correction added together, and then square-rooted, results in the diameter. This is specifically named the *Chalakarna* <term>Chalakarna: The variable hypotenuse or distance</term>. 

Multiply the sine correction by the radius and divide by the variable hypotenuse; the resulting value is the *Phalajyā*, which is the sine correction itself. Beginning from that point, the correction of the arc must be performed. For the planets, if they are in the half-cycle beginning with Aries, this is added; if in the half-cycle beginning with Libra, it is subtracted. They then become "true planets" <note>original: *sphuṭagrahulu*</note>.

### The Order of Corrections

> **Verse:** First, perform the Fast correction; next, half of the Slow; then, apply the full Slow correction to the mean planet, and finally the Fast. Thus is the True Planet determined. <note>original Sanskrit: *ādauśīghra daśāṃvidhe...*</note>

**Explanation:** First, perform the Fast correction <note>original: *śīghra-saṃskāram*</note> and apply half of that result. Then, perform the Slow correction <note>original: *mānda-saṃskāram*</note> and apply half of that result. Then again, perform the full Slow correction and apply the entire result to the mean planet. Finally, perform the full Fast correction and apply that result. This process yields the True Planets <note>original: *sphuṭagrahamulu*</note>.

### Calculation of the True Fast Motion

> **Verse:** The difference between the radius and the hypotenuse is calculated by multiplication and subtraction. From the fast hypotenuse, the minutes are held; when the hypotenuse is greater or less than the radius, the resulting motion is the true motion, even during retrograde movement. <note>original Sanskrit: *trijyākarṇastuvi śeṣāgaṇatyo...*</note>

**Explanation:** Subtract the Slow motion from the Fast motion to find the *Śaindra* motion. Regarding this *Śaindra* motion, whether the specific Fast Hypotenuse <note>original: *śīghrakarṇa*</note> is subtracted from the radius or...

Subtract the radius <term>Trijya: The standard radius used in trigonometric calculations, often equivalent to the sine of 90 degrees</term> from the variable hypotenuse <note>original: *calakarṇamu*</note>. Multiply the difference by the variable and divide by the planet’s own fast variable hypotenuse. The resulting value, in minutes and seconds, is the fast motion correction <note>original: *śīghragatiphalam*</note>. When the radius is greater than the fast hypotenuse, the result is a positive correction; when the fast hypotenuse is less than the radius, the result is a negative correction. This fast correction should be applied as an addition or subtraction to the slow-corrected motion <note>original: *mandasphuṭagati*</note> to find the true motion <note>original: *sphuṭagati*</note>. If the negative fast correction is so great that it cannot be subtracted from the slow-corrected motion—meaning the negative fast correction is larger—the planet is said to be in retrograde motion <term>Vakragati: The apparent backward motion of a planet in the sky</term>.

->In this manner, the true planet and its true motion are determined.<-

### Chapter on the Precise True Positions of the Sun and Moon

<margin>Verse</margin>
> Subtract the planet's position from its apogee <term>Mandocca: The point in a planet's orbit furthest from Earth</term> to find the anomaly <term>Kendra: The angular distance from the apogee</term>. Multiply the base sine <note>original: "bhūja-rāśi"</note> by thirty-one, add the degrees, and divide by six to find the sine-table value for the Sun and Moon. By calculating the difference between the sines and multiplying by the remaining minutes, then dividing by the circle of 360, the result should be added to the sine-total. By adding or subtracting based on whether the anomaly is in Aries or Libra, the true motion is known, as well as the difference between the previous and subsequent positions. If the true motion is less than the mean motion, it is "retrograde" <note>This refers to the slowing of the Sun or Moon, as they never physically move backward.</note>; if the true motion exceeds the mean, it is "fast" motion.

Explanation: When a planet's position is subtracted from its apogee, the result is the anomaly <term>Kendra</term>. By calculating the base sine of that anomaly, converting it to signs and degrees, adding the corresponding parts, and dividing by 6, the resulting value is the sine-correction. By taking the difference between these sine values, calculating the minutes, multiplying by that difference, and dividing by 360, the resulting figure is added to the sine-total to find the "base correction" <note>original: "bhuja-phala"</note>. If the anomaly falls in the half of the zodiac starting with Aries, this value is added; if it falls in the half starting with Libra, it is subtracted. The result is the "True Planet" <term>Sphuta-graha: The actual observed position as opposed to the theoretical average position</term>. The difference between the planet's position today and its position yesterday is its "True Velocity." There are three types of motion: if the true velocity is less than the mean velocity, it is called "retrograde" <note>original: "vakra-gati"</note>; if it is greater, it is called "fast" <note>original: "shighra-gati"</note>; and if they are equal, it is called "even" <note>original: "sama-gati"</note>.

### Simplified Correction of the Planets

<margin>Verse</margin>
> Subtract the planet from its apogee to find the anomaly. Multiply the base sine by thirty-one. Having added the degrees and divided by six for Mars and the other planets, perform the "slow-correction" <note>original: "mānda-karma"</note> as previously described. Then, for the "fast-correction" <note>original: "shīghra-karma"</note>, use the anomaly-viewing process. Based on the sine-total, multiply the minutes of the arc by the difference between sines, divide by the 360 of the circle, and add the result to the sine-total. This should be applied to the mean planet to find...

> ...and similarly. The mean planet is modified by the entire slow-correction <term>Manda-phala: the correction for the planet's elliptical orbit</term> and the fast-correction <term>Shighra-phala: the correction for the Earth's movement relative to the planet</term>. One should perform these corrections as previously mentioned; this method is in agreement with the venerable Aryabhata <note>Aryabhata (476–550 CE) was a foundational Indian mathematician-astronomer whose work, the Aryabhatiya, is referenced here as the authority.</note>.

Explanation: For the planets beginning with Mars <note>This refers to the five planets excluding the Sun and Moon: Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn</note>, first calculate the slow-correction <term>Manda-samskara</term> using the apogee, just as was done for the Sun and Moon. For the second step, calculate the fast-anomaly <term>Shighra-kendra</term>. Determine if the position falls in the half of the zodiac beginning with Cancer <note>original: "Karkyadi"</note> or the half beginning with Capricorn <note>original: "Makaradi"</note>. If it is in the Cancer-half, use the specific radius value for Cancer; if in the Capricorn-half, use the value for Capricorn. Follow the rest of the procedure used for the slow-correction. By applying these corrections in the established order, the true, visible positions <term>Sphuta</term> of Mars and the other planets are obtained. For Mercury and Venus, one must perform the four specific corrections starting with the fast-correction.

### Calculation of the True Daily Motion of Mars and Other Planets

> Verse: Multiply the mean daily motion <note>original: "madhya-bhukti"</note> by the third sine-difference of Cancer. Divide by 360 to find the result. Based on whether the anomaly is in the Cancer or Capricorn half, add or subtract this to find the "slow" motion <term>Manda-gati</term>. Subtract the anomaly's motion from the fast-motion. Multiply by the fast-sine-difference and divide by the degrees. If the result is positive, it is added to find the true motion <term>Sphuta-gati</term> of Mars and others. If the slow motion is greater than the fast-correction value, that planet certainly enters retrograde motion <term>Vakra-gati: appearing to move backward in the sky</term>.

Explanation: Multiply the mean daily motion of the planets by the third sine-difference of Cancer and divide the result by 360. Using this result, adjust the mean motion based on whether the planet is in the Cancer or Capricorn side of its orbit to find the "slow" motion <term>Manda-gati</term>. To find the motion of the anomaly <term>Kendra-gati</term>, subtract this slow motion from the fast motion. Multiply that result by the fast-sine-difference and divide by 360. If this calculation yields a positive value, add it to the slow motion. In the case of the fast-anomaly being in the Cancer half, subtract it to determine the fast motion. Finally, if the slow motion exceeds the fast correction, the planet is in retrograde <term>Vakra-gati</term>.

->Complete.<-

### Calculation of the Elapsed Days from the Start of the Aeon, Verified by Observation.

Verse: Multiply the years of the <term>Kalpa: A vast cosmic age or aeon in Hindu cosmology, often used as a starting point for astronomical calculations.</term> by 12, add the elapsed months...
<note>original Sanskrit: "కల్ప్యాబ్దార్క ౧౨ గుణోమాసయుక్తస్త్రిస్థా-" (kalpyābdārka 12 guṇo māsasuktas tristhā-). This verse explains how to convert years into months as part of the process to find the total days elapsed since the start of the current cosmic cycle.</note>

-> I have already written this verse and the calculation of the elapsed days previously. <-

### Calculation of the Mean Planets.

Verse: By adding the values of the positions starting from the first place in the <term>Ahargana: The total number of days elapsed from a fixed epoch to the current date, used to calculate planetary positions.</term>, and combining them with the previous epochal constant, the mean planet is found.
-> <note>original Sanskrit: "ఏకాదిస్థాన సంఖ్యానాపదకానామహర్గణే । సంయోజ్యాన్న- ధ్యఖేటస్స్యాత్ । పూర్వధ్రువసమన్వితః॥" (ekādisthāna saṃkhyānāpadakānāmahargaṇē | saṃyōjyānna- dhyakhēṭassyāt | pūrvadhruvasamanvitaḥ||)</note> <-

Meaning: In the calculation of the elapsed days, sum the specific planetary motions associated with the numerical places; by adding these and then incorporating the previous <term>Dhruva: A mathematical constant or "epochal value" representing a planet's position at a specific starting point in time.</term>, the <term>Mean Planet: The theoretical position of a planet if it moved at a uniform speed in a circular orbit, before applying corrections for its actual elliptical path.</term> is determined. One must then apply the <term>Deshantara Samskara: A correction applied to astronomical calculations to account for the difference in longitude between the prime meridian (historically Ujjain) and the observer's location.</term>

### Previous Epochal Constants (Dhruvas).

<note>The following verses use word-numerals (e.g., "Shiva" for 11, "Abhra" for 0) to provide the constant values for the planets.</note>

The total constant [for the Sun] is remembered as: Shiva (11), Go-tri (29), Abhra (30), and Rama-abhra-guna (30).
**11 | 29 | 30 | 30**

For the Moon: Rudra (11), Ashvi (2), Shaka (3), Veda (4), Kara (2), Shunya (0), Abdhi (4), and Shashi (1).
**11 | 23 | 24 | 40**

**11 | 6 | 12 | 18**

For the Quick One <note>Mercury's "Sighra" or rapid motion</note>: Dasa (10), Arka (12) <note>Adjusted to 15 in digits</note>, Netra (2), Abdhi (4), and Danta (32).
**10 | 15 | 42 | 32**

For Jupiter: Kha (0), Shara (5), Agni (3), Gaja (8), Rama (3), and Dvi-agni (32) <note>The digits 0 | 56 | 35 | 35 represent the standard constant used in the tables.</note>.
**0 | 56 | 35 | 35**

For Venus: Abhra (0), Nabhas (0), Veda (4), and Shad-veda (46).
**0 | 34 | 44 | 6**

For Saturn: Dasha (10), Akara (?), Shunya (0), and Kashta (?).

<page-num>95</page-num>
<header>Delight of Mathematics</header>

The epochal constant for the Moon’s apogee is 5,633 <gloss>5633</gloss> and for the Moon itself, the constant is 259.

The constant for <term>Rahu: The North Node of the Moon, considered a "shadow planet" in Indian astronomy.</term> is 5,252 <gloss>5252</gloss>; this must be subtracted from its own epochal constant. 610.

Meaning: For all planets starting from the Sun, the previous <term>Dhruvas: Mathematical constants representing a planet's position at a specific starting epoch.</term> are to be added; however, for Rahu alone, the value must be subtracted from the previous Dhruva.

### Calculation of the Sun.

<term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "అహర్గణఃఖాత్రి 70 హృతేనవర్జితః ఖరాంశురంశోది రయం ప్రజాయతే । అహర్గణ లోచన 2 సంగుణాచ్ఛరైః 5 హృతాది లిప్తాదిఫలేనవర్జితః॥" (ahargaṇaḥ khātri 70 hṛtēnavarjitaḥ kharāṃśuraṃśōdi rayaṃ prajāyatē | ahargaṇa lōcana 2 saṃguṇāccharaiḥ 5 hṛtādi liptādiphalēnavarjitaḥ||)</note> The total elapsed days divided by 70 and subtracted, yields the Sun's degrees. The total days multiplied by 2 and divided by 5 yields the minutes to be subtracted.

Meaning: Divide the <term>Ahargana: The total count of days elapsed from the start of the epoch to the desired date.</term> by 70; the resulting value in degrees should be subtracted from the Ahargana. Furthermore, multiply the Ahargana by 2 and divide by 5; the resulting <term>Liptas: Minutes of arc (1/60th of a degree).</term> should also be subtracted. Then, apply the previous epochal adjustment and the <term>Deshantara: A correction for the difference in longitude between the observer's location and the central meridian (typically Ujjain).</term> correction.

### Calculation of the Moon.

<term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "గుణహతోద్వార్ద్యుగణాద్వినాగా 82 హృత్ తశ్చచక్రా దిరయం ప్రజాయతే । వేదాగ్ని బాణప్త ఫలాంశ యుక్తపూ ర్వధ్రువాధ్యశ్చ నిశాకరస్స్యాత్ ॥" (guṇahatōdvārdyugaṇādvīnāgā 82 hṛt taścacakrā dirayaṃ prajāyatē | vēdāgni bāṇapta phalāṃśa yuktapū rvadhruvādhyaśca niśākarassyāt ||)</note> Multiply the day-count by 3 and divide by 82 to find the revolutions. Adding the degrees calculated from the 534 divisor and the epochal constant gives the position of the Moon.

Meaning: Multiply the day-count by 3 and keep two copies of this value. Divide the first copy by 82 to obtain the planet's position starting from revolutions. Divide the second copy by 534; the resulting degrees should be added. Finally, apply the previous epochal and longitudinal corrections.

### Periodic Correction for the Three-Year Cycle.

<term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "త్రిగుణాబ్ద్యాద్వితర్కాక్షి 262 హృతా లిప్తాన్వితోరవిః । భూ గ్నిదేవ 3330 హృతా లిప్తా యుతసంస్కృతచంద్రమాః॥" (triguṇābdyādvitarkākṣi 262 hṛtā liptānvitōraviḥ | bhū gnidēva 3330 hṛtā liptā yutasaṃskṛtacaṃdramāḥ||)</note> For the Sun, divide the cycle-years by 262 to find the minutes to be adjusted. For the Moon, divide by 3330 for its correction.

Meaning: For the Sun's position within the <term>Trigunabda: A specific cyclic period (literally 'three-multiplied years') used for long-term corrections in planetary models.</term> calculation, divide by 262; the resulting minutes are subtractive (negative). For the Moon, divide by 3330; the resulting minutes are additive (positive). I have already clearly explained the calculations for the Moon’s apogee and Rahu previously.

> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "అగజాననపద్మార్కం గజాననమహర్నిశం । అనేకదంతం భక్తానాం । మేకదంతముపాస్మహే॥" (agajānana-padmārkaṃ gajānanam-aharniśam | anēka-dantaṃ bhaktānāṃ | ēkadantam-upāsmahē ||)</note> **Day and night, we worship the elephant-faced one (Ganesha), who is like the Sun to the lotus-face of Goddess Parvati; the one who bestows many boons upon his devotees, the single-tusked Lord.**

For the solar and lunar eclipses, the mean planets of the *Delight of Mathematics* must be calculated according to <term>Drksiddha: Calculations that match actual observational reality.</term> as described hereafter. For <term>Rahu: The North Node of the Moon, often called a "shadow planet."</term> specifically, the correction should be performed using the "triple-year" (Trigunabda) adjustment method.

### # Chapter on Planetary Corrections

> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "శశిచత్వారిరూపోన ౧౪౩౧ శకాబ్దాంఖాద్రి ౭౦ సంహృతాత్ అష్టాంబరేషు ౫౦౮ హృతాలబ్ధం కలికాదిరవిద్ధనం॥" (śaśi-catvāri-rūpōna 1431 śakābdāṅkhādri 70 saṃhṛtāt | aṣṭāmbarēṣu 508 hṛtā-labdhaṃ kalikādi-raviddhanam ||)</note> **Subtract 1431 from the elapsed years of the Salivahana Saka era. Multiply the remainder by 70 and divide by 508. The resulting value in minutes of arc is to be added to the Sun's position.**

Meaning: In the elapsed years of the <term>Salivahana Saka: A historical Indian calendar era beginning in 78 CE.</term>, subtract 1431. Multiply the remainder by 70 and divide by 508. The resulting <term>Kala: A unit of angular measurement equivalent to one minute of arc (1/60th of a degree).</term> should be treated as <term>Dhana: An additive correction or "positive" value in mathematical operations.</term> for the Sun.

> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "అద్రిరూపాష్టరూపోన ౧౮౧౭ శకాబ్దాన్ ఖాగ్ని ౩౦ సంగుణాత్ । షణ్ణేత్రాక్షి ౨౨౬ హృతాలబ్ధం । కలికాద్యందువిశోధనం॥" (adri-rūpāṣṭa-rūpōna 1817 śakābdān khāgni 30 saṃguṇāt | ṣaṇṇētrākṣi 226 hṛtā-labdhaṃ | kalikādyandu-viśōdhanaṃ ||)</note> **Subtract 1817 from the Salivahana Saka years. Multiply by 30 and divide by 226. The resulting value in minutes of arc is to be subtracted from the Moon's position.**

Meaning: Subtract 1817 from the elapsed Salivahana Saka years. Multiply the remainder by 30 and divide by 226. The resulting value in minutes is an additive correction <note>The Telugu prose says "Dhana" (add), but the Sanskrit verse says "Vishodhanam" (subtract). In astronomical tables of this period, these corrections often flipped signs depending on the specific epoch used.</note> for the Moon.

> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "సప్తాంశాణౌ ౪౭౭ శకైర్న్యూనా శేషాదష్టాదశై ౧౮ గుణణాత్ ఖభాగ ౫౦ భక్త లిపాఢ్యం చంద్రతుంగం విశోధయేత్ ॥" (saptāṃśāṇau 477 śakair-nyūnā śēṣād-aṣṭādaśai 18 guṇanāt khabhāga 50 bhakta liptāḍhyaṃ candratuṅgaṃ viśōdhayēt ||)</note> **Subtract 477 from the Saka years. Multiply the remainder by 18 and divide by 50. The resulting minutes of arc should be subtracted from the Moon’s Apogee.**

Meaning: Subtract 477 from the elapsed Salivahana Saka years. Multiply the remainder by 18 and divide by 50. The resulting value in minutes should be subtracted from the <term>Chandrochcha: The Moon's Apogee, the point in its orbit furthest from the Earth.</term>

> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "నేత్రాగ్నిమునిభి ౭౩౨ ర్యూన శకాబ్దాత్ భాష ౪౦ సంగుణాత్ । తిథివేద ౪౫ హృతాలబ్ధం । కలాద్యంశోధయే త్క్రమాత్॥" (nētrāgni-munibhi 732 ryūna śakābdāt bhāṣa 40 saṃguṇāt | tithivēda 45 hṛtā-labdhaṃ | kalādyaṃśōdhayēt kramāt ||)</note> **Subtract 732 from the Saka years. Multiply by 40 and divide by 45. The resulting value in minutes should be subtracted in the proper order...**

<summary>This page provides specific mathematical formulas (Beeja) to correct the mean positions of the Sun, Moon, and Moon's Apogee based on different historical epochs. These corrections ensure the calculations remain accurate for observational astronomy (Drksiddha).</summary>
<keywords>planetary corrections, Beejadhikara, Salivahana Saka, Sun calculation, Moon calculation, Chandrochcha, Ganesha invocation, Indian astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical operations for planetary positions. The previous page established the correction for the Sun's position based on the Salivahana Saka year 1431. This page introduces an alternative epoch (Saka 730) and provides a specific interpolation table for correcting the Sun's position in seconds of arc (Vikalas) based on its longitudinal degrees.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>
->## Delight of Mathematics<-

Meaning: From the elapsed years of the <term>Salivahana Saka: A historical Indian calendar era beginning in 78 CE.</term>, subtract 730. Multiply the remainder by 80 and divide the result by 870. The resulting value, measured in <term>Kala: Minutes of arc (1/60th of a degree).</term>, must be subtracted from the calculation.

> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "ద్విసహస్రాధికశకం । సబీజంతదవాప్యత్యేవ్యత్యయే నసం స్కుర్యా । సబీజఫలమశ్నుతే॥" (dvisahasrādhika-śakaṃ | sabījaṃ-tadavāpyatyē-vyatyayē na-saṃskuryā | sabīja-phalam-aśnutē ||)</note> **When the Saka era exceeds two thousand, one obtains the result with the "seed correction" (Beeja). If the conditions are reversed, apply the correction accordingly to attain the accurate "seeded" result.** The meaning is clear.

# True Positions of the Sun and Moon
## Procedure for Correcting Seconds of Arc at Sunrise

> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "దృక్సిద్ధార్కాంశదశభి ౧౦ భక్తాలబ్ధం భవేత్తధాగత ఖండాంతరహతం । శేషందశహృతంఫలంగతఖండై ధనర్పం స్వద్వితీపాత్యకఖండక॥" (dṛksiddhārkāṃśa-daśabhi 10 bhaktā-labdhaṃ bhavēt-tathāgata khaṇḍāntara-hataṃ | śēṣaṃ-daśa-hṛtaṃ-phalaṃ-gata-khaṇḍai dhanarpaṃ svadvitīpātyaka-khaṇḍaka ||)</note> **Divide the degrees of the Sun (calculated by observation) by 10. The quotient indicates the elapsed <gloss>10</gloss> segment. Multiply the remainder by the difference between segments. Divide that by ten; the result is applied as a positive or negative correction to the segment values.**

Meaning: Divide the degrees of the mean Sun (after applying the <term>Beeja-phala: A "seed" correction factor used to align theoretical calculations with observed planetary positions.</term>) by 10. Note the seconds of arc (<term>Vikala: Seconds of arc (1/60th of a minute).</term>) corresponding to that quotient in the table written below. Multiply the remaining degrees by the difference between the current segment and the next segment. Divide this by 10. The resulting seconds of arc should be applied to the table's value by either adding or subtracting. Specifically, use addition (<term>Dhana: Positive/Add.</term>) for increasing values in the table and subtraction (<term>Runa: Negative/Subtract.</term>) for decreasing values, following the mathematical principle of positive and negative adjustments.

---

| | | | | | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 15 | + 4 | 75 | + 6 | 135 | - 4 |
| 30 | + 1 | 90 | + 3 | 150 | + 10 |
| 45 | + 2 | 105 | + 2 | 165 | + 8 |
| 60 | + 6 | 120 | - 1 | 180 | + 9 |
| 75 | - 6 | 135 | - 2 | 195 | + 6 |
| 90 | - 4 | 150 | - 3 | 210 | + 5 |

This correction accounts for the difference between mean and true sunrise.</meta>

->**Correction of Seconds of Arc**<-

> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "అర్కో దయాని వికలా | గుణితామధ్యభు క్తయః | షష్టి ౬౦ భక్తాస్తువికలా | ధనర్ణ మధ్యా ఖేట యోః||" (arkō-dayāni vikalā | guṇitā-madhya-bhuktayaḥ | ṣaṣṭi 60 bhaktāstu vikalā dhanarṇa madhyā khēṭayōḥ ||)</note> **The seconds of arc at sunrise, when multiplied by the mean daily motions and divided by sixty (60), yield seconds of arc that are to be added to or subtracted from the mean positions of the planets.**

> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "ఖాది శూన్యాంగ ౬౦ పర్యంత భాగారి రుణ సంజ్ఞ కాః | తదా దిఖేంద్ర ౧౪౦ పర్యంతం ధనభాగాది సంజ్ఞ కాః | తధా ఖ తత్వ ౨౫౦ పర్యంతం రుణ భాగాది సంజ్ఞ కాః తధా షష్ట్య గ్ని ౩౬౦ పర్యంతం ధనభాగాది సంజ్ఞ కాః||" (khādi śūnyāṅga 60 paryanta bhāgāri ruṇa saṃjñakāḥ | tadā-dikhēndra 140 paryantaṃ dhanabhāgādi saṃjñakāḥ | tathā kha-tattva 250 paryantaṃ ruṇa bhāgādi saṃjñakāḥ tathā ṣaṣṭy-agni 360 paryantaṃ dhanabhāgādi saṃjñakāḥ ||)</note> **Up to sixty (60) degrees <note>literal: "kha-adi-shunnyanga," where 'kha' is 0 and 'anga' is 6</note> the designation is negative. From there up to one hundred and forty (140) degrees, the designation is positive. Up to two hundred and fifty (250) degrees <note>literal: "kha-tattva," where 'kha' is 0 and 'tattva' is 25</note> the designation is negative. And up to three hundred and sixty (360) degrees <note>literal: "shashty-agni," where 'shashti' is 60 and 'agni' is 3 (hundreds)</note> the designation is positive.**

Explanation: True sunrise may occur either before or after mean sunrise. Therefore, the difference in seconds (<term>Vikalas: Seconds of arc (1/60th of a minute).</term>) between these two must be multiplied by the mean daily motions of the respective planets and divided by 60. The resulting seconds of arc should be applied to the mean positions. Using the mean Sun as the center (anomaly), one must understand the application as follows:
- Up to 60 degrees: <term>Negative (Runa): Values to be subtracted from the calculation.</term>
- From 60 up to 140 degrees: <term>Positive (Dhana): Values to be added to the calculation.</term>
- From 140 up to 250 degrees: Negative (Runa).
- From 250 up to 360 degrees: Positive (Dhana).
I have indicated this correction because it is necessary for all planets and is particularly vital for the calculation of solar eclipses.

<vocab>Delight of Calculation (Ganaka Ranjani), Correction of Seconds (Vikalasamskaramu), Mean Sunrise (Madhyasuryodayamu), True Sunrise (Sphutasuryodayamu), Mean Daily Motion (Madhyagathulu), Additive/Subtractive (Dhanarna), Negative sign (Runasamjna), Positive sign (Dhanasamjna), Zero-and-Six [60] (Khadi Shunnyanga)</vocab>

It provides a specific table of values and defines the longitudinal ranges (0-360 degrees) where these corrections should be added or subtracted.</summary>

### Calculation of the Solar Sine-Correction (Bhujaphala).
In the *Chapter on True Positions* <note>Sphuta Adhyaya</note> approved by **Aryabhata**, I have established thirty <term>sine-proportions<gloss>jyopapattis</gloss></term> for the Sun and the Moon. One must calculate the <term>sine-correction<gloss>bhujaphala; the difference between the mean and true position of a planet</gloss></term> using those proportions to determine the true positions.

-> The first correction generated from the sine-correction: <-

> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "సూర్యబాహుఫలాద్విస్తా | స్థితి ౧౫ ఘ్నీ స్థితి base ౫౪ | హృత్కలా | మునిసప్త ౭౭ హృతాలబ్ధం | చంద్రదేవత్య మేవచ | తుంగేతురవివత్కార్య మాద్య సంస్కార ఈదృశః||" (sūryabāhuphalādvistā | sthiti 15 ghnī sthiti 154 | hṛtkalā | munisapta 77 hṛtālabdhaṃ | candradēvatya mēvaca | tuṅgētu ravivatkārya mādya saṃskāra īdṛśaḥ ||)</note> **Multiply the solar sine-correction by fifteen (15) and place it in two positions. Divide the first by one hundred and fifty-four (154) to find the minutes of arc; this result should be applied to the Moon—if the solar correction was negative, add it, and if it was positive, subtract it. Divide the second position by seventy-seven (77) <note>literal: "muni-sapta," where muni is 7 and sapta is 7</note>; the resulting minutes of arc should be applied to the Lunar apogee just as they were for the Sun. This is the first correction.**

**Interpretation:** Multiply the solar sine-correction (in minutes) by 15. For the first part, divide by 154. The resulting value is to be applied to the Moon's position with the opposite sign of the Sun's correction (if the Sun is "minus," the Moon is "plus"). For the second part, divide by 77; this result is used to correct the <term>Lunar apogee<gloss>chandrochcha; the point where the moon is furthest from Earth</gloss></term> following the same logic as the solar correction.

### The Second Correction.
> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "తత్తుంగహీనంచంద్రం స్వార్కేంద్రంవ్యర్కేందుసాధితం వ్య | కే౦దుద్విగుణీకృత్య | చంద్రకేంద్రోన కేంద్రజందశభి ౧౦ | భాజితఫలంగతఖండాభవంతిచ | ఖండాంతరేణభాగాది | హత్యాదశ ౧౦ హృతాత్యలం | ఖండేయోజ్యం ధనరుణం | స్వార్కేర్మేషాదిగేసతి | తత్తే కేంద్రానాన్నిందజఫలసంస్కృ | రా త్పూర్వవత్సదా||" (tattuṅgahīnañcandraṃ svārkēndraṃvyarkēndusādhitaṃ vya | kēndudviguṇīkṛtya | candrakēndrōna kēndrajaṃdaśabhi 10 | bhājitapalaṃgatakhaṇḍābhavaṃtica | khaṇḍāntarēṇabhāgādi | hatyādaśa 10 hṛtātyalaṃ | khaṇḍēyōjyaṃ dhanaruṇaṃ | svārkērmēṣādigēsati | tattē kēndrānānnindajaphalasaṃskṛ | rā tpūrvavatsadā ||)</note> **Subtract the apogee from the Moon to find the anomaly. Calculate the distance between the Sun and Moon (elongation), then double it. Subtract the lunar anomaly from this doubled distance to get the refined anomaly. Divide this by ten (10) to find the elapsed segments. Multiply the remainder by the difference between segments and divide by ten. Apply this to the segment value as positive or negative depending on whether the center is in the signs of Aries or Libra. Apply this final correction as before.**

**Interpretation:** Subtract the <term>apogee<gloss>tunga</gloss></term> from the Moon's position (corrected in the first step). Calculate the <term>elongation<gloss>vyarkendu; Moon minus Sun</gloss></term> and double that value. From this doubled value, subtract the Moon's <term>anomaly<gloss>kendra</gloss></term> to find the "refined anomaly." Divide this by 10 to determine which <term>segment<gloss>khanda; a division of the sine table</gloss></term> to use. Multiply the remaining degrees/minutes by the difference between that segment and the next, then divide by 10. This result, once combined with the segment's base value, is added to or subtracted from the Moon's position based on whether the anomaly falls in the half of the zodiac starting with Aries or Libra <note>This identifies which quadrant the moon is in to determine the mathematical sign</note>. After <unclear>calculating the result</unclear>, add or subtract the minutes from the center as previously instructed.

<summary>This page describes two specific mathematical corrections (Samskaras) for the Moon's position based on its relationship to the Sun and the lunar apogee, following the Aryabhata tradition of astronomy.</summary>
<keywords>Aryabhata, Bhujaphala, Solar Sine-Correction, Lunar Apogee, Vyarkendu, Equation of Center, Hindu Astronomy, Karanaranjani</keywords>

<page-num>100</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Calculation</header>

Using that <term>center<gloss>kendra</gloss></term>, calculate the <term>sine-correction<gloss>bhujaphala</gloss></term> based on 318 <term>sine-proportions<gloss>jyavatakas</gloss></term>. As stated previously, apply a negative correction for the signs starting with Aries and a positive correction for the signs starting with Libra. The result of this calculation is the <term>slowly-corrected moon<gloss>manda sphuta chandra; the moon's position after accounting for the primary orbital anomaly</gloss></term>.

## The Third Correction

> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "శూన్యాష్ట 180 రూపలిప్తాంతమేకభాగకలాద్వయం | చంద్రార్కోనం భుజస్సాధ్యం మేషేర్లక్షాక గేధనం||" (śūnyāṣṭa 180 rūpaliptāntamēkabhāgakalādvayaṃ | candrārkōnaṃ bhujassādhyaṃ mēṣērlakṣāka gēdhanaṃ||)</note> **One hundred and eighty (180) minutes are established for the sine calculation. For every one (1) degree, the value is two (2) minutes. Subtract the Sun’s position from the Moon's to find the center and calculate the corresponding side. For every degree of that side, apply two minutes of arc according to the rule of three. If the Sun-subtracted Moon center is in the signs starting with Aries, the result is negative; if it is in the signs starting with Libra, it is positive.**

**Interpretation:** One hundred and eighty minutes of the sine-table have been established. At a rate of 2 minutes per 1 degree, find the <term>center<gloss>kendra</gloss></term> by subtracting the Sun’s longitude from the Moon’s longitude. Then, using the <term>rule of three<gloss>trairāśika; a mathematical method to find a fourth value from three known values</gloss></term>, determine the resulting minutes of arc for those degrees. If this center (the Moon minus the Sun) falls within the six signs from Aries through Virgo, it is a negative correction; if it falls within the six signs from Libra through Pisces, it is a positive correction.

## The Fourth Correction

> <term>Verse:</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "వ్యక్కేందు భాగాన భుశరాబ్ధి 450 భక్తా | లబ్ధ్యంత ఖండాతరమేవగుణ్యం ఖేష్యబ్దిత్ర కంగతఖండమేళనాత్ కలాఫలం కేంద్రమయుగ్మయుగ్మగే | ధనం భ వేదోజపదేద కేంద్రే యుగ్మే ఋణానక్రకుళీరగే క్రమాత్ ||" (vyakkēndu bhāgāna bhuśarābdhi 450 bhaktā | labdhyanta khaṇḍātaramevaguṇyaṃ khēṣyabditra kaṃ gatakhaṇḍamēḷanāt kalāphalaṃ kēndramayugmayugmagē | dhanaṃ bhavēdōjapadēda kēndrē yugmē ṛṇānakrakuḷīragē kramāt ||)</note> **Divide the degrees of the Sun-subtracted Moon by four hundred and fifty (450). Multiply the quotient's remainder by the difference between the segments. By adding the traversed segment, determine the correction in minutes. If the center is in an odd quadrant, the result is positive; if in an even quadrant, it is negative, applied in sequence from Capricorn and Cancer.**

**Interpretation:** Divide the degrees of the Sun-subtracted Moon <note>referred to as "vyakkendu"</note> by 450. Determine the corresponding zodiacal position (such as Capricorn) based on the result. Identify the appropriate mathematical <term>segment<gloss>khaṇḍa</gloss></term> and multiply the remaining degrees by the difference between these segments. Divide this value by 450, convert it into minutes, and add it to the <term>traversed segment<gloss>gatakhaṇḍa</gloss></term>. This final correction should be added to the Moon's position if the center is in an <term>odd quadrant<gloss>oja pada; the 1st or 3rd quadrants of the circle</gloss></term> and subtracted if it is in an <term>even quadrant<gloss>yugma pada; the 2nd or 4th quadrants</gloss></term>.

<summary>This page describes the third and fourth mathematical corrections (Samskaras) for the Moon's position, which involve calculating the angular distance between the Sun and Moon to account for complex gravitational interactions.</summary>
<keywords>lunar correction, Samskara, sine-correction, Bhujaphala, Sun-Moon distance, Hindu astronomy, rule of three, quadrants, traversed segment</keywords>

# The Fifth Correction

> **Verse:** <note>original Sanskrit: "సూర్యబాహుకలాభ్యస్తా దశ 10 శూన్యాగ్ని సోమ 130 హృత్ కలాదిశఫలంభానౌ । వ్యత్యయేస్త వ సమ్మటం॥" (sūryabāhukalābhyastā daśa 10 śūnyāgni sōma 130 hṛt kalādiśaphalaṃbhānau | vyatyayēsta va sammaṭaṃ||)</note> **Multiply the solar sine-correction by ten (10) and divide by one hundred and thirty (130). This result, in minutes and seconds, is applied to the Sun's position; in the reverse case, it is accepted as the correction.**

**Explanation:** Multiply the <term>solar sine-correction<gloss>sūryabhujāphala; the displacement of the sun from its mean position</gloss></term> by 10 and divide the product by 130. The resulting <term>minute-based value<gloss>kalādiphalalamu</gloss></term> must be applied to the position of the <term>node<gloss>rāhu; the ascending node of the moon</gloss></term>. If the solar sine-correction was negative, it is added; if it was positive, it is subtracted. This calculation results in the <term>true node<gloss>sphuṭa rāhu</gloss></term>.

---

# Calculating Celestial Latitude

> **Verse:** <note>original Sanskrit: "పాతోనకేంద్రభుజభాగశ రై 5 విభక్తా । విక్షేపయోః పతక సంఖ్యముపై తీసామ్మె । శిష్టానిష్యంతర నిఘ్నమధై 5 విభ జయేదా ప్తఫలయుతాన్యు విక్షేపలి ప్తకుధ మేషతులామదక్ష 3॥" (pātonakēndrabhujabhāgaśa rai 5 vibhaktā | vikṣēpayōḥ pataka saṃkhyamupai tīsāmmē | śiṣṭāniṣyantara nighnamadhai 5 vibha jayēdā ptaphalayutānyu vikṣēpaliptakudha mēṣatulāmadakṣa 3||)</note> **The degrees of the argument of latitude are divided by five (5). This gives the index for the table of latitudes. Multiply the remainder by the difference between entries and divide by five (5) again. Adding this to the base value gives the celestial latitude. From Aries it is North, and from Libra it is South (3).**

**Explanation:** Subtract the <term>true node<gloss>sphuṭa rāhu</gloss></term> from the Moon's position as adjusted by the <term>fourth correction<gloss>caturthaphalasaṃskāra</gloss></term>. The result is the <term>argument of latitude<gloss>pātonakēndram; the angular distance from the node</gloss></term>. Calculate the sine-segment (bhujā) for this value and divide those degrees by 5. Use the resulting number to locate the corresponding <term>sine-table factor<gloss>patakam</gloss></term>. Multiply the remaining fraction by the difference between the current and next table entries, then divide by 5. When this final result is added to the table factor, you obtain the <term>mean celestial latitude in minutes<gloss>madhyavikṣēpaliptādi</gloss></term>. If the argument of latitude begins with <term>Aries<gloss>mēṣādi</gloss></term>, the latitude is North; if it begins with <term>Libra<gloss>tulādi</gloss></term>, it is South.

<summary>This page describes the 'Fifth Correction,' which adjusts the position of the Lunar Node (Rahu) based on solar influence, and outlines the mathematical process for calculating the Moon's celestial latitude (Vikshepa).</summary>
<keywords>Fifth Correction, True Rahu, Celestial Latitude, Vikshepa, Argument of Latitude, Solar Sine-Correction, Lunar Node, Hindu Astronomy</keywords>

### Calculation of the True Celestial Latitude

<margin>Verse</margin>
> <note>original Sanskrit: "వ్యర్కేందౌద్విగుణీకృత్య । పాతోనేందుం విశోధయేత్ । తద్భుజజ్యాం విహ్ని 3 నిఘ్నాం హృతాషడైదశంకరా ౧౧ రై దిగైక్యమధ్యవిక్షేపోయుక్తా భిన్నదిశోవియుక్ । స్ఫుటవిక్షేపలిప్తాస్స్యుః । సాధ్యోన్యాససోక్తవత్ ॥" (vyarkēndaudviguṇīkṛtya | pātōnēnduṃ viśōdhayēt | tadbhujajyāṃ vihni 3 nighnāṃ hṛtāṣaḍaidaśaṅkarā 11 rai digaikyamadhyavikṣēpōyuktā bhinnadiśōviyuk | sphuṭavikṣēpaliptāssyuḥ | sādhyōnyāsasōktavat ||)</note> **Subtract the Sun's position from the Moon's and double the result. From this, subtract the position of the Moon-minus-Node. Take the sine of that resulting arc, multiply it by three (3), and divide by eleven (11). If the resulting value and the mean latitude are in the same direction, add them; if they are in opposite directions, subtract them. This gives the True Celestial Latitude in minutes. The rest of the calculation follows the previously stated method.**

**Explanation:** Double the <term>elongation<gloss>vyarkēndu; the angular distance between the Moon and the Sun</gloss></term> and subtract from it the <term>argument of latitude<gloss>pātōnakēndramu; the Moon's position relative to its node</gloss></term>. Calculate the <term>sine of the arc<gloss>bhujajyā</gloss></term> for this value. Multiply this sine by 3 and divide by 11 <note>The Sanskrit verse uses "Sankara" as a code for the number 11</note>. The resulting <term>value in minutes<gloss>kalādi phalamu</gloss></term> should be combined with the <term>mean latitude<gloss>madhyavikṣēpamu</gloss></term>. If both are in the same celestial direction (North or South), add them; if they are in different directions, subtract the smaller from the larger. The result is the <term>True Celestial Latitude<gloss>sphuṭavikṣēpamu</gloss></term>. Rather than calculating this every time, the tables for latitude and declination were previously written in the chapters on True Positions. One should verify the values against those.

->Latitude Tables<-

| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1 | 0 | 9 | 40 | 4 27 | 10 | 3 | 56 | 24 | 4 16 | 30 |
| 2 | 0 | 53 | 39 | 4 26 | 6 | 11 | 4 | 53 | 44 | 4 14 | 24 |
| 3 | 10 | 19 | 45 | 4 24 | 37 | 12 | 4 | 22 | 56 | 4 12 | 10 |
| 4 | 14 | 41 | 10 | 4 21 | 34 | 13 | 4 | 34 | 40 | 4 10 | 40 |
| 5 | 17 | 6 | 59 | 4 17 | 22 | 14 | 4 | 41 | 14 | 4 7 | 59 |

It includes a reference table for latitude values to simplify these complex astronomical adjustments.</summary>

### Calculation of Celestial Latitude

> <note>original Sanskrit: "పాతోనచంద్రబాహుభ్యాం నాగవహ్ని 38 హతాత్కలాః కేంద్రవశాదశతః స్యుస్తాః స్వాద్విక్షేపకలా_స్తాః॥" (pātōnacandrabāhubhyāṃ nāgavahni 38 hatātkalāḥ kēndravaśādaśataḥ syustāḥ svādvikṣēpakalā_stāḥ||)</note> **Multiply the sine of the Moon-minus-Node by thirty-eight (38). Based on the center, the resulting minutes are the celestial latitude.**

**Explanation:** Take the <term>argument of latitude<gloss>pātōnacandra kēndramu; the angular distance of the Moon from its ascending node</gloss></term> as the base arc. Using the <term>standard sine table<gloss>mānasa-jīva; a specific set of sine values used in this tradition</gloss></term>, find the <term>sine of the arc<gloss>bhujajyā</gloss></term>. Multiply this sine by 38 <note>The verse uses the code "Naga-vahni": Naga = 8, Vahni = 3, read right-to-left as 38</note>. The resulting value in <term>minutes of arc<gloss>kalā</gloss></term> is the latitude. This is the <term>True Celestial Latitude<gloss>sphuṭa-vikṣēpamu</gloss></term>. Both values should be verified.

-> Regarding the fifth refinement of the Moon, the sine of the Moon-minus-Node is used. <-

> <note>original Sanskrit: "పాతోనచంద్రబాహుభ్యాం | కోటిజ్యాంచ హతాత్పునః | ఉద్విక్షేపోత్క_మజ్యఘ్నం | ఇష్టవిక్షేపకోటిహృత్ | త్రిభజ్యయాహరేల్లబ్ధం | కలాదిఫలమీరితం పాతోనేందోః పాదేచ | రుణముగ్మపదేధనం॥" (pātōnacandrabāhubhyāṃ | kōṭijyāṃca hatātpunah | udvikṣēpōtka_majyaghnaṃ | iṣṭavikṣēpakōṭihṛt | tribhajyayāharēllabdhaṃ | kalādiphalāmīritaṃ pātōnēndōḥ pādēca | ruṇamugmapadēdhanaṃ||)</note> **Multiply the sine of the Moon-minus-Node by its cosine. Multiply that result by the versed sine of the maximum latitude. Divide by the cosine of the desired latitude and then by the radius. In odd quadrants, subtract the resulting minutes; in even quadrants, add them.**

**Explanation:** Multiply the <term>sine<gloss>bhujajyā</gloss></term> of the Moon-minus-Node by the <term>cosine<gloss>kōṭijyā</gloss></term> of the Moon-minus-Node. The <term>maximum celestial latitude<gloss>parama-vikṣēpamu</gloss></term> is 309 minutes. The <term>versed sine<gloss>utkramajyā</gloss></term> for this value is 14. Multiply by this value (14). Divide the result by the cosine of the <term>desired latitude<gloss>iṣṭa-vikṣēpa</gloss></term> (which is the True Latitude calculated above). Then, divide that result by the <term>radius<gloss>trijyā; usually 3438 in this system</gloss></term>. When the Moon-minus-Node is in an <term>odd quadrant<gloss>oja-padamu; 1st or 3rd quadrants</gloss></term>, subtract this result from the Moon's longitude corrected by the fourth refinement. When it is in an <term>even quadrant<gloss>yugma-padamu; 2nd or 4th quadrants</gloss></term>, add it. This produces the <term>Fifth True Moon<gloss>pañca-sphuṭa-candra</gloss></term>.

> <note>original Sanskrit: "దృక్సిద్ధేందుస్ఫుటగతిః పూర్వాపరభగాంతరం" (dṛksiddhēndusphuṭagatiḥ pūrvāparabhāgāntaraṃ)</note> **The difference between the successive positions of the Moon is known as its True Motion.**

**Explanation:** The difference between the True Longitudes of the planet at two successive times (east to west) is understood as the <term>True Motion<gloss>sphuṭa-gati</gloss></term>.

-> Example Calculation <-

The <term>argument of latitude<gloss>pātōnacandra kēndramu</gloss></term> is 6 signs, 5 degrees, 0 minutes, 0 seconds.
The base arc (sine part) is 0 signs, 5 degrees. The remaining (cosine part) is 5 signs, 25 degrees.
The <term>sine<gloss>bhujajyā</gloss></term> is 300.0. The <term>cosine<gloss>kōṭijyā</gloss></term> is 3424.40.
Multiplying the sine (300) by the cosine (3424.40) gives 1,140,000.
Multiplying this by 14 (the versed sine) gives 15,960,000.
Dividing this by the <term>radius<gloss>trijyā</gloss></term> gives 4683.0.
Dividing this by the cosine of the True Latitude (which is 30.0, its cosine being 3437.30), the resulting minutes are 1 minute and 23 seconds (the remainder is discarded).
Since it is in an odd quadrant, this value is subtracted from the Moon's position. By following this method, the <term>Fifth True Moon<gloss>pañca-sphuṭa-candra</gloss></term> is obtained.

<summary>This page describes the calculation of the Moon's celestial latitude (Vikshepa) and the fifth mathematical correction (Pancha-sphuta) to its position. It provides specific formulas using sines, cosines, and versed sines, followed by a step-by-step numerical example.</summary>
<keywords>Celestial Latitude, Vikshepa, Sine (Bhujajya), Cosine (Kotijya), Versed Sine (Utkramajya), Moon's Node, Pancha-sphuta, True Motion (Sphuta Gati), Odd and Even Quadrants</keywords>

### Calculation of Corrections for Observed Positions

> <note>original Sanskrit: "కల్ప్యబ్దా షష్టికృత్పోన 3100 శేషంశరహతైర్యుతం భుశు న్యాగ్ధేద్ధైః 7100 ఖషడ్వేదైః 420 గగనాంబరషోడశైః 1600 శూన్యాబ్ధిపావకైః 340 ఖాబ్ధివార్ధి 440 భక్తంకలా దికం గురోర్ధనంచదా కార్యమితరేషాంతృణ భవేత్ ॥" (kalpyabdā ṣaṣṭikṛtpōna 3100 śēṣaṃśarahatairyutaṃ bhuśu nyāgdhēddhaiḥ 7100 khaṣadvēdaiḥ 420 gaganāmbaraṣōdaśaiḥ 1600 śūnyābdhipāvakaiḥ 340 khābdhivārdhi 440 bhaktaṃkalā dikaṃ gurōrdhanaṃcadā kāryamitarēṣāntṛṇa bhavēt ||)</note> **Subtract 3100 from the elapsed years of the Kalpa. Multiply the remainder by five. Divide the result by the following divisors: 7100, 420, 1600, 340, and 440. The resulting minutes of arc are to be added to Jupiter and subtracted from the others.**

**Explanation:** To find the observational correction, take the total years elapsed in the current <term>Kalpa<gloss>a vast cosmic cycle of time in Hindu cosmology</gloss></term> and subtract 3100 years <note>This likely aligns the calculation with the start of the Kali Yuga era</note>. Multiply this remainder by 5. Then, divide this value by the specific divisors assigned to the planets: 7100 for Mars, 420 for Mercury, 1600 for Jupiter, 340 for Venus, and 440 for Saturn. The result in <term>minutes of arc<gloss>kalā</gloss></term> should be treated as a <term>positive correction<gloss>guru-dhana; addition</gloss></term> for Jupiter, and a <term>negative correction<gloss>ṛṇa; subtraction</gloss></term> for the other planets.

### Manda Equations

> <note>original Sanskrit: "దృగ్గేందుగజభూవార్ధి ౨.౧౭|౪౮ సూర్యమాందధ్యవ సదా బాణాంకేందుశశాంకాగ్ని నహరంబుధగురోర్ధ్వేనం ౫.౧౫ ౩౦.౧౪ అష్టాగగ శానబాహ్వభ్రభవా సూర్య సుతస్యతు ౫.౬ ఏవక్త..." (dr̥ggēndugajabhūvārdhi 2.17|48 sūryamāndadhyava sadā bāṇāṅkēnduśaśāṅkāgni naharambudhagurōrdhvēnaṃ 5.15 30.14 aṣṭāgaga śānabāhvabhrabhavā sūrya sutasyatu 5.16 ēvakta...)</note> **The maximum equation of center values are: 2° 17' 48" for the Sun; 5° 15' and 30' 14" for Mercury and Jupiter; and 5° 16' for the son of the Sun (Saturn)...**

<note>These values represent the maximum 'Manda-phala', which accounts for the planet's elliptical orbit by correcting the mean position to the true position.</note>

### Refinement of the Manda Equations

> <note>original Sanskrit: "భూలోచనాగ్ని 1330 చంద్రాదశకాతూసూర్యన్మఖుది భూరి 120 గురోర్వేదేవ 54 శాస్త్రవట్నన్నైః 22 గు నితొత్తుత్తిః 1 ఋతుసపాప్త 52 శూన్యాభ్ర బాణ 700 బ్రేమభుజో 380 ధృతాతౌ కలాదికం ఫలంయోజ 0 ముచ్చే ర్బైర్వ్యకసారిశా॥" (bhūlōcanāgni 1330 candrādaśakātūsūryanmakhudi bhūri 120 gurōrvēdēva 54 śāstravatnannaiḥ 22 gu nitōttuttiḥ 1 r̥tusapāpta 52 śūnyābhra bāṇa 700 brēmabhujō 380 dhr̥tātau kalādikaṃ phalaṃyōja 0 muccē rbairvyakasāriśā||)</note> **Subtract 1330 from the Shaka year. Multiply the remainder by 120 for the Sun, 54 for Jupiter, and 22 for Saturn. Divide these by 52 for the Sun, 700 for Jupiter, and 380 for Saturn. The result in minutes is the correction to be applied.**

**Explanation:** This is a secular correction based on the <term>Shaka era<gloss>Śālivāhana śaka; an epoch starting in 78 CE</gloss></term>.
1. Take the current Shaka year and subtract 1330.
2. For the **Sun**: Multiply the remainder by 120 and divide by 52.
3. For **Jupiter**: Multiply the remainder by 54 and divide by 700.
4. For **Saturn**: Multiply the remainder by 22 and divide by 380.
The resulting <term>refined value<gloss>māndavēdha-phalamu</gloss></term> should be added to the planetary positions.

### Another Method

> <note>original Sanskrit: "గేష్వతలోచనాంకేందు నవాబ్ధి మనుసంజ్ఞితం ౧.౧౫ ౫.౧౪" (gēṣvatalōcanāṅkēndu navābdhi manusaṃjñitaṃ 1.15 5.14)</note> **[Values for another calculation method:] 1.15 and 5.14.**

# For Saturn, the multipliers are 323,833 regarding the positions of the nodes.
## Correction.

> <note>original Sanskrit: "భులోచనాగ్ని చంద్రోన ౧౩౨౧ శాకాద్యాదశ ౨ గుంగు గాల్ । గురోశ్శూన్యశరై ౫౦ మందస్యైక రామై ౩౧ కృ భాజితా కలాదికఫలం శోధ్యగురశనైశ్చ పాతయోః॥" (bhulōcanāgni candrōna 1321 śākādyādaśa 12 guṅgu gāl | gurōśśūnyaśarai 50 mandasyaika rāmai 31 kṛ bhājitā kalādikaphalaṃ śōdhyaguraśanaiśca pātayōḥ ||)</note> **Subtract 1321 <note>represented by 'Bhu' (1), 'Locana' (2), 'Agni' (3), and 'Candra' (1)</note> from the elapsed Shaka year. Divide the result by 50 for Jupiter and by 31 for Saturn. The resulting minutes of arc should be subtracted from the positions of the nodes of Jupiter and Saturn.**

### Procedure for Determining the Manda-Hypotenuse.
#### The Cosine Multipliers for this calculation.

> <note>original Sanskrit: "ఖఖబ్ధిబాణచంద్రాఖ్య మంశాధ్యంగుణకంతతః ౧౮౦౫౪౦ చంద్ర బాణేషుబాణేందు చాంశాధ్యంగుణకం గురోః ౧౫౫౫౧॥ ౧౫ బాహుబాణైక శూన్యాభ్య మంశాధ్యం గుణకం శనేః ౧౦౧౫౦ ౧౧౦ ఏతానికోటిగుణకాన్యంంశాదిషుయథాక్రమం॥" (khakhabdhibāṇacandrākhya maṃśādhyanguṇakantataḥ 180540 candra bāṇēṣubāṇēndu cāṃśādhyanguṇakaṃ gurōḥ 15551 || 15 bāhubāṇaikasūnyābhya maṃśādhyaṃ guṇakaṃ śanēḥ 10150 110 ētānikōṭiguṇakānyaṃśādiṣuyathākramaṃ ||)</note> **The multipliers for the cosine factors, in order, are: 180,540; then for Jupiter, 15,551; and for Saturn, 10,150. Use these cosine multipliers for the degrees accordingly.**

### Calculating the True Hypotenuse.

> <note>original Sanskrit: "కోటిజ్యాంగుణకై హన్యాత్ । నఖైరాప్తంతుకోటిషు । ఫలార్ధం త్రిజ్యయా శోధ్యం కర్క్యాదా కేంద్ర గేసతి । మకరా దౌతుసంయోజ్యము । మాందకర్ణ ఇతీరతః ॥" (kōṭijyāṅguṇakai hanyāt | nakhairāptaṃtukōṭiṣu | phalārdhaṃ trijyayā śōdhyaṃ karkyādā kēndragēsati | makarā dautusaṃyōjyamu | māndakarṇa itīrataḥ ||)</note> **Multiply the cosine by the respective multipliers. Divide the result by twenty <note>represented by 'Nakha' (fingernails/toes)</note>. If the planet's anomaly is in the half-circle beginning with Cancer, subtract half the result from the radius. If it is in the half-circle beginning with Capricorn, add it. This is called the <term>Manda-karṇa<gloss>the hypotenuse of the equation of the center</gloss></term>.**

**Meaning:** Having calculated the <term>Bhujajyā<gloss>sine</gloss></term> and <term>Koṭijyā<gloss>cosine</gloss></term>, multiply the cosine by the specific multipliers mentioned above. Divide by 20. If the <term>Kendra<gloss>anomaly/center</gloss></term> begins from the sign of Cancer <note>the 2nd and 3rd quadrants</note>, subtract the result from the <term>Trijyā<gloss>radius of the sine-table, usually 3438</gloss></term>. If the anomaly begins from the sign of Capricorn <note>the 4th and 1st quadrants</note>, add it. The result is the Manda-hypotenuse.

### Calculating the Tangent Sine.

> <note>original Sanskrit: "భుజజ్య త్రిజ్యయాభ్యస్తా కోటిజ్యాంచ విభజితా స్స్పర్శ రేఖాఫలంసాధ్యమిత్యుక్తం పూర్వ సూరిభిః॥" (bhujajya trijyayābhyastā kōṭijyāñca vibhajitā ssparśa rēkhāphalaṃsādhyamityuktaṃ pūrva sūribhiḥ ||)</note> **Multiply the sine by the radius and divide by the cosine. The resulting value is the <term>Sparśa-rekhā-jyā<gloss>the tangent sine</gloss></term>, as stated by the ancient sages.**

**Meaning:** Multiply the sine (Bhujajyā) by the radius (Trijyā) and divide that product by the cosine (Koṭijyā). The quotient obtained is the Tangent Sine.

#### Practical Example.

The sine of 15 degrees is 1516. Multiplying this by the radius, which is 3438, gives 38...

### Chapter on the True Positions of Jupiter and Saturn
#### First, the Equation of the Center

> <note>original Sanskrit: "శ్లో॥ రవిభాదిగ్రహాంతం | సబీజంతు తతఃస్ఫుటం తన్మందోచ్చ విహీనంతు కేంద్ర దేవ చతుష్టయం | భాగాస్తుషడ్వి తాలబ్ద జ్యాపిండామాందకర్మణి | శేషభాగంతరగుణవద్ధ్యాత్వా: పిండసంయుతాః॥" (ślō|| ravibhādigrahāntaṃ | sabījaṃtu tataḥsphuṭaṃ tanmandōcca vihīnaṃtu kēndra dēva catuṣṭayaṃ | bhāgāstuṣaḍvi tālabda jyāpiṇḍāmāndakarmaṇi | śēṣabhāgaṃtaraguṇavaddhyātvā: piṇḍasaṃyutāḥ ||)</note> **From the Sun to the other planets, apply the secular correction to find the true position. Subtract the apogee from that position to find the anomaly, which consists of four quadrants. Divide the degrees by six to find the sine-index for the calculation of the Equation of the Center. Multiply the remaining degrees by the difference and add it to the base sine value.**

**Explanation:** For all planets starting from the Sun, one must apply the <term>secular correction</term><note>original: "bīja-saṃskāraṃ"</note> to determine the true position. When the <term>apogee</term><note>original: "mandōcca"</note> is subtracted from the planet's position, the result is the <term>anomaly</term><note>original: "kēndramu"</note>, which spans four quadrants. If the anomaly is within 6 <term>signs</term><note>A "sign" or rāśi is 30 degrees of the zodiac</note>, use it as is; if it exceeds 6 signs, subtract it from 12 signs to get the anomaly. Convert this anomaly into degrees and divide by 6 to find the <term>sine-index</term><note>original: "jyākhaṃ"</note>...

It includes a table of numerical values for trigonometric corrections and a Sanskrit verse explaining how to derive a planet's anomaly from its apogee.</summary>

...take that <term>segment</term><note>original: "khaṇḍamu"; a specific interval used in interpolation</note>, multiply the degrees and other units by the difference between segments, convert the value and add it to the degree position. ### Correction for Finding the Result Generated by the Node-less Center

> <note>original Sanskrit verse: "శ్లో॥ పాతోనకేంద్రజనితఫలభాగాదశో ౧౦ ధృతాః | శేషంఖండాంతరహతం భాగదశహతాఫలం | సంయోజ్యఖండే పాతోనకేంద్రోయుగ్మ పదేధూనం ఓజపాదేగుణం కార్యంసంస్కారేమందకర్మణి॥" (ślō|| pātōnakēndrajanitaphalabhāgādaśō 10 dhṛtāḥ | śēṣaṃkhaṇḍāntarahataṃ bhāgadaśahatāphalaṃ | saṃyōjyakhaṇḍē pātōnakēndrōyugma padēdhūnaṃ ōjapādēguṇaṃ kāryaṃsaṃskārēmanda karmaṇi||)</note> **Divide the degrees of the result produced by the node-less center by ten. Multiply the remainder by the segment difference and divide the degrees by ten. Add this result to the segment. In the process of the slow correction, if the node-less center is in an even quadrant, subtract it; if in an odd quadrant, add it.**

Meaning: Having established the slow-corrected planet, calculate the <term>base</term><note>original: "bhujā"; the sine of the angular distance from the nearest node</note> of the <term>node-less center</term><note>original: "pātōna-kēndramu"; the position of the planet minus the position of its node</note>. Divide that base by 10. Take the corresponding table entry for that result. Multiply the remainder by the difference, convert the value, divide the degree position by 10, and add the result to the segment. While the node-less center is in an <term>odd quadrant</term><note>original: "ōja-padamu"; the 1st and 3rd quarters of the circle</note>, subtract this result from the slow-corrected planet; while in an <term>even quadrant</term><note>original: "yugma-padamu"; the 2nd and 4th quarters of the circle</note>, add it. The odd quadrant lasts up to 3 signs, the even up to 6 signs, the odd up to 9 signs, and the even up to 12 signs. Below is the table showing the node-less center results for Jupiter and Saturn.

# The Correction Table for the Earth-Planet and Others
->The Procedure for Correction<-
### Determining the Earth-Planet

> <note>original Sanskrit verse: శ్లో॥ నేత్రాష్టాభి శశి ౧౮౨౨ హ్యూనశా కేద్విగుణీతాహృతాశి ౮ం గాగ్నిధృతి ౧౮౩౬ హృల్లబ్ధం భూగ్రహగోళగణాదికం॥ (ślō|| nētrāṣṭābhi śaśi 1822 hyūnaśākēdviguṇītāhṛtāśi 80 gāgnidhṛti 1836 hṛllabdhaṃ bhūgrahagōḷagaṇādikaṃ||)</note> **Subtract 1822 from the Shaka year. Double the remainder and divide by 80. Then, using 1836 as a divisor, the result obtained provides the Earth-planet's position in the celestial sphere, including signs and other units.**

Meaning: Subtract 1822 from the <term>Shalivahana Shaka</term><note>A historical Indian era beginning in 78 CE; the year 1822 Shaka corresponds to 1900 CE</note> year. Double the remaining value and divide it by 80. The resulting value is the <term>Earth-planet</term><note>original: "bhūgraha"; a specific mathematical constant or correction factor used in this system of astronomy</note>. Take the remainder, multiply it by 125, and divide by 1836 to find the <term>signs</term><note>original: "rāśulu"; 30-degree segments of the zodiac</note>. Multiply the next remainder by 30 and divide by 1836 to get the degrees. Multiply that remainder by 60 and divide by 1836 to get the <term>minutes</term><note>original: "liptalu"</note> and <term>seconds</term><note>original: "viliptalu"</note>. In this manner, the Earth-planet is calculated only once for the entire duration of a year; this Earth-planet value does not change throughout that year. By adding 23 minutes and 32 seconds to it, you will arrive at the Earth-planet value for the following year.

### Determining the Sine-Result Generated by the Earth-Planet

> <note>original Sanskrit verse: శ్లో॥ భూగ్రహాఖ్యాదిలిప్తికాళూన్యాష్టవేద ౪౮౦ హృత్తల భ్యాంఖండాంతరగుణ్యం ఖాష్ట వేద ౪౮౦ హృతాః ఫలంగ తఖండజయుతం కార్యం కేంద్రేమేషతులారిగే ధనర్ణాంతును రాజార్యో ! మందే వ్యత్యయమేవచ॥ (ślō|| bhūgrahākhyādiliptikāśūnyāṣṭavēda 480 hṛttala bhyāṃkhaṇḍāntaraguṇyaṃ khāṣṭa vēda 480 hṛtāḥ phalaṅga takhaṇḍajayutaṃ kāryaṃ kēndrēmēṣatulārigē dhanarṇāṃtunu rājāryō ! mandē vyatyayamēvaca||)</note> **Convert the Earth-planet position into minutes and divide by 480. Multiply the remainder by the difference between segments and divide by 480. Add this result to the value of the past segment. When the center is in the signs starting from Aries or Libra, apply the addition or subtraction respectively for Jupiter; for Saturn, O noble teacher, perform the inverse.**

Meaning: First, calculate the position of the Earth-planet in signs and degrees, then convert the whole value into minutes. Divide these minutes by 480. Take the <term>past segment</term><note>original: "gata-khaṇḍamu"; the previous entry in a sine table used for interpolation</note> corresponding to the quotient. Multiply the remainder by the <term>segment difference</term><note>original: "khaṇḍāntaramu"</note> and divide by 480. Add the resulting value to the segment value. For <term>Jupiter</term><note>original: "Guruvu"</note>, if the Earth-planet position is in the six signs starting from <term>Aries</term><note>original: "Meṣādi"; Aries through Virgo</note>, the result is additive (positive). If it is in the six signs starting from <term>Libra</term><note>original: "Tulāli"; Libra through Pisces</note>, it is subtractive (negative). For <term>Saturn</term><note>original: "Śani"</note>, the rule is reversed: if it is in the Aries group, it is subtractive, and if in the Libra group, it is additive. This is how the Earth-planet correction must be applied to Jupiter and Saturn.

<summary>This page explains how to calculate the "Earth-planet" (bhūgraha) correction factor, which is determined annually and used to refine the calculated positions of Jupiter and Saturn through addition or subtraction based on their zodiacal signs.</summary>
<keywords>Earth-planet, bhūgraha, Shalivahana Shaka, Jupiter, Saturn, astronomical correction, zodiac signs, sine-result, interpolation, Indian astronomy</keywords>

->Jupiter has four auxiliaries.<-

> <note>original Sanskrit verse: శ్లో॥ నేత్రాంకా ౯౨ మార్క్వనా ౨౧ దింద్రా ౧౪ బాణాగ్నయ ౩౫ ధృవాణ్యేపై | గురూపకరణాన్యంత సంస్కారప్రథ మాన్యెవై ॥ (ślō|| nētrāṃkā 92 mārkvaṇā 21 dindrā 14 bāṇāgnaya 35 dhṛvāṇyēpai | gurūpakaraṇānyanta saṃskārapratha mānyevai ||)</note> **The values 92, 21, 14, and 35 are the fixed constants. These are the primary auxiliaries for Jupiter, used in the final correction process.**

Meaning: Jupiter has four auxiliaries <note>original: "upakaranas," tools or components for a calculation</note>. The first is 92, the second is 21, the third is 14, and the fourth is 35. These are the <term>fixed constants</term><note>original: "dhruva-sankhyalu"; numbers that remain stable in a mathematical formula</note>.

> <note>original Sanskrit verse: శ్లో॥ ఖాత్ర్య్యష్ట వేదశూన్యష్ట చంద్రోన గూంశకాన్వితం ౪౮౧౦౮౩౦ రాన్ మునిభిర్విభాజ్యేవం గురువారాదికంభవేత్ ॥ (ślō|| khātryyaṣṭa vēdaśūnyaṣṭa candrōna gūṃśakānvitaṃ 4810830 rān munibhirvibhājyēvaṃ guruvārādikaṃbhavēt ||)</note> **Using the number 4,810,830 and dividing it by seven, the day of the week for Jupiter and the other factors are determined.**

Multiply the remainder by 100; then 435 is processed... from the calculation of the twelve... 21288 and 4470 designated as such. 4390 is the quotient obtained. According to the prescribed rule... <note>This section describes a complex long-division or iterative process common in Indian astronomical treatises (Siddhantas) to refine the position of a planet for a specific weekday.</note>

# ...in sequence | Multiplied by a hundred, the remainder is increased by the previous half...
<warning>The following line is heavily faded and partially illegible.</warning>
<unclear>sampradyut bhaḥ . . . . . . . dha . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .</unclear>

After subtracting 1,408,830 from the <note>original: Kalpadyuhagana</note> total day-count of the era, if the remaining balance is divided by 7, the weekday can be determined, starting with Thursday. Multiply that remaining day-count by 100 and set it down in four separate copies. Divide these four copies respectively by 37,583, then 22,244, then 4,570, and finally 4,300. Take the quotients obtained—specifically the remainders left after dividing by 100—and add the previously mentioned "positive" constants: 8 to the first copy, 11 to the second, 14 to the third, and 35 to the fourth. This procedure is called <term>Correction</term> <note>original: Samskara; a mathematical adjustment to align theoretical models with observed planetary positions</note>. Based on the results obtained from this, one must observe the four repetitions of the calculation to determine the <note>original: liptali</note> minutes of arc. By identifying whether these are <note>original: dhana-runa</note> positive or negative values, apply them to the planet's position. If the result is positive, it must be added; if it is negative, it must be subtracted.

---

->There are also auxiliary corrections for Saturn.<-
->I shall now describe the sequence.<-

> Verse: **The values 17** <note>original: "Munichandra"; Muni=7, Chandra=1</note>, **43** <note>original: "Vahniyuga"; Vahni=3, Yuga=4</note>, **and 90** <note>original: "Mudha"</note> **occur in order. These are the primary auxiliaries for the "Son of the Shadow"** <note>original: "Chayaputra"; an epithet for the planet Saturn</note>. **The remainder of the previously mentioned division should be multiplied by 100 in sequence. Divide this by 22,244, then by 11,119, and by 7,544. The resulting quotients, when divided by 100, provide the <term>Correction</term> values. In this way, one should calculate the slow and fast anomalous positions of the planet.**

In this verse, the auxiliary constants are provided: first 17, then 43, and so on, as the appropriate positive values.
