<meta>This page continues the instructions for mathematical astronomy, moving from specific planetary constants to the general methods for the two primary corrections: the "Slow Correction" (relating to elliptical orbit) and the "Quick Correction" (relating to the planet's movement relative to the Sun).</meta>

<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Calculation</header>

The divisors for planetary movement <note>original: bhoga-haratulu</note> are: first, 22,444; second, 11,144; and third, 7,514 <note>the transcription suggests "7514 1/4", as 'nna-kalalu' usually refers to fractions or parts</note>. Having calculated the tabular results according to the method established for Jupiter, and having identified the resulting positive and negative values <note>original: dhana-runamulu</note>, one should adjust the position accordingly. Following this, the Quick Correction <term>Śīghra-saṃskāra; the adjustment for the planet's synodic period or parallax</term> must be performed.

### A Simple Method for the Quick Correction

> Verse: **Subtracting the planet from the Slow Apogee** <note>original: mṛdūccā</note> **and the Quick Apogee** <note>original: caloccā</note> **yields what is called the Anomaly** <note>original: Kendra</note>. **By determining the base of the arc** <note>original: bhuja</note> **and dividing the degrees by six, the sine table position is found. Using the difference between the sine values, multiplied by the remaining time and divided... the result is the positive or negative adjustment for that quadrant.**

Meaning: When the planet is subtracted from its apogees, the result is the Anomaly <term>Kendra; the angular distance of a planet from its apsis</term>. Determine the base sine <note>original: bhuja</note> of that anomaly. Divide those degrees of the base by 6; the quotient identifies whether the sine table entry <note>original: jya-patakamu</note> belongs to the signs starting with Cancer or Capricorn. Multiply the remaining degrees by the "sine difference" <note>original: jyāntara</note>. Add the result to the accumulated sine total <note>original: jyā-piṇḍamu</note>. By identifying whether the anomaly falls in the signs starting with Aries or Libra, apply the correction to make the planet "true" or "clear" <note>original: sphuṭa; the corrected position</term>.

### For Verifying the Calculation of the Slow Correction

> Verse: **The divisors for the Slow Correction** <note>original: Manda-cheda</note> **are: 263, 118, 51, 9,818, 113, 263, and 75. Multiply the sine of the base of the Slow Anomaly by ten and divide by these respective divisors. The resulting minutes of arc are added or subtracted based on whether the Anomaly starts from Libra or Aries.**

Meaning: When the "Slow Apogee" is subtracted from the planet, the Anomaly is found. From that Anomaly, calculate the sine of the base <note>original: bhuja-jya</note>. Multiply that sine by 10. Divide the result by the following constants for each planet:
*   For the Sun: 263
*   For the Moon: 118
*   For Mars: 51
*   For Mercury: 9,818
*   For Jupiter: 113
*   For Venus: 263
*   For Saturn: 75

The resulting value in minutes <note>original: kalā</note> is "positive" <note>added</note> if the Anomaly is in the half-circle starting with Libra, and "negative" <note>subtracted</note> if it is in the half-circle starting with Aries. This yields the "Slow-corrected" <note>original: mānda-sphuṭa</note> position.

### Calculation of the Slow and Quick Corrected Velocities

> Verse: **Multiply the difference between the sines of the Slow Correction by the degrees of the circle, 360, and divide...**

<summary>This page details the mathematical constants and procedures for calculating the "Slow" and "Quick" corrections (equation of center and parallax) for the planets, providing specific divisors for each celestial body.</summary>
<keywords>Kendra (Anomaly), Manda-phala (Slow Correction), Śīghra-saṃskāra (Quick Correction), Bhuja-jya (Sine of the base), Planetary Divisors, Astronomical Calculation</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for astronomical corrections, specifically detailing how to calculate the daily variation in planetary speed based on the "Slow" (Manda) and "Quick" (Shighra) cycles, focusing on the planet Jupiter.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Calculation</header>

> Verse: **Multiply the cosine of the anomaly by the daily motion. If starting from Capricorn, subtract; if starting from Cancer, add to the motion. Then, find the difference between that motion and the daily motion of the "Quick" cycle. Divide the result by 360. The result is the positive or negative correction for the observed speed. If the correction exceeds the speed itself, the motion becomes retrograde.**

Meaning: Take the difference in the "Slow Correction" <term>Manda-antyāntaram; the variation in the eccentricity of the orbit</term> of the second cycle and multiply it by the planet's daily motion <note>original: bhuktya-gati</note>. Divide this product by 360. If the anomaly of this "Slow" cycle begins with the sign of Cancer <note>original: Karkyādi; the signs Cancer, Leo, Virgo, Libra, Scorpio, Sagittarius</note>, the result should be added <term>dhana; positive adjustment</term>. If it begins with the sign of Capricorn <note>original: Makarādi; the signs Capricorn, Aquarius, Pisces, Aries, Taurus, Gemini</note>, it should be subtracted <term>ṛṇa; negative adjustment</term>. 

Subtract this unrefined "Slow" motion from the true motion of the Sun, which represents the "True Quick" motion <note>original: sphuṭa-śīghramu</note>. The resulting daily motion of the moon-like anomaly should then be refined four times. Multiply it by the difference in the "Quick Sine" <note>original: śīghra-jyā</note> and divide by 360. The resulting value is to be applied as a positive or negative correction to the "Slow-refined" motion. Specifically, if the interval ends in the Capricorn signs, it is negative; if it ends in the Cancer signs, it is positive. This should be applied to the "Slow" true motion. If the negative correction is larger than the motion itself, the resulting true motion <note>original: sphuṭa-gati</note> becomes retrograde <term>vakra-gati; appearing to move backward in the sky</term>.

## The Correction of Motion
<note>original: Cālana saṃskāramu. This refers to the mathematical adjustment made to planetary or lunar positions to account for their varying speeds over time.</note>

When a lunar month occurs without a solar transit <note>original: asaṅkrānti; the Sun does not move from one zodiac sign to the next within that lunar month</note> or when a month has two solar transits <note>original: dvi-saṅkrānti</note>, it is time to calculate the intercalary month <note>original: adhimāsa; an "extra" month added to the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar year</note>. According to the previously stated method, when two intercalary months are possible, the month lacking a solar transit is declared the "additional" month.

## Regarding Fast and Retrograde Motions
<note>original: Śīghra-vakra-pracāraiḥ. In ancient astronomy, "fast" (śīghra) and "retrograde" (vakra) refer to the apparent changes in a planet's speed as viewed from Earth.</note>

> **Verse:** When the lunar month is calculated through the cycles of motion, the difference between the equal and unequal speeds must be considered. When the Sun and Moon lack a transit, the correction of that motion determines the month. One must contemplate these two months through the daily variations of the Sun's motion. <note>original: Śaṃsapādaṃ paspatisyāt... māsayadvaṃ viciṃtyaṃ</note>

**Commentary:** A month that does not contain a solar transit is an intercalary month <note>original: adhimāsa</note>. By calculating the fast-cycle motions <note>original: śīghra-cakra-pracāra</note>, we determine if the lunar month is moving quickly or slowly. If the lunar month moves faster than the solar month, it creates an intercalary month. If the lunar month moves slowly while the solar month moves quickly, a "void" or "null" result occurs. The heart of the author’s meaning is this: when the true motion <note>original: sphuṭa-gati</note> is less than the mean motion, it is called "retrograde" <note>original: vakra</note>. When the true motion is greater than the mean motion, he designates it as "fast" <note>original: śīghra</note>.

<summary>This page contains a table of observation-verified lunar position values and explains the methodology for identifying intercalary months (Adhimasa) based on solar transits and the relative "fast" or "retrograde" speeds of the Sun and Moon.</summary>
<keywords>observation-verified values, lunar tables, intercalary month, solar transit, true motion, mean motion, retrograde motion, fast motion, Hindu astronomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 115 regarding the mathematical corrections required for the lunar calendar. It transitions from the basic "Correction of Motion" (Cālana saṃskāra) to the specific calculations used to identify "Aṃhaspati" (expunged) and "Saṃsarpa" (concurrent) months, which occur when the relationship between solar transits and lunar months becomes irregular.</meta>

<page-num>116</page-num>

<header>* The Delight of Calculation *</header>

When the true speed of the Sun is greater than the true speed of the Moon at the end of a period, the <term>Aṃhaspati</term><note>A rare "expunged" or "lost" month (Kṣaya māsa) in the Hindu calendar where two solar transits occur within one lunar month. It is traditionally considered inauspicious for rituals.</note> occurs. When the true speed of the Moon is greater, the <term>Saṃsarpa</term><note>A "concurrent" month that acts as an intercalary month when a Kṣaya māsa occurs in the same year. Unlike a standard extra month, religious festivals can be performed in a Saṃsarpa month.</note> is born. In both even and odd cycles, when the motions are equal to the mean motion, that is the attained motion. Whether the speed of the <term>Apogee</term><note>original: uccagati; the point in an orbit where the celestial body is farthest from Earth and moves at its slowest.</note> is greater or lesser, that motion is the true motion. This is clear.

### Verse
> Multiply the difference between the mean and true positions by two hundred and thirty-nine <note>original: nandvagnayanāni; using the Katapayadi system where Nanda=9, Agni=3, and Nayana=2, read right-to-left as 239.</note> and divide by one hundred. From the maximum correction of the true lunar day, that which is the beginning of the journey should be applied to the mean <term>Parva</term><note>The time of the new moon or full moon conjunction.</note>. If the value for the true Parva is greater, it should be subtracted. In this way, a wise person should first apply the <term>Cycle-shifting correction</term><note>original: cakra-cālana-saṃskāra; a correction factor used to align theoretical calculations with observed reality over long periods.</note> to the mean Parva, and then investigate the Saṃsarpa and Aṃhaspati intercalary months.

**Commentary:** After performing the shifting correction for the expunged month, one should calculate the Parva for the time of the New Moon at the end of that expunged month using the mean measure. The <term>Ghaṭikās</term><note>A traditional unit of time; 1 ghaṭikā equals 24 minutes.</note> of the difference between the mean and true New Moons are multiplied by the <term>Ayanāṃśa</term><note>The angle of the precession of the equinoxes.</note> and divided by half the maximum correction of the mean-true lunar day. Establish the resulting number of Ghaṭikās. If the Ghaṭikās of the true New Moon are more than the mean, add them; if the true New Moon Ghaṭikās are greater, subtract them from the mean New Moon Ghaṭikās. 

In this way, when a lunar month—purified by the cycle-shifting correction—reaches the state of having two solar transits, then there is no Aṃhaspati month, and it does not shift. If there is a "lost" month called Aṃhaspati, then two intercalary months occur within that year. Regarding the phrase "half the maximum correction of the true lunar day," the time for the twelve-degree separation between the mean motions of the Sun and Moon must be obtained. One should calculate the <term>Tithi</term><note>A lunar day, defined as the time it takes for the longitudinal angle between the Moon and the Sun to increase by 12 degrees.</note> in this manner. At that time, using the true speeds of the Sun and Moon, the result of the twelve-degree separation is obtained, and the true Tithi is calculated. By unifying the ten parts of the true Tithi, the half-maximum correction of the mean and true motion is derived. Regarding the maximum values of the mean and true Ghaṭikās, others describe the method as follows:

| Description | Number |
| :--- | :--- |
| The Sun's maximum correction including the northern adjustment | 131 |
| Thus, the circle of the Moon’s slow-correction | 303 |
| The sum of both | 434 |
| Half of that | 217 |
| The difference of the mean motions of the Moon and Sun, multiplied | 530 33 |

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion from page 116 regarding the calculation of lunar months. It focuses on the specific conditions that define intercalary (extra) and expunged (lost) months based on the "Correction of Motion" (Cālana saṃskāra) and the timing of solar transits relative to the New Moon.</meta>

<page-num>117</page-num>

<header>* The Delight of Calculation *</header>

The result obtained from the zodiacal division in <term>Ghatis</term><note>A traditional unit of time; 1 Ghati equals 24 minutes.</note> and <term>Vighatis</term><note>A smaller unit of time; 1 Vighati equals 24 seconds.</note> <margin>17.48</margin> is known as the maximum correction for the time interval between the mean and true positions.

### Verse
When a month is without a solar transit, or when two transits occur within a single month, it is considered "transit-less." In such a case, the month is determined by the appearance of the day of the previous <term>Darsha</term><note>The day of the New Moon, marking the end of the dark fortnight.</note>. In the following month, one should subtract the <term>Nadis</term><note>Another term for Ghatis (24-minute intervals).</note> of the <term>Sankranti</term><note>original: saṃkrānti; the moment the Sun moves from one zodiac sign into another.</note>; the remainder represents the Nadis of the transit after the Correction of Motion has been applied.

-> The appearances of the day of the previous New Moon. <-

**Commentary:** This means the <term>Ghatis</term> of the New Moon occurring after sunrise.

### Verse
Whatever the duration of the <term>Tithi</term><note>A lunar day, or the time it takes for the longitudinal angle between the Moon and the Sun to increase by 12 degrees.</note> may be, whether it decreases or increases on the following day, that same duration should be accepted for the previous day for the purpose of performing one's religious duties.

**Explanation:** The determination of an <term>Adhika Masa</term><note>An "extra" or intercalary month added to the lunar calendar to keep it synchronized with the solar seasons.</note> is made according to the sequence already described for all transit months. When, through the process of the Correction of Motion, a <term>Kshaya Masa</term><note>A "lost" or expunged month that occurs when the Sun transits two zodiac signs within a single lunar month.</note> does not occur, the rule is that two intercalary months should be regulated and utilized instead.

### Verse
One should calculate the <term>Kshaya Masa</term> using the method for releasing the motion. If the motion results in two extra months, then they exist; otherwise, they do not. The <term>Nadis</term> representing the interval between the mean and true positions are multiplied by the <term>Amsas</term><note>Degrees or parts of a circle.</note> and divided accordingly. By finding the difference between the end of the mean lunar day and the true lunar day, one should add the result to the mean <term>Parva</term><note>The time of the New Moon or Full Moon conjunction.</note> or subtract it from the true Parva as required. Thus, a wise person should first perform the Correction of Motion on the mean Parva and then consider the calculation for the intercalary month.

**Syntactic Connection:** Having understood the difference between the mean motions of the Sun and the Moon, and having separately understood the result in <term>Nadis</term> from the total minutes of arc in space, the divisor is found by the combination of those two values.

**The Intent of this Commentary:** These points regarding the loss of the lunar month are clarified here by the scholar **Venkatayyajva**.

<page-num>118</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Calculation</header>

**The Daily Motion of the Sun and Moon** || The Sun’s maximum true daily motion: 61 <term>minutes</term><note>original: liptikā; a unit of arc equal to 1/60th of a degree.</note> and 15 <term>seconds</term><note>original: viliptikā; 1/60th of a minute.</note> (61' 15"). Its minimum true daily motion: 56 minutes and 49 seconds (56' 49").

The Moon’s maximum true daily motion: 856 minutes and 35 seconds (856' 35"). Its minimum true daily motion: 719 minutes and 24 seconds (719' 24"). Thus it is stated in the <term>Siddhanta</term><note>A formal Indian astronomical treatise.</note>:

### Verse
For the Sun, the maximum motion in minutes is described by the numbers "kings, directions, and moon" <note>This uses the *Bhūta-saṅkhyā* system where words represent numbers: Kings (16), Directions (10), Moon (1). The text correlates these to the 61' 15" figure.</note>. The minimum is "earth, minutes, and seconds." For the Moon, the maximum is 856 minutes, and the minimum motion is 719 minutes and 24 seconds.

### Verse
> Having calculated the daily motion of the Sun and Moon in minutes and seconds: the motion of the Sun is 934 <unclear>gajārī</unclear>; for the Moon, it is expressed as "zero, delights, horses, senses, and fires." <note>These are further mnemonic codes for specific astronomical constants.</note>

**Explanation:** Adding the Sun's maximum true daily motion to its mean motion results in 120 minutes and 13 seconds (120' 13"). Adding the Sun’s minimum true daily motion to its mean motion results in 115 minutes and 57 seconds (115' 57"). The sum of both these values is 236 minutes and 20 seconds (236' 20"). When this sum is divided into four parts, the result is 59 minutes. 

<note>The following describes a complex correction process using multipliers.</note>
Multiplying the result by 838 and dividing by 77, the quotient obtained is 4 minutes and 26 seconds. When this is halved, it becomes 2 minutes and 13 seconds (2' 13"). This value is called the "Supreme Half-Motion" <note>original: paramārdhabhukti.</note>.

In the same manner, by adding the Moon’s maximum and minimum true motions to its mean motion, then combining them and dividing by four, and further multiplying by 60 and dividing by 800, the result when halved is 29 minutes and 35 seconds...

<vocab>గణకరంజని (Gaṇakarañjani), రవిచంద్రమఃభుక్తి (Ravicandramaḥbhukti), స్ఫుటగతి (Sphuta-gati), లిప్తికా (Liptikā), విలిప్తికా (Viliptikā), సిద్ధాంతము (Siddhāntamu), మధ్యగతి (Madhyama-gati)</vocab>

<page-num>119</page-num>
<header>->The Delight of Calculation<-</header>

35 <term>ghatikas</term><note>A unit of time equal to 24 minutes.</note> is the maximum lunar correction. The solar maximum correction is 30 ghatikas, 30 <term>vighatikas</term><note>also known as palas; 1/60th of a ghatika.</note>, and 48 <term>viliptikas</term><note>seconds of arc or time.</note> (30; 30, 48). These are known as the maximum corrections for the <term>tithi</term><note>a lunar day, or 1/30th of a lunar month.</note>.

---

## Regarding the Maximum Correction of the Mean and True Time-intervals

The New Moon calculated using the mean positions and mean daily motions of the planets is called the **Mean New Moon**. The New Moon calculated using the true positions and true daily motions of the planets is called the **True New Moon**. By finding the difference between these two, one obtains the desired time in ghatikas. 

Multiply that result by the degrees of the solstice (<term>ayana</term>) and divide by the maximum correction factor for the tithi to obtain the result. If the Mean New Moon is less than the True New Moon, one should add the result to the Mean New Moon. If the True New Moon is less than the Mean New Moon, one should subtract it from the mean conjunction point. 

By following this procedure, one should apply the "shifting correction" (<term>chalana-samskara</term>) to the beginning, middle, and end of the True New Moon. If no solar <term>sankranti</term><note>The moment the Sun enters a new zodiac sign.</note> occurs between two New Moons after this shifting correction is applied, that month is called an **intercalary month** (<term>adhimāsa</term>). If two solar transits occur between two such corrected New Moons, it is called a **lost month** (<term>amhaspati</term> or <term>kṣayamāsa</term>). 

When shifting occurs, because the previous transit was not completed, this lost month is counted as a single unit. When there is no such shifting, then it is considered as two separate months.

## This is observed and stated in the *Vateshwara Siddhanta* <note>original: "Vacheshwara Siddhanta"; likely referring to the 10th-century astronomical treatise by Vateshwara.</note>

> ### Verse
> A lunar month devoid of a solar transit is called "additional" (*adhika*). Due to the irregular and varied speeds of planetary motion, a "lost month" (*amhaspati*) occurs when transits are uneven. This shifting (*chalana*) arises from the difference between the mean and true conjunctions of the Moon and Sun. If the calculation shifts, one must consider the pair of months.

It provides specific correction constants and references the Vateshwara Siddhanta as an authority.</summary>

<page-num>120</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Calculation.</header>

# Main Rapid Method :-

The <term>ghatikas</term> and <term>vighatikas</term> <note>Traditional units of time; a ghatika is 24 minutes, and a vighatika is 24 seconds.</note> are obtained by multiplying the difference in degrees between the mean interval and the true interval by the solstitial degrees (<term>ayanāṃśas</term>) and then dividing by the half-maximum correction. When the mean interval is greater than the true interval, the value should be subtracted from the mean; when it is less, it should be added to the mean. By following this procedure, the correction will be sufficient.

If, even after applying this correction, two solar transits (<term>sankrāntis</term>) do not shift <note>meaning if the expected solar transits do not fall within the lunar month boundaries as calculated</note>, then it is determined to be a **lost month** (<term>kshayamāsa</term>).

By "shifting," it is meant that if a second solar transit does not occur within the duration of the New Moon's <term>ghatikas</term>, there is no lost month.

The half-maximum correction is said to have four variations.

->Method 1 is as follows:<-

Take the half of the mean interval and the half of the true interval; add them together and halve the sum to arrive at the half-maximum correction.

->Method 2 (Alternative):<-

Add the maximum correction of the Sun to the maximum correction of the Moon, halve the result, and then divide the difference between the mean motions of the Sun and the Moon; this result is the maximum correction. This is considered the superior method. The maximum correction represents the refined calculation (<term>kauśala-phalam</term>) <note>literally "the fruit of skill," referring to the precision of the combined solar and lunar positions</note> of the Sun and Moon.

->Nomenclature in this context:<-

1. <term>Sansarpa</term> <note>original: "సంసర్ప"</note> refers to an **intercalary month** (<term>adhikamāsa</term>).
2. <term>Amhaspati</term> <note>original: "అంహస్పతి"</note> refers to a **lost month** (<term>kshayamāsa</term>).

<summary>This page explains the "Rapid Method" for correcting mean planetary intervals into true intervals and provides specific rules for identifying lost months (kshayamāsa) and intercalary months (sansarpa) in the Hindu calendar.</summary>
<keywords>Rapid Method, mean interval, true interval, lost month, intercalary month, sankranti, solar transit, ghatika, vighatika</keywords>

# Chapter on Lunar Eclipses.

> Having bowed to Saraswati <note>the goddess of knowledge</note> and acknowledging Ganesha <note>the remover of obstacles</note>, I salute the Lord of Kamala <note>Vishnu</note> for the successful completion of this work.

> Drawing from the teachings of previous masters, I prepare to speak of these secrets—whatever is subtle, clear, and refined—to provide wisdom and auspiciousness.

> I, Katyantika Kashi Chandra, born of the Bharadwaja lineage <note>original: "Bharadwaja-sagotra-ja"</note>, speak of the procedure for eclipses as taught by Gopalakrishna Siddhanti.

# Chapter on Lunar Eclipses.

> The Moon acts as the coverer of the Sun, appearing like a cloud from below. The Earth’s shadow is directed eastward, and it is this shadow that the Moon enters <note>This explains the physical cause of eclipses: the Moon blocks the Sun during a solar eclipse, and the Moon enters the Earth's shadow during a lunar eclipse</note>.

## Calculation of the Orbs.

<note>This section describes how to calculate the apparent diameter or "orb" (bimba) of the Sun and Moon, which changes based on their distance from Earth.</note>

The orbital motion of the Sun is fixed at 598 minutes <note>original: "shat-vasha-shara" or 598; minutes of arc are called <term>liptas</term></note>. Find the difference between this and the Sun’s true motion, then multiply by 15 to get seconds <note><term>vikalas</term></note>. If the true motion is greater than the orbital motion, add this value to 32 minutes; if it is less, subtract it. The result is known as the Sun’s true orb.

**Explanation:** The Sun's mean orbital motion is 598 minutes. Calculate the difference between this and the Sun's true daily motion and multiply by 15. The resulting seconds should be added to 32 minutes if the true motion is greater than the mean, or subtracted if it is less. This determines the Sun's true orb diameter.

### For the Moon.

> The mean orb of the Moon is said to be 31 minutes and 24 seconds <note>original: "shashi-vinhni" representing 31, 24</note>. The size of the Earth’s shadow <note><term>bhū-chāyā</term></note> is 48 minutes and 35 seconds.

> The Moon’s motion in its orbit is 800 <note>original: "bhuja-naga"</note>. The difference between the Moon’s motion on the ecliptic and its true motion...

The divisor is 40. For the Earth’s shadow, the multiplier is 3. Divide the result by 40.
For the parallax, multiply by 1.5 <note>original: "sārdha-rūpa", meaning "one and a half"</note> and divide by 80.
When the true motion is less than the mean, subtract; if it is more, add.
From the Moon’s orb, the parallax and the shadow diameters are made true <note>This verse provides the "correction" factors to adjust mean diameters to their "true" apparent sizes based on the Moon's speed.</note>.

> **Explanation:** The mean measure of the Moon’s orb is 31' 24" <term>liptas</term> <note>minutes of arc</note>. The mean measure of the Earth’s shadow <note>original: "bhū-chāyā"</note> is 83' 24". The Moon’s mean horizontal parallax <note><term>lambanam</term></note> is 57' 36". The mean orbital motion is 800' minutes. Find the difference between the Moon’s true motion and this mean orbital motion (800). Divide this difference by 40 to find the correction for the Moon’s orb; add or subtract this from the mean 31' 24". To find the correction for the Earth’s shadow, multiply that same difference by 3 and divide by 40; add or subtract from 83' 24". For the lunar parallax, multiply the difference by 1.5 <note>original: 1;30</note> and divide by 40. If the calculated true motion of the Moon is greater than 800, add these corrections; if it is less, subtract them from these three values.

### Another Simple Method.

Add six to the current motion and halve it to find the Sun’s true orb. Subtracting one-sixth of the Sun’s orb gives the true light-rays <note>This refers to a shorthand method for calculating the Sun's apparent diameter.</note>.

**Verse:** Add ten to the Moon’s motion and divide by the relevant factor to find the Moon’s orb. Subtracting a sixteenth part <note>original: "indrāṃśa", referring to the number 14 or 16 depending on the system, but here likely a specific fractional correction</note> from the Moon's orb is said to yield the true orb.

> **Explanation:** To calculate the celestial latitude <note><term>vikṣēpa</term></note>, one requires the Sine <note><term>bhujajyā</term></note> according to the *Surya Siddhanta* <note>a foundational Sanskrit astronomical treatise</note>. Considering that to be difficult, we are setting forth a simpler path for establishing the Sine. For the purpose of eclipses occurring within 15 degrees of the node <note>the point where the Moon's path crosses the ecliptic</note>, I shall indicate three tables for the Sine of the argument <note>original: "pātōna-kēndra-bhujajyā"</note>.

| Verse | Table 1 | 811.37 | Result | 299.55 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| | Table 2 | 596.54 | Result | 297.10 |
| | Table 3 | 889.45 | Result | 297.24 |

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for lunar calculations. Having previously defined the diameters of the celestial bodies, the text now moves to calculating the "arm" (the sine of the argument), the Moon's celestial latitude, and the magnitude of an eclipse.</meta>

<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Calculation</header>

> Divide the degrees of the arm by ten or fifteen to designate the segments. Multiply the remainder by the difference of the desired table entry and divide by five; the result is the sine of the arm <term>bhujajyā</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "tad-bāhu-bhāga..." describing linear interpolation to find sine values from a table</note>.

**Explanation:** For the argument <note>the angular distance from a starting point</note> of the Moon minus its node, identify the table entries: 1 entry for 5 degrees, 2 entries for 10 degrees, and 3 entries for 15 degrees. Based on this number of parts, determine the difference between the first table entry (up to 5°), the second (up to 10°), or the third (up to 15°). Multiply the remaining degrees by that difference and divide by 5. The result is the sine of the arm <term>bhujajyā</term>.

> Multiply the arm of the Moon minus its node by 103 <note>original Sanskrit: "vahni-pūrṇendu," where fire=3, void=0, moon=1</note> and divide by 1146 <note>original Sanskrit: "rasābdhi-rudra," where flavor=6, ocean=4, Rudra=11</note>. The quotient is the Moon's latitude <term>vikṣepa</term> in minutes <term>liptas</term>.

**Explanation:** Subtract the position of the node <note>Rahu</note> from the Moon's position to find the argument. Calculate the "arm" for this argument. Multiply that sine value <term>bhujajyā</term> by 103 and divide by 1146. The resulting quotient is the celestial latitude <note>the distance north or south of the ecliptic</note> in minutes and seconds.

> From the argument of the Moon minus the Sun, subtract the minutes and seconds. If the direction is the same, add them; if the direction is different, find the difference to perform the correction.

**Explanation:** Double the position of the Moon minus the Sun and subtract the argument to find the corrected center. Calculate the "arm" of this center. For every 1 degree of this arm, calculate a result at a rate of 8 seconds <term>vileptas</term>. If this result and the latitude are in the same direction, add them; if they are in different directions, subtract them to find the "true latitude" <term>spaṭa-vikṣepa</term>. **Note:** This is not correct; one should calculate the latitude according to the *Manasa* method <note>referring to the *Karana-manasa*, a concise astronomical treatise by Munjala</note>.

> Subtract the Moon's instantaneous latitude from the sum of the semi-diameters of the two bodies <note>the eclipsed and the eclipser</note>. The remainder is called the obscured portion <term>channa</term>.

**Explanation:** Subtract the calculated instantaneous latitude of the Moon from the combined value of the two radii <note>half the diameter of the Moon plus half the diameter of the Earth's shadow</note>. The result is the obscured portion; these are the minutes of the eclipse <term>grāsa-liptas</term>.

> If this value is greater than the diameter of the body to be eclipsed, it is a total eclipse; otherwise, it is partial. If the latitude is greater than the sum of the semi-diameters, no eclipse occurs...

<meta>catchword: to be eclipsed <note>original: "grāhya"</note></meta>

It also defines how to determine the magnitude of an eclipse by comparing the Moon's latitude to the combined radii of the celestial bodies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical rules for determining eclipse parameters. The previous page established how to calculate the Moon's celestial latitude (its distance from the ecliptic path). This page moves into the specific calculations for the timing and duration of the eclipse.</meta>

<page-num>124</page-num>
<header>The Delight of Calculation</header>

**Explanation:** If the celestial latitude <term>vikṣepa</term> <note>the vertical distance of the Moon from the Sun's path</note> is too great and does not fall within the range required for the bodies to meet, no eclipse will occur. The measurement of how far the Moon enters or misses the shadow is called the latitude. The "obscured minutes" <term>channa-liptas</term> are the same as the "eaten minutes" <term>grāsa-liptas</term> <note>these terms refer to the portion of the Moon's disk covered by the shadow</note>. The measure of the lunar disk that is actually covered is specifically called the obscured minutes.

### Calculating the Half-Duration

> Add the diameters of the eclipsed and eclipsing bodies, and likewise subtract them; halve these values separately. Subtract the square of the latitude from the square of these values. Take the square root of the results, multiply by sixty, and divide by the difference between the daily motions of the Sun and Moon. This yields the half-duration <term>sthityardha</term> and the half-totality <term>vimardha</term> in minutes of time <term>nāḍis</term>.
<note>original Sanskrit: "grāhya grāhaka saṃyoga viyoga dalita pṛthak..." This verse provides the geometric formula to find how long the eclipse lasts from first contact to the middle point.</note>

**Explanation:** Add the diameter of the lunar disk and the diameter of the shadow disk, then square the sum. Subtract the square of the latitude from this, and find the square root of the remainder. Multiply this root by 60 and divide the result by the difference in the speeds <term>gatyantara</term> of the Sun and Moon; the result is the half-duration <term>sthityardha</term> <note>the time from the start of the eclipse to its peak</note>. To find the half-totality <term>vimardha</term> <note>the time from the start of total darkness to the peak</note>, perform the same calculation but use the difference between the diameters of the two disks instead of their sum.

### Refining the Half-Duration and Half-Totality

> Subtract the node <term>Rāhu</term> from the Moon and find the "arm" <note>the sine of the distance from the nearest node</note>. Multiply the degrees by two. In even quadrants, these should be subtracted from the half-duration; in odd quadrants, they should be added. This applies similarly to the immersion and emergence during half-totality as understood by the wise.
<note>original Sanskrit: "dvighna pāto nadho bhāgau..." This verse describes corrections for the eclipse duration based on the Moon's position in its orbit.</note>

**Explanation:** Subtract the position of the node <note>Rahu</note> from the Moon's position and calculate the "arm" <term>bhuja</term>. Multiply the resulting degrees by two. If the Moon is in an even quadrant <note>the 2nd or 4th sections of the orbital circle</note>, this value must be subtracted from the calculated half-duration. If it is in an odd quadrant <note>the 1st or 3rd sections</note>, it must be added.
